# MOSES, THE GOD OF AARON,
<note>The title refers to Exodus 4:16, where God tells Moses he will be as a "God" to his brother Aaron the priest. In 18th-century political theory, this was a common metaphor used to argue that the civil ruler (the "Moses" figure) holds authority over the religious establishment (the "Aaron" figure).</note>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page contains the opening of the preface (Lectori Benevolo Salutem). It establishes the author's intent to provide a simplified guide for students regarding the legal relationship between a ruler and the church, using the biblical metaphor of Moses and Aaron.</meta>

## Greetings to the Benevolent Reader <note>original: "L. B. S.", an abbreviation for *Lectori Benevolo Salutem*, a standard Latin greeting in early modern books.</note>

**I** consider it necessary to advise the Benevolent Reader above all else that I have not written for learned men. For such experts, it is easy to examine and judge the lengthy writings of others in which either the whole of sacred law or some part of it is discussed, and to discern the true from the false among the various opinions of many. Rather, I have written for those who are just beginning to cultivate their minds with the study of the liberal arts, so that they might be freed, in some measure, from the great labor and the significant loss of time otherwise required to understand this branch of <term>Natural and Biblical Jurisprudence</term> <note>original: "Jurisprudentiæ Naturalis ac Biblicæ"; this refers to a legal philosophy derived from both human reason/nature and the Holy Scriptures.</note>.

Therefore, for their benefit, there now appears **MOSES THE GOD OF AARON** <note>original: "MOSES DEUS AARONIS"</note>, or—what is one and the same thing—**THE KING ABOVE THE CHURCH**, **THE PRINCE AS THE GUARDIAN AND DEFENDER OF RELIGION**, **THE PRINCE AS THE RULER OF THE CHURCH**, and **THE SUPREME POWER OF THE PRINCE OVER THE CHURCH**.

This title is taken from the book of Moses, Exodus 4:16 <note>In this verse, God tells Moses that his brother Aaron will be his spokesman, and Moses will be "as God" to him.</note>. From that passage, it is clear that when Moses was commanded by God to go before Pharaoh, he made many excuses, and at the last, simply <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "recu-", likely for the Latin "recusasse," meaning "refused."</note>

<summary>In this preface, the author explains that he has written a simplified legal guide for students regarding the ruler's authority over the church. He introduces the metaphor of "Moses as the God of Aaron" to describe the supreme power of a prince in religious matters.</summary>
<keywords>ecclesiastical law, civil authority, Moses, Aaron, church and state, natural law, biblical jurisprudence</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of Moses's reluctance to lead. The catchword from the previous page was "recu-", which completes here as "recusasse" (refused).</meta>

refused; however, God gave him his brother Aaron as a companion, who would be his Prophet and speak words on his behalf. Therefore, since Moses was to command Pharaoh in the place of God <note>original: "vice Dei"</note>, so that he would permit the Israelites to depart from Egypt, and Aaron was given to him as a Prophet to whom Moses would command what should be said according to the word of the Lord (Exodus 7:1). For this reason, Moses was called the "God of Aaron" by God himself. Furthermore, even after Aaron had been established by God as the <term>High Priest</term> <note>original: "summo Pontifice"</note>, Moses exercised <term>authority over sacred matters</term> <note>original: "imperium sacrorum"</note>, just like other Kings, who are likewise called "gods" <note>A reference to the biblical tradition where rulers are called "gods" (elohim) because they act as God's representatives on earth.</note>.

I have demonstrated, therefore, that the Prince has authority over all aspects of religion, and I have done so from the <term>canonical Scripture</term> alone, setting aside the arguments of <term>Natural Law</term> and the examples of the Roman Emperors <note>original: "Cæsarum Augustorum"</note>, who are recorded as having exercised authority over sacred matters outside of the authority of Scripture. I have hardly mentioned Natural Law for this reason: those who deny the authority over sacred matters owed to the Prince are generally those who have not studied Natural Law and are ignorant of the principles of true Political Science <note>original: "Politicæ"</note>.

As for the examples of Princes that historians have recorded for posterity, I have not included them for this reason: if they are examined closely, many objections can be raised against them. <term>Constantine the Great</term>, to mention him at least—both because of the title of this writing and because it pertains to authority over sacred matters considered generally—was the first

<summary>The author argues that civil rulers possess authority over religious matters, using the biblical relationship between Moses and Aaron as the primary legal model. He justifies his reliance on Scripture over historical examples or natural law, which he claims his opponents do not understand.</summary>
<keywords>Moses, Aaron, Pharaoh, Israelites, Egypt, Exodus 7:1, High Priest, Canonical Scripture, Natural Law, Roman Emperors, Political Science, Constantine the Great, authority over sacred matters</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal and historical basis for a ruler's authority over the church. The previous page concluded with a mention of the Emperor Constantine, and this page expands on his specific self-identification as a "bishop."</meta>

# )( 3

The **Christian Prince**, in order that he might be permitted to organize the affairs of the church, found it necessary even then to assume the title of **bishop** <note>The word "bishop" comes from the Greek *episkopos*, meaning "overseer." The author is arguing that a King is the ultimate overseer of his realm.</note>. And lest the bishops themselves take offense at this—as they, given the great growth of the **Papacy**, already believed that beyond the ministry of sacred rites, the **authority over sacred matters** <note>original: "imperium sacrorum"</note> belonged to them—it had to be added that he was a bishop at least of those things conducted *outside* the church, while they were bishops of those things *inside* the church.

This saying can indeed be explained in a way that is true; for the Prince is a "Bishop" in the grammatical sense of the word, possessing supreme oversight over the entire **commonwealth**, and even over the church, insofar as the church exists within the commonwealth. 

He is a Bishop *outside* the church, for *inside* the church he is a listener <note>The term "listener" (auditor) refers to a member of the congregation or a layman who receives instruction.</note> like other Christians, and the means of grace <note>Refers to the sacraments and the preaching of the Word.</note> are applied to him; however, *outside* the church, he is the Prince and a Bishop with the power of command. Conversely, the bishops are *inside* the church, having oversight over their listeners, so that they may perform their ministry as the need arises among them. 

Because **Constantine** <note>Constantine the Great (r. 306–337 AD), the first Roman Emperor to convert to Christianity, famously called himself "a bishop of those outside the church."</note> called himself a "Bishop"—of the same kind as the ministers of the church regarding sacred rites—and also a "common bishop of all appointed by God," and "one among the bishops," and because the care of sacred things was called "episcopal concern," it clearly appears that he [claimed] the **authority over sacred matters** by a true and due title...

<meta>The page ends here. The catchword for the next page is "non" (not).</meta>

<summary>This page discusses how Christian rulers, specifically Constantine the Great, adopted the title of "bishop" or "overseer" to justify their legal authority over church administration while distinguishing their role from the spiritual ministry of the clergy.</summary>

<keywords>Christian Prince, Constantine the Great, bishop, oversight, church and state, Papacy, authority over sacred matters, commonwealth</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the Prince's authority over the church. The previous page discussed how Constantine the Great styled himself as a "bishop of outside affairs." This page clarifies that while the Prince acts in a supervisory role, his authority is not derived from being a clergyman (bishop), but from his status as God's representative (Vicar) and civil ruler.</meta>

...not have exercised it. Indeed, the **Prince** is not a **bishop** <note>The author is distinguishing between a "bishop" as a church official and the Prince's role as a supreme overseer.</note> in this sense, nor does he possess and exercise **command over sacred matters** <note>original: "imperium in sacra"</note> as a bishop would; rather, he does so because he is the Prince and the **Vicar of God** <note>A title indicating that the ruler acts as God's direct representative on Earth in matters of governance.</note>. This is just as **Moses** exercised the **right of sacred things** <note>original: "jus sacrorum"</note> because he was as a god to **Aaron** <note>A reference to Exodus 4:16, where God tells Moses that Aaron shall be his spokesman, but Moses shall be "as God" to him. The author uses this to show the civil leader (Moses) held authority over the high priest (Aaron).</note> and performed the office of a King. 

Therefore, it is useless to attempt to prove this right by citing the practices of **secular authors** <note>original: "profanis Auctoribus"; literally "profane" or non-religious writers of history.</note> for the sake of validating the law itself; for not everything that *is* done *ought* to be done. Instead, we must learn what the law actually is from **canonical Scripture**, so that we may use it as a standard to examine examples from secular history. 

Furthermore, if the law of sacred matters is illustrated by examples from every era of history, the minds of those first approaching the subject become so distracted that they can find no certain foundation. This is especially true because the opinions of scholars vary so greatly on this point. Some fail to understand because of the subject's difficulty, confusing things that should be kept separate. Others, however, show favor to the **Doctors** <note>In this context, the "Doctors" refers to the clergy, theologians, or church teachers.</note> or even to other subjects against both reason and Holy Scripture, attempting to limit the Prince and even daring to strip him of his **sacred command** <note>original: "imperium sacrorum"</note> in their writings.

For these reasons, I have finally taken up this work—whatever its merit—to demonstrate the power and duty of Princes in sacred matters from the **only** <note>The Latin text ends mid-sentence with "ex sola" (from the only...). Given the context of the Reformation-era argument, the author is likely about to say "from Scripture alone."</note>

<summary>The author argues that a ruler’s authority over the church is derived from their status as God’s representative (like Moses), rather than from clerical titles. He asserts that this authority must be defined by Scripture rather than by historical precedent or the conflicting opinions of scholars who seek to limit royal power.</summary>
<keywords>Vicar of God, Moses and Aaron, jus sacrorum, authority over sacred matters, canonical Scripture, royal supremacy, ecclesiastical law</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface, wrapping up the argument that a clear understanding of the "law of sacred matters" must be derived from Scripture rather than historical precedent alone. It transitions from the theoretical discussion of Moses and the Prince to the practical goal of the book: defining the distinct rights of the ruler, the clergy, and the laity.</meta>

...from **canonical Scripture** alone, which I might demonstrate briefly, yet at the same time distinctly and clearly. In this way, once its true principles and foundations have been explained, nothing is taken away from the rights of the **Prince**, nor of the **Ministry**, nor of any **Christians** whatsoever; nor is too much attributed to any one of them. Instead, each person’s own right is upheld: to the **Prince**, indeed, the **command of sacred matters** <note>original: "imperium sacrorum"; the legal right to govern and oversee the church's external affairs.</note>; to the **Ministers** of the church, the **ministry of sacred matters** <note>original: "ministerium sacrorum"; the spiritual duties of teaching, preaching, and administering sacraments.</note>; and finally, to individual **Christians**, the **private care of their own salvation**. 

For the rest, <term>Kind Reader</term> <note>original: "L. B.", abbreviation for "Lectori Benevolo," a common polite greeting used by authors in the 17th and 18th centuries.</note>, I would wish you to show yourself a fair judge toward me, as I have written to the point and with a zeal for the truth, using insulting words against no one. Farewell. Written at Old York <note>original: "Jorci Palæogea." "Jorci" refers to York (Eboracum); "Palæogea" is a Latinization of "Old Land," likely a scholarly or poetic way of naming the location.</note>.

->IN THE YEAR 1717.<-

...did not exercise it. For indeed, the **Prince** <note>original: "Princeps"; referring to the sovereign or civil ruler.</note> is not a **Bishop** in this sense, nor does he possess or exercise **command over sacred matters** <note>original: "imperium in sacra"; the legal authority to oversee the external administration of the church.</note> as a **Bishop** would. Rather, he does so because he is the Prince and God’s **Vicar** <note>original: "Vicarius"; a representative acting on behalf of God in earthly administration.</note>, just as *Moses* exercised the **law of sacred matters** <note>original: "jus sacrorum"</note> because he was as God to *Aaron* <note>A reference to Exodus 4:16, where God tells Moses that he shall be "as God" to his brother Aaron the priest.</note>, and performed the office of King. Therefore, to demonstrate the exercise of the law of sacred matters by citing secular authors <note>original: "profanis Auctoribus"; writers from the classical or non-Christian world.</note> in order to prove the law itself is useless; for not everything that happens, *ought* to happen. 

Rather, one must learn what the law is from **canonical Scripture** <note>original: "*Scriptura canonica*"</note>, so that examples from secular history may be examined against it. To this is added the fact that if the law of sacred matters is expanded by examples from every history, then the minds of those first learning of it are distracted too much, so that they are unable to find or establish anything certain. This is especially true because the opinions of the learned vary on this particular argument. Some, due to the difficulty of the subject and because they confuse things that ought to be kept distinct, fall into error and cannot understand; others, however, favoring the **Doctors** <note>Theologians or church authorities.</note> or even the rest of the subjects against reason and **Holy Scripture** <note>original: "*Scriptura Sacra*"</note>, seek to limit the Prince and venture in their writings to strip him of his command over sacred matters. 

For these reasons, therefore, I have finally taken it upon myself to perform this labor, such as it is, so that [I might explain] the power and duty of Princes in sacred matters from

...true that through **canonical Scripture** <note>original: "Scriptura canonica"</note> alone, I would demonstrate as briefly, yet as distinctly and clearly as possible, that by explaining its true principles and foundations, nothing might be taken away from the rights of the **Prince** <note>original: "Princeps"; the civil sovereign or ruler.</note>, nor of the **Ministry** <note>original: "Ministerium"</note>, nor of any Christians whatsoever; nor should too much be attributed to any of them, but rather each one’s right should be upheld: to the Prince indeed, the **command over sacred matters** <note>original: "imperium sacrorum"; the legal authority to oversee the church's external administration</note>; to the Ministers of the church, the **ministry of sacred matters** <note>original: "ministerium sacrorum"; the performance of spiritual duties and rituals</note>; and finally, to individual Christians, the **private care of their own salvation** <note>original: "cura salutis suae privata"</note>.

Farewell. Written at **Gorinchem in the Old Country** <note>original: "Gorci Palaeogea"; Gorinchem is a city in the Netherlands.</note>.

# Table of Contents <note>original: "Index Capitum"</note>

Chapter I. On the **Right of Reformation** <note>original: "Jure reformandi"; the legal and theological right to change or restore church doctrine and practice.</note>.
Chapter II. On the **Right of Judging** <note>original: "Jure dijudicandi"</note> theological controversies.
Chapter III. On **Councils** <note>original: "Conciliis"; formal assemblies of church leaders to decide on matters of doctrine.</note>.
Chapter IV. On the **Symbolic Books** <note>original: "Libris Symbolicis"; the official creeds and confessions of faith that define a church's position.</note>.
Chapter V. On the **Religious Oath**.
Chapter VI. On the **Political Tolerance** of **Heterodox Persons** <note>original: "Heterodoxorum"; those whose religious beliefs differ from the established or official church.</note>.
Chapter VII. On ecclesiastical **Rites and Ceremonies**.
Chapter VIII. On the **Ecclesiastical Ministry**.
Chapter IX. On **Ecclesiastical Judgments**.
Chapter X. The **Church** does not hold **sovereignty over sacred matters** <note>original: "imperium sacrorum"; the supreme legal authority to govern religious institutions.</note>.
Chapter XI. The **Ministry** does not hold sovereignty over sacred matters.
Chapter XII. The **Prince** <note>The civil ruler or sovereign.</note> holds sovereignty over sacred matters by virtue of being Prince.
Chapter XIII. On the **Order** necessarily to be established within the **Three Estates** <note>original: "tribus Ordinibus"; likely referring to the traditional early modern division of the ruling Prince, the Clergy, and the People.</note>.

# MOSES THE GOD OF AARON
<note>original: "Moses Deus Aaronis"; a biblical metaphor based on Exodus 4:16 and 7:1, where God tells Moses he will be "as a god" to his brother Aaron the priest. In the context of 17th and 18th-century political theology, this was often used to argue that the civil ruler holds authority over the ecclesiastical hierarchy.</note>
->OR<-
## THE POWER AND DUTY OF PRINCES IN SACRED MATTERS
->Demonstrated from<-
### the canonical Scripture alone.

---

### CHAPTER I.
### On the Right of Reformation.
<note>original: "De Jure Reformandi"; the legal and theological principle concerning who has the authority to change, restore, or correct church practices and doctrines.</note>
->§. I.<-

Since without <term>natural religion</term> <note>The moral and religious truths accessible to all people through human reason and conscience, independent of divine revelation.</note> everyone would be permitted to do whatever they wished to others, and no commonwealth could survive, no one doubts that the Prince—or whoever holds supreme power—must take care that its core doctrines are preserved uncorrupted. However, whether the care of <term>revealed religion</term> <note>Religion specifically communicated by God through Scripture or prophets, rather than through reason alone.</note> also belongs to the Prince is a matter not fully agreed upon among scholars.

§. II. To be sure, a Prince is by no means able to establish new doctrines or new <term>Sacraments</term> <note>Sacred rites, such as Baptism or the Lord's Supper, considered to be ordained by God.</note>, nor to change or abolish religion. Neither the <term>Ministry</term> <note>The clergy or church leadership.</note> nor other Christians possess this power either, since God alone can grant eternal salvation and prescribe the means for achieving it. (Deuteronomy 4:2 <note>The verse states: "You shall not add to the word which I command you, nor take from it..."</note>).

<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: §. III.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the foundational argument that while a civil ruler (the Prince) has a duty to protect natural religion for the stability of the state, their power over revealed religion is limited; they cannot create or abolish divine laws or sacraments.</summary>
<keywords>Natural Religion, Revealed Religion, Right of Reformation, Authority of the Prince, Sacraments, Political Theology, Deuteronomy 4:2</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Chapter I, moving from the definition of the Prince's authority in §. I and II into specific limitations on how that authority should be exercised regarding the conversion of subjects.</meta>

### Chapter I. On Right
<note>original: "De Jure"; referring here to the legal and moral right or authority of the ruler.</note>

<meta>catchword: tol-</meta>

§. III. The Prince also must not compel his subjects toward the true religion, nor to hold faith in the Word of God; he must not do so, first, because of the very nature of the matter. This is because the human **intellect** cannot be forced to believe or not believe a thing, and **faith** is a persuasion of the intellect. Therefore, it is an internal act, and such acts are not capable of being subjected to human **compulsion**. Secondly, he must not do so because of the will of God, who forbids it, as seen in Luke 9:52–56. <note>In this Gospel passage, Jesus rebukes his disciples for wanting to call down fire upon a Samaritan village that did not receive Him, demonstrating that faith should not be imposed by force.</note>

§. IV. Furthermore, subjects are not to be invited to the true religion with rewards, since Christ does not desire such **hypocrites** who wish to follow him for the sake of worldly gain, as in Matthew 8:19–20. <note>Jesus warns a scribe that "the Son of Man has nowhere to lay his head," implying that following Him brings no earthly comfort or profit.</note> However, if some embrace the true faith because they see that those who hold correct religious views perform public duties and enjoy other advantages—hoping that they too might later obtain such things—this fault lies with them, not with the Prince; for God Himself gives many gifts to those who worship Him rightly.

§. V. Nor indeed should subjects be led to the truth by the Prince through fictions <note>referring to manufactured miracles or pious legends</note>, because it is unworthy of the truth for it to be supported by pretenses or lies. Otherwise, the truth might eventually be held in the same low regard as fictions, which would harm both religion and the salvation of men. Even the name **pious fraud**, <note>original: "pia fraus"; a term used for a deception practiced for a supposedly good or religious end.</note> by which such things are called, involves a <term>contradiction in terms</term> <note>original: "contradictionem in adjecto"; a logical error where the modifier (pious) contradicts the noun (fraud).</note>, as is commonly said.

§. VI. Now, the **power** and **duty** of the Prince consists in this: that [he] [removes] false religion—
<note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "tol-," which likely begins the word *tollat* (removes) or *tollenda* (to be removed) on the following page.</note>

<summary>The author argues that while a ruler has authority in religious matters, he cannot use force, bribes, or lies to convert his subjects, as faith must be an internal, voluntary conviction of the intellect.</summary>

<keywords>religious liberty, coercion, pious fraud, duties of the prince, conversion, hypocrites, intellect and faith</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument from Chapter I, §. V regarding the Prince's role. Having previously discussed what a Prince should *not* do (compulsion, bribery, or deceit), the author now shifts to the Prince's positive duty to promote true religion and protect the Word of God.</meta>

<meta>catchword: oscu-</meta>

[the Prince should] remove [falsehood], and on the contrary, propose the true religion known from the Word of God to his subjects, command them to follow it, and preserve it once it has been introduced. This is proven by the fact that Moses is called the "God of Aaron," and Princes are called "Gods" and "Vicars of God," and likewise they are called the "nursing fathers of the church." <note>The author cites Exodus 4:16, Deuteronomy 33:4, Psalm 82:6, and Isaiah 49:23. These titles were historically used to argue that a ruler has a divine mandate to care for the spiritual well-being of their people, just as a parent cares for a child.</note> Therefore, they must care for divine things above all else, so that true religion may be introduced and preserved, and so that the **pure milk of the Gospel** <note>original: "purum lac Evangelii"; a common biblical metaphor for basic, essential Christian teaching.</note> may be offered to their subjects by their authority.

§. VII. Next, we see it commanded specifically to the King, **in his capacity as King**, that he should read and keep the law of God (Deuteronomy 17:18–19, Joshua 1:8). For insofar as he is a **man**, he—along with the rest of the Israelites—has already been commanded elsewhere to read and keep the word of God (Leviticus 18:4–5, Deuteronomy 4:2, 12:32). Since the preceding and following passages discuss the office of the King, these cited words also pertain to the King’s specific duty. Therefore, a unique **safekeeping** of the divine word is required of a King, which is proper to him alone and does not belong to other men or even to Teachers. And this is <term>legislative custody</term> <note>original: "custodia legislatoria"</note>: namely, that by his authority, he keeps the word of God uncorrupted, and punishes or restores it if it has been corrupted. To this same end, a book of the law was placed in the hands of Kings at their coronation (2 Kings 11:12, 2 Chronicles 23:11). They must, therefore, be the guardians and defenders of the divine word.

God desires all people to worship Him rightly and to come to the knowledge of the truth. However, because He speaks here specifically to Kings—telling them to understand, to be instructed, and to "kiss the Son"—it is clear that they are obligated to command their subjects to also embrace the true religion. Similarly, Kings are commanded to remove all evil and to act as agents of justice on God's behalf, as seen in Proverbs 20:8 and Romans 13:4. Indeed, Christ Himself says that false prophets "work iniquity" <note>original: "operari peccatum"; or "work lawlessness."</note> (Matthew 7:23). This is not only because true piety cannot exist where true doctrine is lacking, but primarily because of the "fruits" of false doctrine which Christ mentioned earlier, and by which Christians must recognize them. This matter likewise shows that it is the office of Kings to preserve religion uncorrupted and to establish appropriate penalties for those who violate it.

§. IX. Subjects are also commanded by the Apostle Paul to pray for Kings and for all who are in high positions, so that they may lead a quiet and tranquil life in all godliness and honesty (1 Timothy 2:2). Therefore, Kings themselves, **in their capacity as Kings** <note>original: "qua Reges sunt"; referring to their official legal role rather than their private lives.</note>, must take care that their subjects live piously, think rightly about God, and worship Him correctly. For this reason, the same Apostle calls them **ministers of God** for the sake of what is good (Romans 13:4).

# Chapter I. On Law

...is the nature of **revealed religion** <note>original: "religionis revelatæ"; religion based on divine revelation rather than just human reason</note>, that it commands men not only to flee from sins through dogmas concerning the omniscience and omnipresence of God and similar principles and rules of conduct, but also to restrain—with divine help—the internal vicious impulses of the heart, from which external vicious acts proceed. From this, a greater tranquility of the **republic** <note>original: "reipublicæ"; refers generally to the state or organized political community</note> cannot fail to emerge. Furthermore, God, by His free promise, rewards piety with many prizes, as seen in **Deuteronomy 28**.

**§. XII.** The power and duty of a **pagan Prince** <term>Princeps ethnicus: a non-Christian sovereign who rules by natural right</term> is the same. Nor does such a ruler possess only the right itself, but also its exercise. For although these two things—the right and the exercise of it—can sometimes be separated by the will of the person who holds that right, a right is nonetheless fictitious and void if one is clearly not permitted to exercise it, even if they wish to. Indeed, a ruler even sins if he neglects its exercise. Examples of this matter are read in **Daniel 3:29** and **6:25, 26, 27** <note>referring to the decrees of Nebuchadnezzar and Darius regarding the worship of God</note>. **Ezra** also and **Nehemiah** did not hesitate, by the order and in the stead of the Kings of the Persians, to restore the temple of God and to organize all religious matters. And even the Holy Spirit commanded Christians to obey the pagan emperor **Nero**, in **Romans 13:1**.

**§. XIII.** This duty a pagan Prince can perceive even from the **light of nature** <note>original: "naturæ lumine"; the inherent human capacity for reason independent of biblical revelation</note> alone. For just as he ought to investigate the true way of worshipping God and the path of his own salvation—which if he does not do, he is inexcusable, according to **Romans 1:20**—so also he ought to lead his subjects to the true worship of God

<meta>catchword: lead-</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the state benefits from religion because it restrains internal immoral impulses, and asserts that even non-Christian (pagan) rulers have the divine right and duty to oversee religious practices within their realms.</summary>

This page clarifies that the Prince's obligations are both natural and scriptural, yet do not diminish his sovereign power.</meta>

->REFORMANDI.<-
<page-num>7</page-num>

...ought to lead, not only as a man, but also, and indeed much more, insofar as he is a **Prince** <term>Princeps: the sovereign or supreme ruler of a state</term>. But if indeed it were said that this duty is unknown by **natural law** <term>jus naturæ: a system of law based on human reason and the inherent nature of the world, rather than specific religious revelation</term>, as some contend, nevertheless such a Prince is obligated to it because of the mandate of God revealed in **Scripture**. This is because, once the way of eternal salvation is known, he both can and ought to recognize it. 

Nor, however, does it follow from this that the Christian religion brings any change to the **rights of Majesty** <note>original: "juribus Majestatis"; the supreme powers and legal privileges inherent to a sovereign</note>. For God, by this new precept—if indeed it were new—commanded nothing contrary to natural law. Instead, He at least added a new obligation to it, which He was able to do by the same authority by which He prescribed natural law itself and universal **positive laws** <note>man-made laws established by a government or ruler</note> even to Princes. In the meantime, the **royal rights** <term>jura regalia: the specific powers and rights belonging to a monarch by virtue of their office</term> which belong to a Prince by natural law remain preserved; they are neither removed nor diminished by this reasoning. 

Rather, royal rights are confirmed through **revealed religion**. For **Samuel** repeated the "right of the King" <note>original: "jus Regis"; referring to the description of monarchical power in 1 Samuel 8</note>—as it is called by the Holy Spirit Himself—to deter the **Israelites** from their purpose. This is a right a King possesses not only when he is first appointed or at the start of his royal duties, but also once he is established throughout his entire reign. This is clear to anyone looking at specific examples in **1 Samuel 8:9–17** and **Romans 13:6–7**. 

To ensure he uses this right correctly and prudently, he is cautioned by God in **Deuteronomy 17:16–20**. Therefore, the Christian religion brings no change to the rights of majesty.

# Chapter I. Concerning Law
<page-num>8</page-num>
->**A**<-
<meta>catchword: gione (religion)</meta>

§. XIV. After the **Prince** <term>Princeps: the sovereign or supreme ruler of a state</term> has proposed the true religion to his subjects from the word of God, with a mandate that they embrace it, they ought to show obedience, not only because of the will of God, who requires such faith and worship in His word, but also because of the will of the Prince, who has repeated and confirmed divine precepts through his own command and new law as the **Vicar of God** <term>Vicarius Dei: a deputy or representative of God on earth, acting with divine authority in earthly matters</term> from His word. Therefore, if subjects do not obey, they sin for a double reason, as they have neglected the precept not only of God, but also of their Prince (Romans 13:1-2) <note>Romans 13:1-2 is a biblical passage often cited to support the idea that all authority comes from God and resisting the ruler is resisting God's ordinance</note>. The Prince, however, ought to leave the matter to God if subjects are unwilling to obey Scripture and his law; for there is no place for coercion, punishments, and persecution. If, however, some teach false things against his prescription, they can be restrained by punishments, because this is an external act, which is subject to coercion, and can also be harmful both to the **commonwealth** <note>original: "rempublicam"; refers to the state or the public interest</note> and to religion; this is different if some merely believe false things, but do not spread them to others.

§. XV. Just as no power or right is given which men do not abuse, and no duty is so holy that it is not sometimes violated—just as even among Princes there are sometimes those who use their right and fulfill their duty incorrectly in **secular** <note>original: "profanis"; literally "profane," meaning worldly or non-religious matters</note> affairs—so the same happens regarding the power and duty of Princes in sacred matters. Hence, just like false Teachers, they sometimes lead subjects away from the true religion...

<summary>This page argues that subjects have a dual obligation to follow a ruler’s religious mandates, yet it limits the Prince's power by forbidding coercion of private belief, while allowing punishment for the public spreading of false doctrines.</summary>

(1 Kings 12:26-31).

§. XVI. However, although subjects ought to obey their Prince when he commands something that is indeed a sin on his part, but can be done by them without sinning—just as Joab, when King David from the pride of his heart ordered him to count the people, first tried to dissuade him, but because he could not persuade him, he obeyed (1 Chronicles 22:3-4 <note>The author cites chapter 22, but the story of David’s census and Joab’s protest is found in 1 Chronicles 21</note>)—nevertheless, subjects ought not to obey when a Prince orders something to be done which cannot be committed by them without sin; for one must obey God rather than men, because He is superior. And this is true not only in secular matters <note>original: "rebus profanis"</note> but also in sacred ones. Therefore, if a Prince commands that subjects follow a false religion, they nevertheless must not follow it. (Exodus 1:16-21; 2 Chronicles 11:16; Daniel 3:9-28; Daniel 6:12-13; Acts 4:19; Acts 5:29).

§. XVII. But if subjects do obey and follow a false religion proposed by a Prince, not only does the Prince sin, but they also sin and are punished, which can be seen in the case of Jeroboam and his subjects who followed his will. # CHAPTER I. ON LAW
<page-num>10</page-num>

...[they are] expelled from their own borders. (1 Kings 14:9–10; 15:29; 14:15; 2 Kings 17:23). This is exactly as if a Teacher <note>original: "Doctor"; here referring to a religious authority or professor of theology</note> misleads his listeners with false doctrine: for then both the one who misleads and those who allow themselves to be misled sin, and are punished by God.

§. XVIII. When, however, subjects rightly refuse to accept a false religion which they are commanded to follow, and the Prince <note>term: "Princeps"; the sovereign ruler of a territory. In the 17th-century context, this refers to the legal head of state who held the "Right of Reformation"</note> employs force against them for that reason, then he indeed sins. This is because he is not permitted to persecute subjects with force and punishments even when they refuse to embrace the true religion. Nevertheless, the subjects ought not to resist or oppose him with force; for this is prohibited by God in sacred matters just as it is in secular <note>original: "profanis"</note> affairs. Flight is permitted—as Elijah did (1 Kings 19:2–3) and as Christ taught His disciples (Matthew 10:23)—but resistance is not. Instead, every injury, pain, exile, and even death itself must be borne with patience. (1 Samuel 24:7, 13; 26:8–11; 2 Samuel 1:14; Matthew 26:52; Romans 13:2; 1 Peter 2:23; Revelation 13:10).

§. XIX. Nor should the Prince's authority over sacred matters <note>term: "imperium in sacra"; the legal power of a ruler to regulate the outward affairs and legal standing of the church within his territory</note> be denied because of the abuse of that right, and much less because of the fear of such abuse. For although a subject can be deprived of his own right by a Prince if he abuses it too much, the Prince nevertheless retains his right, as he is to give an account of his actions to God alone, being subject to Him alone. Furthermore, abuse would not be absent even if the command over sacred matters were granted by God to the Ministry <note>The "Ministerium" or the body of ordained clergy</note> or to other Christians.

### [THE RIGHT OF] REFORMATION
<header>REFORMANDI</header>

<note>original: "religionis" (continued from previous page)</note> [as if the care of] religion had been granted by God [to others—such as the clergy], since even among them many are found who violate the word of God, as witnessed by both the Holy Spirit and experience. Indeed, there is less danger to be feared for both the state <note>original: "respublica"; the common-wealth or civil society</note> and for religion from the **right over sacred matters** <term>jus sacrorum: the legal authority of a ruler to govern the external affairs and organization of the church within their territory</term> when the Prince <note>term: "Princeps"; the sovereign ruler</note> himself exercises it, than if others were to exercise that same power.

For the Prince has been commanded by God to introduce and preserve the true religion through his sovereign power and his laws. This command was not given to the ministry or to other subjects; the former <note>the ministers</note> ought only to teach, while the latter <note>the subjects</note> ought to attend to the private care of their own salvation. Furthermore, it is said that "the king's heart is in the hand of the Lord; He turns it wherever He will" (**Proverbs 21:1**). We see also that in the **Papacy** <note>the Roman Catholic Church system</note> almost no salvific truth remained once the Bishops, and especially the **Roman Prelate** <note>original: "Praeful romanus"; referring to the Pope</note>, took away the rights over sacred matters from the Lords of the lands. I need not even mention the calamity that **Germany** suffered because of this. By contrast, among the **Jewish people**, where the Kings (and they alone) exercised authority over sacred matters, the true religion was repeatedly restored.

§. XX. In the meantime, it must be observed that the damages and inconveniences which states often suffer do not arise from religion itself, nor from the Prince's right over sacred matters, but from the abuse of both and from the vice of men. Examples of this include: if a Prince persecutes those devoted to the true religion; if he wishes to force subjects to a religion (whether true or false) by fire and sword; or if subjects become a burden to the state under the pretense of religion and expect to be supported by it; or if those who, under the title of religion...

He clarifies that political or religious strife results from the personal vices of rulers and subjects rather than from the legal "right over sacred matters" itself.</summary>

<header>Chapter II: On the Right of Judging</header>

[reli]gion, if any stir up disturbances, if any seditiously defend or attack true or false dogmas, if any under the appearance of religion detract from the rights of the Prince <note>term: "Princeps"; the sovereign ruler</note> whether in secular or sacred matters, or if any endeavor to make the Prince fall into contempt among his subjects, or clearly turn them away from obedience.

§. XXI. What I have explained so far regarding the **right over sacred matters** <term>jus sacrorum: the legal authority of a ruler to govern the external affairs and organization of the church</term> applies differently when the Prince has promised his subjects the free exercise of religion by a treaty <note>original: "pacto"</note>, even if that religion is false. For even with heretics, agreements must be kept, and this is according to the **law of nature** <term>jus naturæ: universal moral principles known through reason rather than revelation</term>. Hence, three religions are accepted throughout Germany: the **Evangelical** or **Lutheran**, restored through the ministry of Luther two hundred years ago (which is truly universal <note>original: "catholica"</note> and truly reformed—see **Genesis 3:15, 12:3, 2:18**; **Acts 15:10-11, 16:30-31**); the **Pontifical** <note>original: "Pontificia"; the Roman Catholic Church</note> or so-called Catholic; and the likewise so-called **Reformed** <note>original: "Reformata"; the Calvinist tradition</note>. Writers on public law explain this arrangement based on the treaties between the Princes or the fundamental laws of the Empire.

### CHAPTER II.
->On the Right of judging theological controversies.<-

->§. I.<-

After it has been demonstrated that the care of all religion, even revealed religion, belongs to the Prince, it is easily understood that the power of judging theological controversies... <meta>catchword: cas (likely "casus" or "casu," meaning case or occasion)</meta>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion from the previous chapter regarding the sovereign’s authority over religious matters. <page-num>13</page-num>

<header>THEOLOGICAL CONTROVERSIES.</header>

[controversies] <note>original: "cas"; this suffix completes "theologicas" or "ecclesiasticas" from the previous page's discussion on religious disputes</note> belong to him, lest the truth be corrupted by false Teachers or any others: for he who has a right over the whole cannot be denied a right over its parts.

§. II. Indeed, every man ought to **test the Spirits** <note>original: "Spiritus probare"; a reference to 1 John 4:1, meaning to discern whether a teaching is truly from God</note> and recognize false teachers; for each shall give an account of himself to God, and the just shall live by his faith, and by his own judgment and knowledge he must be certain of the **way of salvation** <term>via salutis: the theological path or method by which a soul attains eternal life</term> (**1 John 4:1; Matthew 7:15, 16; Romans 14:12; Habakkuk 2:4**). But this judgment pertains to each person's own salvation, as a private act, so that he himself may believe and do what God has commanded, for the private guarding of the **divine word** <note>original: "verbi divini"; the Holy Scriptures</note>, and for private instruction among children, household members, and others. Hence are the warnings of Christians toward one another (**Deuteronomy 4:2, 6:6, 7; James 5:19**).

§. III. Furthermore, Teachers have the power of judging concerning divine matters, so that they may teach their listeners the saving truth, as **Pastors of souls** <note>original: "Pastores animarum"; the clergy's role in spiritual guidance</note>. But this judgment pertains to the salvation of their listeners; it is a public act indeed, but a doctrinal one, so that they may teach their listeners what they ought to believe and do according to the divine will. It is for a public guarding of the divine word indeed, but it is **ministerial** <note>meaning service-oriented or subordinate to the Word, rather than legislative</note>, and pertains to the public office of teaching. Hence are the warnings of Teachers toward listeners and others (**1 Timothy 6:20**).

§. IV. The **Prince** <note>term: "Princeps"; the sovereign ruler</note>, however, has the power of judging concerning divine matters for the salvation of his subjects

<summary>This page distinguishes between three levels of religious judgment: the private judgment of individuals for their own salvation, the ministerial judgment of clergy for teaching their congregations, and the sovereign judgment of the Prince for the welfare of the state.</summary>

<header># Chapter II. On the Right of Judging</header>

...of his subjects, pertaining to sovereign power, to an imperative public act, to the prescribing of public doctrine, and to the legislative guardianship of the **divine word** <note>original: "verbi divini"; the Holy Scriptures</note>, so that he may fortify the true opinion with public authority and command his subjects to embrace it. He does this because he is the **Vicar of God** <note>original: "Vicarius Dei"; one who represents God's authority in earthly matters</note> and the **nurturer of the church** <note>original: "ecclesiæ nutricius"; a title implying the ruler's duty to provide for and protect the church's physical and legal existence</note>. By his command, he must impose silence upon those teaching falsehoods because he is obligated to guard and preserve true religion. He must also take notice of those who, despite his commands, spread their errors to others, for he **bears the sword** <note>original: "gladium gerit"; a reference to Romans 13:4, symbolizing the state's power to punish</note> as a servant of God and an avenger of wrath against all who do evil. This ensures that the commonwealth receives no harm from religious dissensions, as he is bound to care for the **public welfare** <term>Salus publica: the safety and well-being of the state, often cited as the supreme law justifying a ruler's intervention</term>.

And this judgment belongs to the **Prince** <note>original: "Principi"; the sovereign ruler</note> alone, not only because it is enjoined upon him by God, but also because neither the ministry nor other Christians, but the Prince alone holds sovereign power. Therefore, he alone can preserve religion through his command and impose silence on false Teachers. Hence we find the commands of Princes issued to their subjects, Teachers, and all other Christians (**Daniel 6:26** <note>A reference to King Darius decreeing that all people must tremble and fear before the God of Daniel</note>).

§. V. No one, except the impious, would say that the standard for any judgment concerning matters of faith should be left to mere luck or the outcome of a physical contest. Furthermore, it is not permitted to measure the truth solely by the opinions of ancestors or by the doctrine passed down to descendants. If that were the case, the idols could not have been removed by the Israelite Kings, nor could the Gospel have been received by the **Lutherans** <note>The author uses the example of the Reformation to argue that tradition must yield to truth</note> (**Acts 18:13**). Also, of the Teachers...

...human doctrine should not be observed as the standard, because both true and false **Teachers** <note>original: "Doctores"</note> appeal to Scripture and contend that their own opinion is the true one. Otherwise, it would follow that all **Roman Catholics** <note>original: "Pontificios"; those adhering to the Papacy</note> would be joined to the assembly of the blessed, which no one would claim. 

Nor do the most votes show the truth at all times, because occasionally the greatest number consists of false Teachers, while those who teach the truth are few. This happened in the time of Elijah (1 Kings 18:29), Micaiah (1 Kings 22:6, 13), Jeremiah (chapter 26:8, 11, 16), Christ (John 7:52), and even **Luther**. Generally, it is the doctrine of the **Anti-Christ** that Christians must follow the teaching of men to recognize the truth (Acts 4:5, 6, 17, 18; John 7:47, 48, 49). 

On the contrary, the Holy Spirit directs Christians back to the law and the testimony (Isaiah 8:20). Hence, no one should put their faith in men, but every doctrine must be examined against the word of God. For this reason Paul, although a man **divinely inspired** <term>θεόπνευστος (theopneustos): literally "God-breathed"; a Greek term used to describe the divine origin of the Scriptures and the authority of the Apostles</term>, praises the **Bereans** <note>original: "Berrhoënses"; the people of Berea who, in Acts 17, were commended for checking Paul's teachings against the Scriptures</note> (Acts 17:11; John 4:42).

**§. VI.** But if no one ought to judge matters of faith based on the doctrine of men, it is even less permitted for the **Prince** <note>original: "Principi"; the sovereign ruler</note> to do so. This is because he is responsible not only for the private guarding of religion for his own salvation, but also for its public guarding...

# Chapter II. Concerning the Right of Judging
<meta>catchword: word</meta>

### §. X.
It remains true, even though it is often denied, that a **Ruler** <term>Princeps: literally "Prince" or "First Citizen," here referring to the sovereign civil authority or head of state</term> can provide laws for the intellect by prescribing to his subjects what they ought to believe from the word of God for eternal salvation. This is because coercion and punishments are not the law itself (as many suggest), nor even a part of the law. Instead, they are merely added and joined to other laws—which do not deal with saving faith—to serve as their strength and support. This necessity arises from the fault of the subjects who refuse to observe the laws (Deuteronomy 28:15). 

Certainly, no one doubts that it is permissible to pull up thorns and other useless weeds, or to eat bread without the sweat of one's brow <note>original: "sine sudore vultus"</note>—even though God threatened these as punishments for the Fall of Man (Genesis 3:18–19). Punishments are linked to laws as a motivation <note>original: "ratio": the underlying reason or incentive</note> by which men should be moved (Genesis 2:18 <note>The author likely refers to Genesis 2:17 regarding the warning of death</note>). Therefore, they are not the law itself. Similarly, the rewards that God added to His laws are likewise not the law itself nor part of it (Deuteronomy 28:1–2). 

Furthermore, the Ruler adds punishments to the **moral law** <term>lex moralis: the universal rules of right and wrong, often identified with the Ten Commandments</term>, yet the moral law itself remains perfect in its own right. More obedient subjects observe the Ruler's laws in their entirety and yet suffer no punishments; from this, it can be understood that the threat of punishment and the punishments themselves are not the law or a part of the law. The establishment of punishments is a specific type of law, known as **penal law** <note>original: "lex poenalis"</note>, because it prescribes the penalties by which a **judge** <term>judex: a civil magistrate or official responsible for enforcing legal penalties</term> is bound to inflict upon disobedient citizens. Therefore, if punishments and their execution are not the law itself nor part of it, and the Ruler [prescribes to] his subjects from...

<summary>This page argues that a sovereign ruler has the authority to prescribe religious belief based on Scripture, clarifying that legal punishments are external reinforcements rather than the substance of the law itself.</summary>

### §. XI.
Great inconveniences can befall the **commonwealth** <term>respublica: the state or public body</term> if these judgments I have mentioned are in opposition to one another. In such cases, either the Ruler persecutes his subjects, or the subjects stir up disturbances, riots, and seditions. Nevertheless, one must stand by the divine will and ordination. For this distinction is not a mere invention—as many distinctions on this subject are often fabricated against **Holy Scripture** by those who dare, not without scandal, to deny the Ruler the right of judgment over his domain. 

Instead, they attempt to transfer that right to the church under the title of "care for private salvation" <note>original: "curæ salutis privatæ"</note> (which God has enjoined on everyone) or—at least because they mention the church for appearance's sake, which they then say is represented by its ministers—to the **Teachers** <term>Doctores: the professional theologians or ordained clergy</term> under the title of the "office of teaching" mandated to them by Christ. Along with the **Papists** <term>Pontificiis: followers of the Pope and Roman Catholic doctrine</term>, they try to turn the Ruler into a mere executor of whatever the Teachers have decided. 

However, this [the Ruler's right] has its foundation in Scripture itself. Therefore, regarding any danger to the state, the whole matter must be left to God. Furthermore, the commonwealth is safe from all danger if the Ruler, the **Ministry**, and the rest of Christians each use their own right correctly and do not violate the rights of others. Finally, if the commonwealth suffers any harm from human vice in this matter, then indeed this distinc— <note>The text breaks off mid-word: "discri-" likely leading to "discrimen" or distinction/crisis</note>

<header>Chapter III. On Councils.</header>

...nevertheless <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's discussion on the limits of ecclesiastical versus civil authority.</note> holds that the **Ruler** <term>Princeps: the sovereign head of state</term> is indeed subject to God alone and shall give an account to Him alone. If, through injustice, they are met with force, imprisonment, torture, exile, or even death itself, they ought to bear these things patiently, following the example of the Prophets and the **Teachers** <term>Doctores: the recognized theologians and leaders of the early church</term> recounted in the Old and New Testaments (Romans 13:2, 4 <note>In this passage, the Apostle Paul describes the civil authority as "God's servant for your good" and an "avenger who carries out God's wrath on the wrongdoer."</note>). Furthermore, if these three kinds of judgments are clearly distinguished, then there is no danger that too much will be attributed to the Ruler, the Ministry, or the rest of the Christians, nor that the rights of any of them will be diminished.

### CHAPTER III.
### On Councils.
#### §. I.

There is no divine law commanding that **Councils** <term>Concilia: formal representative assemblies of church leaders convened to decide on doctrine or discipline</term> must be convened to judge theological controversies. Therefore, the Ruler may—out of his own knowledge and his own **scrutiny of Scripture** <term>Scripturæ scrutinium: the diligent, prayerful study of the biblical text</term>, in which he must persevere until the very end of his life (Deuteronomy 17:19 <note>This biblical law required the King to keep a copy of the Torah with him and read it daily to ensure his rule remained faithful to God’s commands.</note>)—put an end to controversies. He may command that the true opinion be taught publicly and the false one be set aside, even adding the threat of punishment if anyone should dare to refuse obedience and teach to the contrary.

<summary>This page argues that the Ruler is accountable only to God but has the authority to punish religious errors that disrupt the state. It also asserts that church councils are not biblically required, as a Ruler has the right to resolve religious disputes based on his own study of Scripture.</summary>

<header>Chapter III. On Councils.</header>

...dare. In this way, Herod could undoubtedly have dispensed with the assembly of the **Scribes** <term>Scribae: Jewish scholars and experts in the law</term> if he himself had known the place of Christ's birth from **Scripture** <term>Scriptura: the Holy Bible</term>, or had wished to investigate it for himself (Matthew 2:4 <note>In the Gospel of Matthew, King Herod calls together the chief priests and scribes to ask where the Messiah was to be born, as he was ignorant of the prophecy in Micah.</note>).

#### §. II.

However, because councils are not prohibited by any divine law, it is left to the judgment of the **Ruler** <term>Princeps: the sovereign head of state</term> whether he wishes to convoke a council when a controversy has arisen, especially if the matter is somewhat difficult. For by this method—once the opinions and arguments of both dissenting parties have been heard—it is easier for the Ruler to discern the true from the false, just as someone in a **University** <term>Academia: literally "Academy," referring to institutions of higher learning</term> who hears a **Doctor** <term>Doctor: a high-ranking teacher or theologian</term> disputing and teaching on both sides of an issue is able to discern truth from falsehood. 

In such cases, councils are indeed useful; nevertheless, they should not for that reason be considered strictly necessary. The Ruler can, even without summoning a formal Council, call to himself **Theologians** or even others who are knowledgeable in the Scriptures, so that they may instruct him more fully regarding the controversy at hand. Once this is done, he can judge from Holy Scripture which opinion is true just as effectively as if a council had been formally convoked. Therefore, it depends solely on the will of the Ruler whether councils ought to be held or not.

#### §. III.

If the Ruler decrees a council, the authority to summon it also belongs to him, because this cannot be done without the **power of command** <term>imperium: the supreme legal and administrative authority of a sovereign</term>, which he alone possesses. Hence, one reads in the **Old Testament** that the Leaders and Kings always convoked the Israelite people to receive commands.

### CHAPTER III.
### On Councils.
#### §. I.

There is no command in **divine law** <term>lex divina: laws believed to come directly from God, such as those in the Bible</term> that **Councils** <term>Concilia: formal assemblies of church leaders gathered to decide on doctrine</term> must be summoned to settle **theological controversies**. XVII. 19." This verse commands the King of Israel to keep a copy of the law with him and read it all his life to ensure he follows God’s decrees.</note>)—put an end to disputes. He may command that the true opinion be taught publicly and the false one be suppressed, even adding the threat of punishment if anyone should dare to refuse to obey and teach the opposite.

# CHAPTER III. ON COUNCILS.

**Elijah** <note>original: "Ælias"</note> also requested from the impious **King Ahab** <note>original: "Achabo Rege"</note> that he assemble the **Priests of Baal**, as seen in 1 Kings 18:20 <note>original: "I. Reg. XIIX. 20."</note>. Since it was stated earlier that a **Council** <term>concilium: a formal gathering of religious leaders to decide on doctrine or discipline</term> is summoned so that the **Prince** <term>Princeps: the sovereign head of state</term> may be instructed regarding a theological controversy, it follows that it is also placed within his own will to decide which of those who possess knowledge of **Scripture** <term>Scriptura: the Bible</term> he wishes to call to the council. This is just as he has the free power to choose whose opinions and advice he wishes to seek in secular or "profane" matters.

§. IV. Regarding the **Presidency** <term>Præsidium: the office of leading or chairing the assembly</term>, a distinction is usually made between a political and an ecclesiastical president; between the common presidency of all those assembled and that of the ministry; between the presidency of jurisdiction and of order; and between an **authoritative** <term>ἐξουσιαστικὴν (exousiastikēn): possessing governing power or command</term> and a **directive** <term>διορθωτικὴν (diorthōtikēn): managing the procedure or correcting the order of business</term> presidency. All these distinctions agree with one another. The **political presidency** is granted to the Prince, so that he may command which controversies are to be handled and how they ought to be treated, and so that he may prevent the heat of the debaters, the intensity of their emotions, quarrels, insults, external force, disturbances, riots, and more serious unrest. The **ecclesiastical presidency**, which is also called the "directory," is said to belong to the **Theologians** <term>Theologos: scholars of divinity</term>, so that one of them may collect the opinions along with the reasons of each dissenting party and see that they are recorded in writing.

§. V. And indeed, for the reason stated, such a subordinate president from among the theologians may be present in a council, but he must nevertheless be designated by the Prince; and thus far, he also [acts in] the Prince’s...

### CHAPTER III. ON COUNCILS.

...acts in his place <note>The text continues from the previous page, referring to a deputy or legate acting on behalf of the sovereign.</note>, to whose supreme and general **Presidency** <term>Præsidium: the office of leading or chairing the assembly</term> such a special and inferior presidency is subject. This arrangement ensures that the one presiding cannot favor one of the dissenting parties, but rather ensures that the opinions and reasons of both sides are collected and recorded. 

If it should please the **Prince** <term>Princeps: the sovereign ruler</term>, he may exercise both types of presidency himself or through a **legate** <term>legatus: an appointed representative</term>. This is indeed preferable, not only for the reason I have just stated, but also because this "special presidency" is usually called "ecclesiastical" due to a so-called "internal ecclesiastical power." This power is a fabricated concept made up of the duty of teaching and other parts of the ministry, over which—it is claimed—the Prince has no legal right, and which supposedly belongs to **Theologians** alone. They also call this the "care of internal sacred matters," deliberately confusing the **governance of sacred things** <term>imperium sacrorum: the state's legal authority over religious institutions</term> with the **ministry of sacred things** <term>ministerium sacrorum: the actual performance of religious rites and teaching</term>. This error, however, is effectively refuted if the Prince himself, or his legate, exercises the entire Presidency and separates no part of it from his authority.

§. VI. Therefore, the Prince is even more capable of undertaking the Presidency in all matters within a **Council** <term>Concilium: a formal assembly for deciding church doctrine</term>, where it is not yet a matter of compelling subjects to follow the true religion, but rather first laying out the controversy and debating it. Additionally, this very thing is done for the sake of the Prince,

§. VII. In a council, everyone must show from the **Holy Scriptures** what they have decided and why they have so decided, according to the command of the **Prince** <term>Princeps: the sovereign ruler or head of state</term>. Although **Theologians** are rightly employed because they can handle the word of God more solidly, accurately, and diligently, if they nevertheless take it upon themselves to go beyond the <note>original: "τό" (Greek), meaning "the [act of]"</note> teaching and attempt to usurp the right of prescribing public doctrine, they then truly invade and violate the **governance of sacred matters** <term>imperium sacrorum: the state's legal authority over religious institutions and public order</term>. For the judgment to define governance and public doctrine belongs to the Prince alone. Nevertheless, the Prince can also debate both sides with those present in the Council, so that the truth may finally appear more clearly.

§. VIII. After there has been sufficient debate on both sides, it is the Prince’s duty to examine the opinions of everyone against the word of God—since all, both true and false Teachers, claim their own opinion is true—so that he may understand which opinion is true and which, on the contrary, is false. Once the true opinion is identified, he ought to confirm and guard it by his authority and law, so that all subjects believe accordingly, and specifically that the Teachers teach accordingly, both because of the command of God and because of the command of the Prince (see **Romans 13:2** <note>A biblical passage often cited to support the idea that resisting the governing authorities is resisting what God has appointed.</note>). If they then refuse to have faith in the truth, the matter must be left to God, for the **intellect cannot be coerced**.

...cannot <note>original: "neqvit"; this completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the Prince's command</note>. If, however, anyone dares to teach falsehoods against the command of the **Prince**, they can be restrained with punishments, for this is an external act. Indeed, **Peter** and **John** ignored the silence imposed upon them; however, because of the command of our **Savior** and the prompting <note>original: "instinctum," referring to internal divine inspiration</note> of the **Holy Spirit**, they rightly chose not to obey men (Acts 4:18–20; Luke 24:47–49). Today, however, it is not enough merely to claim divine inspiration; it must be proven and made manifest through the successful outcome of those efforts.

**§. IX.** This right also belongs to a **Prince** who follows a false religion or is entirely a pagan <note>original: "gentilis," referring to a non-Christian or heathen ruler</note>. The Apostles **Peter** and **Paul** themselves did not refuse to plead their case when accused on account of religion before pagan and ungodly Princes and judges—such as the **Sanhedrin** <term>Synedrium: the supreme religious and legal council in Jerusalem</term> in Jerusalem, **Gallio**, **Felix**, **Festus**, and the **Roman Emperor**—but they even appealed to them (Acts 4:9, 10, 18, 19; combined with Luke 24:46, 47; Acts 18:13, 14; 24:10, 14, 15, 21; 25:6, 10). **Christ** also, the supreme Teacher, when arrested because of His teaching, did not deny that **Pilate** held power over Him, but instead declared that this power had been given to him by God. And while those who disagree present the reasons for their views from the **Scriptures**, the word of God remains just as effective for such a Prince (or one sent by him) as it is for other men, so that he may be enlightened (Acts 13:12; 26:28). If, however, they refuse to recognize the truth, or if they [misuse] their power once the truth is known...

In that case, it is clear they met by common consent, and that by the common consent of the entire church present there (not a "representative" church) they approved the opinion of **Peter** and **James**, because they judged it to be in agreement with **Scripture**. **§. XI.** A **Council** is incorrectly called a <term>representative church <note>original: "ecclesia repraesentativa"</note></term>, as if the gathered **Doctors** <note>The term "Doctors" here refers to learned teachers and professors of theology</note> were the core of the community and held power delegated to them by each of their respective churches, and thus represented them. Indeed, **Doctors** are called to councils by the **Prince**, so that controversies may be proposed and explained more accurately and diligently by each of the dissenting parties, so that it may then be easier for the **Prince** to discern the true from the false. 

Others besides **Theologians** can also be called to councils; when these individuals are similarly commanded by the **Prince**, they must teach what they think. This practice shows that even the distinction between **Doctors** and **Hearers** <note>original: "Auditores"; the lay members or non-teaching members of the congregation</note>—which is maintained within the church—is absent in a **Council**. Therefore, because **Theologians** (just like others imbued with knowledge of **Scripture**) must speak their opinions and—to put it in a word—teach, and are called by the **Prince** for this specific purpose, it is manifest that they have no need of a delegation from the church. 

Nor are they even sent by the church, much less do they hold power delegated from it. The church uses them as ministers for sacred rites, but it has not given them the right of judging matters of faith in its stead. **§. XIII.** Regarding the judgment that **Doctors** <note>original: "Doctores"; referring to learned theologians or professors of divinity</note> possess by virtue of their **office of teaching**, they do not require a delegation from the church for this; rather, they hold it—like all parts of the ministry—from Christ, even though the **Prince** holds and exercises the right of designating those who ought to be ministers of the church. From Christ, however, they are sent for no other purpose than to teach. Yet, because of their office of teaching and their greater skill and fitness for handling **Scripture**, neither the **Prince** (for the purpose of defining public doctrine) nor individual **Christians** (for the purpose of their own salvation) should be excluded from exercising judgment. Just as the ministers of the church should not be excluded from the judgment belonging to their office of teaching simply because the **Prince** has the right to govern, they likewise should not be excluded because of the private concern each individual has for their own salvation. On the contrary, **Doctors** ought to be those fit individuals who can handle **Scripture** more solidly, accurately, and thoroughly, so that it may be easier for both the **Prince** (for governance) and for individual **Christians** (for their salvation) to discern truth from falsehood out of the **Scripture**. Therefore, it is clear that the <term>representative church <note>original: "ecclesia repraesentativa"; a concept where a small group of clergy claims to represent the authority of the entire body of believers</note></term> can in no way be defended.

**§. XIV.** Likewise, a **Council** cannot be called a "representative church," as if delegates of all **three Orders** <note>original: "tres Ordines"; referring to the traditional Three Estates of society: the Clergy, the Nobility/Rulers, and the Commons/Laity</note> were present in it. ...wishes <note>original: "vult"; completing the sentence from the previous page</note>, and consequently he alone can choose those who ought to be present at a council; on the other hand, subjects can prescribe nothing to him in this matter, nor send anyone who has not been called by the **Prince**. Furthermore, the **three Estates** <note>original: "tres Ordines"; historically referring to the Clergy, the Nobility, and the Commoners or Bürgers</note> do not possess **sacred authority** <note>original: "imperium sacrorum"; the right to govern religious affairs</note>, and thus they can delegate it to no one; rather, the **Prince** alone exercises sacred authority during the Council or after the Council. And since the judgment regarding public doctrine that belongs to the **Prince** is included as a part of his sacred right, while the estates or the subjects as a whole do not have sovereign authority, they also cannot delegate that part of authority to others—whether to **Doctors** <note>theology professors</note> or to **heads of households** <note>original: "Patribusfamilias"</note>.

The **Prince** also does not "represent" the church, nor does he hold a right delegated from it; nor can the church delegate any right to the **Prince**, the **Doctors**, and the **heads of households** simultaneously. If it could, the **Prince** would merely be a colleague with his subjects and a sharer in sacred authority. Instead, the **Prince** alone holds and exercises all authority—as in secular matters, so also in sacred ones—as the sovereign or the **Vicar of God** <note>original: "Vicarius Dei"; here used to mean God's earthly representative in governance</note> by virtue of supreme power. This leaves to the subjects nothing but the private care for their own salvation, and to the **Doctors** the specific duty of teaching.

**§. XV.** Once the shell has been removed, it is now clear that the "**representative church**" <note>original: "ecclesiam repræsentativam"; the idea that a council of prelates represents the whole body of the church</note> is the core of the papacy, a Popish doctrine and a Papistical non-entity. <page-num>31</page-num>
<header>### CHAP. III. ON COUNCILS.</header>

...to determine <note>original: "decidere" or "definire"; completing the verb from the previous page</note> those things which the church believes—namely, the **representative church** <term>representative church (ecclesia repraesentativa): the idea, often argued by Roman Catholic and some high-church Protestant theologians, that a council of clergy stands in for the entire body of believers</term> or the **ministry**.

In truth, however, there is no place for representation in matters of faith; consequently, delegation <note>the act of giving one's authority or right to another</note> cannot occur. A Christian ought not to delegate his judgment regarding his own salvation or regarding private doctrine to either a **Prince**, a **Doctor** <note>a professor or learned teacher of theology</note>, or any other Christian. Nor can he be represented by any man; rather, he himself falls into ruin if he follows false doctrine, whether commanded by the **Prince** or led astray by a **Doctor** or another Christian.

A **Doctor** likewise ought not to delegate his judgment regarding the salvation of his listeners, or regarding the public office of teaching, to either the **Prince**, another **Doctor**, or another Christian. Nor can he be represented by another; instead, he will be numbered among the false prophets by Christ if he teaches false doctrine, whether commanded by the **Prince** or led astray by another.

So also the **Prince** ought not to delegate his judgment regarding the salvation of his subjects, or regarding the definition of public doctrine, to a **Doctor** or another Christian, nor can he be represented by them. Rather, he himself must render an account to God if—on account of the false doctrine of **Doctors** or others, and having neglected the **Scrutiny of Scripture** and failed to recognize the truth—he commands by his own law that errors be taught publicly. 

Therefore, since the **representative church** is a **non-entity** <note>original: "non ens"; a philosophical term meaning a thing that has no real existence</note>, theologians cannot, under this name, thrust themselves into a partnership of **sacred authority** <note>original: "imperium sacrorum"; the right to govern religious affairs</note>. Indeed, such a thing cannot be done without sacrilege.

<header>CHAPTER IV. ON THE SYMBOLIC BOOKS.</header>
<meta>catchword: ver-</meta>

### CHAPTER IV.
## On the Symbolic Books.

#### §. I.

Since in theological controversies both true and false Doctors appeal to **Scripture**, yet the latter attach a foreign meaning to its words, it is therefore sometimes useful to explain the articles of faith—and especially those that are disputed—in specific books drawn from the **Word of God**. By giving these books the force of law for public teaching, they are called **Symbols** <term>Symbols (symbola): from the Greek word for a "sign" or "token," these are official creeds or confessions of faith used to identify authorized church doctrine</term>. There can be no doubt that this practice is permitted; for a **Prince** <note>the sovereign ruler or civil magistrate</note> must guard the true religion within his domain because he is the **Vicar of God** <term>Vicar of God (Vicarius Dei): a ruler acting as God’s representative on earth to maintain justice and piety</term>. He must preserve pure and uncorrupted nourishment for the souls of his subjects because he is the **nourisher of the church** <note>original: "nutrix ecclesiae"; a traditional title for Christian rulers based on Isaiah 49:23, emphasizing their duty to protect and provide for the church</note>, and he must take care that the **commonwealth** <note>original: "respublica"</note> does not suffer harm from varied doctrine or from the dissensions of Doctors and others, since the care of public safety belongs to him. Therefore, he lawfully employs **Symbols** as suitable means to obtain that end.

§. II. Those to whom the task has been assigned by the **Prince** draft the **confession** <note>a formal statement of religious beliefs</note>. The Prince may select for this purpose anyone possessing knowledge of **sacred Scripture**. Because the **Prince** himself must give an account to God for the administration of his realm—in sacred matters just as in secular ones—it follows that such a writing must...

The author argues that the Prince, as God's representative, has the legal right and duty to establish these doctrinal standards to protect the spiritual and physical well-being of the state.</summary>
