<header>Chapter IV. On the Symbolic Books.</header>

[...the Prince's duty is] to examine [them by] the Word of God, and to condemn, correct, and change those things which he understands to be contrary either to Scripture or to public safety, and, where there is need, to add something to it or take it away. For neither ought a Prince to do anything in the **government of sacred matters** <term>government of sacred matters (imperio Sacrorum): the ruler's authority to regulate the external affairs and legal standing of the church</term> without judgment, nor follow with blind impulse and obedience those things which the **Doctors** <note>original: "Doctores"; refers to learned theologians or authorized church teachers</note> hand down; but he ought to judge divine matters from the Word of God, so that he may understand what the true doctrine is—whether the Doctors are true Doctors through whom Christ speaks and is heard, or indeed false ones through whom the **Father of Lies** <note>a traditional reference to the Devil</note> speaks and is heard. This is all the more necessary because not even individual Christians can have the certainty of their salvation without their own judgment concerning divine matters. Furthermore, it is the nature of animals, which lack reason, to act without judgment, but not of men—especially in matters of faith. Therefore, just as authority over religion itself cannot be denied to the Prince, so also the judgment concerning religion and the distinction between true and false opinions—which is necessary for exercising that authority rightly—must not be denied to him.

§. III. After this has been done, if the Prince then confirms such a book by his own law, then at last it begins to be a **Symbol** <term>Symbol (Symbolum): from the Greek word for a "sign" or "token," these are official creeds or confessions of faith used to identify authorized church doctrine</term>, and it can be called a publicly received book. It therefore possesses public authority because of the Prince's command, who has added a new obligation by his mandate to the obligation that exists by God’s command—which likewise must not be neglected, according to **Romans 13:1-2** <note>a biblical passage teaching that all authority is derived from God and that resisting the authorities means resisting what God has appointed</note>. <header>CHAPTER IV. CONCERNING BOOKS</header>

...subjects are bound by law, so that they do not decide otherwise, and specifically the **Doctors** <note>original: "Doctores"; the learned theologians and authorized teachers of the church</note>, so that they do not teach otherwise. Therefore, a **Confession** <term>Confession (Confessio): a formal statement of faith or doctrine, such as the Augsburg Confession, which defines the beliefs of a specific religious group</term> of this kind must be observed by subjects in their believing and in their teaching, because it is a **Symbol** <term>Symbol (Symbolum): a creed or foundational statement of doctrine that serves as a standard for a church</term> or a book acting as the norm for public doctrine, established from the Word of God by the sovereign command of the **Prince** <note>original: "Princeps"; the secular head of state or ruler</note>. 

On the other hand, it is not true that a confession becomes a "Symbol" simply because it is observed by subjects in their believing and teaching. For the Prince alone can grant public authority or the force of law to such a Confession, because he alone holds sovereign power—not the subjects. Therefore, the law of the Prince alone creates the Symbol; the obedience of the subjects in believing and teaching, or their approval, does not. Rather, these things follow the Symbol; they do not create it.

§. IV. If, however, the Prince does not confirm a confession written by Doctors or other Christians with his own law, but nevertheless permits it—having recognized that there is nothing in it contrary to either the commonwealth or **Scripture** <note>The Holy Bible</note>—and allows a congregation of one or more churches to retain that confession, then it is not truly a Symbol. It lacks public authority and binds no one by way of legal command. Instead, the members of that congregation approve it by common consent and adopt the opinions written therein as their own. The same holds true if the Doctors introduce a book into the church while the Prince is neglecting or even persecuting the church; for in this case also, from co- <note>The text breaks off mid-word, likely intended to be "common consent" (consensu)</note>

## CONCERNING SYMBOLS ##

<sig>C 2</sig>

[...com]mon consent is valid, not by sovereign command, seeing as neither the church nor the ministers of the church possess such power. Nevertheless, against these **Confessions**—which the church retains by common consent (at least while the **Prince** <note>original: "Princeps"; the secular ruler or sovereign</note> does not contradict it or neglects the church)—its ministers ought not to teach. This is not indeed because of the Prince’s law, for that is lacking in this case (wherefore such a Confession is not a **Symbol**), but because of a pact; for at the start of his office, the minister promised the church that he would be willing to teach those things which the church itself believes and holds as true.

§. V. Therefore, subjects have no part in the establishing of **Symbols**. For although **Theologians** are customarily employed to draft a confession of faith—because they, above all others, can best know by their way of life and ought to know by their duty of teaching, in what manners of speaking the truth may most conveniently be explained and defended—nevertheless, their confession binds no one by way of command. For they do not have **sovereign authority** <note>original: "imperium"</note>, since nothing besides <term>the act of teaching</term> <note>original: "τὸ docere"; a mix of the Greek article and Latin verb</note> has been commanded to them by God, Titus 1:7. Furthermore, the Prince can choose one from several confessions of the **Teachers** <note>original: "Doctores"</note> to which he may add the force of law. This is a manifest sign that the confession of the Teachers does not inherently have the force of law or public authority, but involves nothing besides doctrine, which as a law binds no one. The Prince can also use the labor of others who are not Teachers, yet possess knowledge of **Scripture**, in drafting a confession

<page-num>34</page-num>

### CHAPTER IV. CONCERNING BOOKS ###

...subjects are bound by law, so that they may not determine otherwise, and specifically the **Teachers** <note>original: "Doctores"; referring to theologians and doctors of the church</note>, so that they may not teach otherwise. Therefore, a **Confession** <note>a formal statement of religious belief</note> of this kind must be observed by subjects in their believing and in their teaching, because it is a **Symbol** <note>original: "Symbolum"; here meaning an official, legally-binding creed</note> or a book serving as a norm for public doctrine, established from the word of God by the **sovereign command** <note>original: "Principis imperio"</note> of the **Prince** <note>original: "Princeps"; the ruler or head of state</note>. Conversely, it is not true that such a confession becomes a Symbol simply because it is observed by subjects in their believing and teaching; for the Prince alone can add public authority or the force of law to such a Confession, because he alone possesses **sovereign power** <note>original: "imperium"</note>, whereas the subjects do not. Therefore, the law of the Prince alone creates a Symbol, and not the obedience or approval of the subjects in their believing and teaching; rather, these actions follow the Symbol, they do not create it.

§. IV. But if indeed the Prince does not confirm a confession written by Teachers or other Christians with his own law, yet nevertheless permits it—because he recognizes that there is nothing in it contrary to either the state or the **Scriptures**—and allows the assembly of one or more churches to retain that confession, then indeed it is not a **Symbol**. It does not possess public authority and binds no one by way of command; instead, the members of that assembly approve it by **common consent** and make the opinions written therein their own. The same is true if the Teachers—at a time when the Prince is neglecting or persecuting the church—introduce some book into the church; for here also, from com- <note>This unfinished word "com-" connects to "common consent" on the following page.</note>

->CONCERNING SYMBOLS<- <note>original: "SYMBOLICIS"; referring to the official creeds or "Symbolic books" of a church</note>

<meta>catchword: confession</meta>

...prevails by **common consent**, not by sovereign command <note>original: "imperio"</note>, since neither the church nor the ministers of the church possess such power. Nevertheless, ministers should not teach against these **Confessions**, which the church retains by common consent—at least while the **Prince** <note>the sovereign or ruler</note> does not oppose it or simply neglects the church. For at the beginning of his service, the minister promised the church that he would teach what the church itself believes and holds as true.

§. V. Therefore, subjects have no role in the creation of **Symbols**. For although **Theologians** are usually employed to write a confession of faith—because they, by their way of life, are best able to know, and by their duty of teaching, are required to know which modes of speech can most suitably explain and defend the truth—nevertheless, their confession binds no one by way of sovereign command. They do not possess such command, since nothing has been commanded of them by God except <term>the act of teaching</term> <note>original: "τὸ docere"</note> (Titus 1:7). Furthermore, the Prince can choose one confession from many written by **Doctors** <note>learned theologians or "Doctors of the Church"</note> to which he may add the force of law. This is a clear indication that a confession by Doctors does not have the force of law or public authority on its own; beyond the doctrine itself—which binds no one as a law—it involves nothing else. The Prince can also use the work of others who are not Doctors, but who nevertheless have knowledge of the **Scripture**, in writing a confession...

Finally, nothing has been enjoined by God upon Theologians beyond the duty of teaching, nor upon the rest of Christians beyond the care for their private salvation. The **Prince**, however, must preserve religion through his sovereign command, and thus define public doctrine. Therefore, he alone must also exercise the right of prescribing a **Creed**, and indeed he alone is able, because he alone possesses the sovereign command, without which no one is legally obligated to believe or teach according to another's will.

§. VI. If, then, the Prince alone can and should prescribe the doctrine to be taught publicly and establish **Creeds**, it follows that the subjects—both the **Theologians** and the rest of the Christians—are bound to [accept] the truths [contained] in the **Creeds**...

# ON SYMBOLIC BOOKS.

G 4

<meta>catchword: mo-</meta>

As was stated above, the right of judgment belongs to each individual for their own salvation, and specifically to the **Teachers** <note>original: "Doctoribus"; refers to those authorized to teach doctrine within the church or university</note> regarding the ministry or the office of teaching. However, if a false **Creed** <note>original: "Symbolum"</note> was introduced in the past, then the **Prince** <note>the sovereign ruler</note> not only can, but also must, abolish it—just as the **Kings of Israel** removed the worship of idols and were praised by God for doing so. Nevertheless, a **treaty** <note>original: "Pactum"</note> concerning the free exercise of religion granted to subjects who adhere to a different faith binds the Prince; he must not change their **Confessions** <note>formal statements of faith</note>, even if they contain false doctrine, but must let them remain inviolate. For agreements must be kept even with those of a different faith <note>original: "heterodoxis"</note> because of **natural law** <note>original: "jus naturæ"; the philosophical concept of a universal law of right and wrong that is inherent in human nature and binding on all rulers</note>.

This is called the **oath of religion** <note>original: "juramentum religionis"</note>. By this very act, future Teachers of the church signify that they hold all things read in the Creeds to be true, and they also promise that they do not wish to teach against them. This cannot be disapproved of, because even those who are appointed to civil offices—

<header>Chapter V. On the Oath</header>

...are moved <note>original: "moventur"; completing the sentence from the previous page regarding the appointment of teachers</note>, they first promise their loyalty to the **Prince** <note>the sovereign ruler</note> with an oath and bind themselves to him, just as the rest of the subjects also swear an oath to the laws enacted concerning secular matters.

->§. II.<-

If anyone does not regard the **Symbolic Books** <note>original: "libros Symbolicos"; the official confessions of faith, such as the Augsburg Confession, which define a church's doctrines</note> as true, he is permitted to admit just that, and he ought not to undertake the office of teaching; for no one is compelled to take this oath. Indeed, if someone who has already sworn to the **Creeds** <note>original: "Symbola"</note> later begins to disagree with them and favors a different opinion, he must report this to the Prince and resign his office, which is then conferred upon another who agrees with the Creeds. For since an oath regarding an unlawful thing has no binding force, he can no longer be bound by the oath of religion from the moment he begins to regard the Creeds, to which he swore, as false. It is, however, in the interest of the Prince that such ministers of the church are chosen who do not disagree with the Symbolic Books, so that true religion may be preserved, and so that no disturbances among the subjects or other evils arise in the **commonwealth** <note>original: "republica"</note> from uncertain doctrine and disagreements—the care of all these things rests upon the Prince.

->§. III.<-

It is therefore manifest that no force is brought against the consciences of men by the oath of religion, for no one is compelled by it to hold as true what he thinks is false; rather, such a person should neither swear the oath nor undertake the office of **Teacher** <note>original: "Doctoris"; a formal position authorized to instruct in theology</note>. For this reason, this oath is required at least so that he who wishes to undertake the office may show that he already believes those things which are contained in the Creeds—

<header>ON RELIGION.</header>

...are held <note>original: "nentur"; completing the sentence from the previous page: "what they do not believe. Rather..."</note>, and, because he believes, he simultaneously gives his sworn word that he will not teach against those doctrines. Therefore, all coercion of the intellect is absent here; the goal is simply that those who obtain an **ecclesiastical office** <note>original: "functione ecclesiastica"; a position of leadership or teaching within the church</note> should not be those who stray from true religion or harbor doctrines in their minds that are contrary to the purpose of the **commonwealth** <note>original: "republicae"</note>.

->§. IV.<-

The examples that are read in **Holy Scripture** <note>the Bible</note> also allow no one to doubt the justice of the **oath of religion** <note>original: "juramenti religionis"</note>. For the **Israelite people** <note>the biblical nation of Israel</note> took that oath quite often and rejoiced because of it; they were even driven by this reasoning to seek God, who allowed himself to be found by them and gave them rest on every side. Furthermore, the Kings who demanded this oath are said to have done what pleased God. <note>See: Deuteronomy 29:12–14; 2 Kings 11:4, 17; 2 Chronicles 15:12–15, in conjunction with chapter 14:2; and Nehemiah 10:29.</note>

Now indeed, the **Princes** and **Kings** of the New Testament era, just like Moses and the other Kings of the Old Testament, are "Gods" <note>a reference to Psalm 82:6, often used in this period to describe the divine authority of rulers</note> and **vicars of God** <note>original: "vicarii Dei"; those who act as earthly deputies or representatives of divine authority</note>; therefore, by the same right, they can demand an oath of religion regarding the **Symbolic books** <note>original: "libros Symbolicos"; the official confessions of faith, such as the Augsburg Confession, which define a church's core doctrines</note>. For these also contain the **Word of God** <note>original: "verbum Dei"</note>, and certainly no one ought to swear an oath unless they are firmly convinced of them. Nor is the dignity of the divine word diminished in any way by the Creeds or the oath taken upon them; rather, it is preserved through these means. For the Creeds are established and the oath is taken upon them so that a foreign meaning cannot be falsely attached to the Word of God, nor can anyone teach against it.

### Chapter V. On the Oath

§. V. There are some, however, who are displeased by this oath, for they think that while the **Israelite people** <note>the biblical nation of Ancient Israel</note> swore an oath regarding **things to be done** <note>original: "agendis" or "agenda"; refers to outward conduct and legal obedience</note>, they did not swear regarding **things to be believed** <note>original: "credendis" or "credenda"; refers to internal faith and doctrine</note>. Yet in truth, they did not only swear to what they wished to do, but also to what they believed. For in their oath to worship the true God and keep his laws, this was simultaneously, silently, and implicitly contained: that they certainly believed the idols of the heathens were not true Gods, but that He—specifically the one who led them out of Egypt—was the true God whom they promised they were willing to serve. Likewise, by that very act, they simultaneously swore that they certainly believed an obligation lay upon them to worship that God to whom they promised obedience.

And generally, it is true that things to be done are also things to be believed; for regarding the things we do, it is necessary that we first believe we ought to do them. Conversely, things to be believed are also things to be done. For because we believe in one God—the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit—and that He is omnipresent and all-knowing, and that the Son of God redeemed us by taking on flesh, we must therefore also worship and adore God as the one <term>Thrice-Holy</term> <note>original Greek: τρισάγιον (*trisagion*); a traditional hymn or title meaning "thrice holy," used here to emphasize the three persons of the Trinity</note>. We must keep His omnipresence and omniscience before our eyes throughout our lives, and live piously, as befits those who have been redeemed. Thus, the example of the Israelites shows that it is lawful to swear not only about things to be done, but also about things to be believed.

§. VI. This is easily understood, simply

<meta>The text ends mid-sentence. The catchword at the bottom of the Latin page "juran-" indicates the next page will likely begin with a word related to the oath (juramentum).</meta>

<summary>This page argues that religious oaths properly cover both internal beliefs and outward actions, using the biblical Israelites to show that swearing to serve God inherently requires a belief in His divinity. The author maintains that faith and conduct are inseparable, as one must believe in a duty before they can perform it.</summary>

<keywords>oath of religion, agenda and credenda, Israelite people, Trinity, Trisagion, redemption, religious obligation, worship</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and theological argument regarding the "Oath of Religion." Having established that the state can require oaths of faith, the author now addresses the specific phrasing of these oaths—arguing that they must be absolute rather than conditional to ensure the stability of the church and state.</meta>

-> OF RELIGION. <-

...one must swear an oath to the **Symbolic books** <term>Symbolic books</term> <note>original: *libros Symbolicos*; the official confessions of faith, such as the Augsburg Confession, which define a church's specific doctrines</note> because they agree with the word of God, and not merely *insofar as* they agree with the word of God. For if it were permitted to swear conditionally, then nothing would prevent someone from swearing even to false Creeds, since at least some truth is found within them. Furthermore, if such swearing were allowed, there would be no practical use for this oath; for the one swearing could nevertheless teach against those books afterward. It is in the interest of the **Prince** <note>the sovereign or secular ruler responsible for the territory</note> to prevent this, as he is the one who must preserve both true religion and the prosperity of the state.

Consequently, such a conditional oath would also be an abuse of the divine name, which is forbidden by God's law. Moreover, an oath must be interpreted according to the mind and intention of the one demanding it, not the one performing it. All **mental reservation** <term>mental reservation</term> <note>original: *reservatio mentalis*; the practice of making a statement while secretly holding a different meaning in one's mind to avoid a lie or a commitment</note> is illicit, lest agreements and oaths—which are essential to human society—become useless.

It follows, therefore, that he who has sworn to the Symbolic books cannot lawfully teach against them, even if, during the swearing, he used a mental reservation or swore with a condition. Finally, no one is forced either to believe or to teach. Rather, just as every Christian has the right of free judgment regarding divine matters for their private knowledge, and the Prince has the right to define **public doctrine** <note>original: *doctrinam publicam*; the officially sanctioned teaching of the state church</note>, so too is it the case for those seeking the **ministry** <note>original: *ministerium*; the office of a pastor or priest</note> and the duty of teaching. If someone believes that the Creeds are not true, they are neither forced nor ought they to swear the oath or take up the office of teaching; indeed, they should even resign <note>original: *remittere*; the text cuts off at "remit-", but the catchword "tere" completes the word for "resign" or "give back"</note> an office already undertaken—

<summary>This page argues that oaths of religious office must be binding and literal rather than conditional, preventing clergy from using "mental reservations" to teach against established church doctrines.</summary>
<keywords>Symbolic books, oaths, mental reservation, Prince, public doctrine, ministry, religious freedom, legal interpretation</keywords>

...ly he must, if he changes his opinion after he has sworn. Therefore, a future minister of the church ought to read the **Creeds** <term>Creeds</term> <note>original: *Symbola*; the foundational statements of faith, such as the Apostles' or Nicene Creed</note> and examine them against the word of God; if he judges them to be true, he should swear to them simply, without conditions and without profaning the divine name. If, however, he considers them false, he should neither swear the oath nor even seek the office.

§ VII. If someone takes an oath on the **Symbolic books** <term>Symbolic books</term> <note>the official collections of doctrine for a specific denomination, such as the Lutheran Book of Concord</note> having never read them—thinking perhaps it is enough if he has only read **Luther’s Small Catechism** <note>a basic manual of Christian doctrine written by Martin Luther in 1529 for the education of children and the unlearned</note> and heard Protestant sermons; or likewise, if someone swears even though he does not hold them to be true, simply so that he may obtain the office of teaching; and likewise, if someone, having taken this oath, changes his mind but does not disclose it so that the office might be committed to another—all these things do not happen through the fault of the **Prince** <note>the sovereign ruler of the territory</note> who requires the oath of religion, but by the vice of those who perform it.

## On the political tolerance of those who hold different beliefs.

->§ I.<-

After the Prince has commanded his subjects to embrace the true religion and has used all means to preserve it, if he nevertheless cannot bring matters to the point where all subjects follow the truth, it is not necessary for him to drive out from his borders those who dissent from the true religion...

# THE POLITICS OF THE HETERODOX <note>original: *Politica Heterodoxorum*; referring to the state's policies toward "heterodox" people—those whose religious beliefs differ from the official or "orthodox" doctrine</note>.

...commands, but he has the power to tolerate them in his lands—not only if their number is so great that he would either have to abandon the kingdom himself or expel a large part of the citizens if he refused to have them as citizens due to their **heterodoxy**, but also when their number is not so great. This is because no harm results to the **republic** <term>republic</term> <note>original: *respublica*; here referring generally to the state or the "commonwealth" rather than a specific form of representative government</note> from the fact that they do not believe correctly; for they can be good citizens as long as they perform those duties which the **law of nature** <term>law of nature</term> <note>original: *jus naturae*; a system of universal ethical principles held to be common to all humans and discoverable through reason</note> requires. Indeed, they understand themselves to be all the more bound to these duties because they are indebted to the **Prince** <note>the sovereign ruler</note> for the special favor of being allowed to inhabit his lands despite disagreeing with him in religion. This is called **political tolerance** <term>political tolerance</term> <note>original: *tolerantia politica*; a legal or civic acceptance of religious diversity for the sake of social stability, rather than a theological endorsement of the beliefs</note>, which even **Holy Scripture** permits, as can be seen in **Matthew 13:30** <note>the Parable of the Tares: "Let both [wheat and weeds] grow together until the harvest," a verse often used to argue that the state should not execute or expel heretics</note>. Christians are also commanded to obey a pagan Emperor in **Romans 13:1**; hence, conversely, a faithful Prince can also defend subjects who do not think correctly about divine matters within his borders.

## § II.
This political tolerance is also useful, both regarding what pertains to the **republic**, since the number of citizens is preserved by it—or even increased by receiving others who have been ordered to emigrate by another Prince—and some price is received for their defense <note>referring to taxes or fees paid by dissenters in exchange for legal protection</note>. It is also useful regarding what concerns the true religion, which the ministers of the church then demonstrate and confirm more diligently with suitable words and arguments from **Scripture**, because they know that dissenters are present who observe their doctrine, and

It justifies this tolerance through both practical benefits to the state (economic and population growth) and biblical precedents like the Parable of the Tares.</summary>

### Chapter VI: On Tolerance

...that they must take care that the **orthodox** <note>those who adhere to the established or "correct" religious doctrines of the state</note> are not led astray by **heterodox** <note>those whose religious beliefs differ from the official doctrine; dissenters</note> citizens, and that they must apply a moral **compulsion** <term>compulsion</term> <note>original Greek: *anagkasmon*; referencing the "compel them to come in" passage from Luke 14:23, interpreted here as persuasive instruction rather than physical force</note>, by which even the dissenters might be led to the true religion through more solid doctrine, as seen in **Luke 14:23**.

**§. III.** However, this **political tolerance** is not a command from God; hence, a **Prince** <note>the sovereign ruler</note> can command those subjects who do not follow the true religion to depart from his borders—not only if he fears some disadvantage to religion or the state from their presence, but even if it simply pleases him to do so. Nevertheless, emigration must be ordered without the loss of honor or the loss of property (which can either be sold or managed by others). For such a loss would constitute a punishment, and there is no place for human punishment, coercion, or persecution in matters of faith, which pertain to the intellect; instead, God alone wishes to punish those who do not believe His word, as noted in **Matthew 13:30** <note>The Parable of the Tares, where the householder allows weeds to grow alongside wheat until the harvest, often cited as a biblical argument against the forced execution of heretics</note>. Furthermore, simple emigration cannot be considered a punishment, just as no injury is done to a Prince when subjects move of their own accord into another Prince's lands—provided this is not done at an inopportune time, such as during a war or another public calamity that requires expenses that ought to be fairly borne by all those to whom the Prince previously provided security.

**§. IV.** Indeed, this political tolerance is to be praised if the Prince ensures that the heterodox subjects are instructed by faithful and orthodox teachers, or even if [he permits] them to attend temples with other citizens...

<summary>This page argues that while a ruler has the right to expel religious dissenters from his territory, he must do so without seizing their property or harming their reputation, as faith is a matter of the mind and only God may punish unbelief. It emphasizes "moral compulsion"—persuasion through teaching—rather than physical force as the proper way to handle religious differences.</summary>

### POLITICS REGARDING DISSENTERS

...[he] commands them to go to those places where the uncorrupted Word of God is preached, as in **James 5:19–20** <note>original: *Jac. V. 19. 20.*</note>. For by this reasoning, subjects are not forced into faith itself, but rather toward the **means** <note>referring to the *media gratiae* or "means of grace," the instruments like scripture and preaching through which the divine is said to act</note> through which the **Holy Spirit** <note>original: *Spiritus Sanctus*</note> generates faith. They are then able to obey the Word of God, or, if they do not care for their own salvation, they are also able to resist.

**§. V.** This **political tolerance** could even be harmful if **heterodox** <note>original: *heterodoxis*; those whose religious beliefs differ from the state-sanctioned doctrine</note> people were permitted to spread their errors—which are contrary to the true religion—to others. It is therefore the duty of the **Prince** to impose silence upon them so they do not lead others astray, but rather keep their errors to themselves if they refuse to abandon them. They can also be compelled to this silence through punishments, because this is an **external act** <note>In legal philosophy of this era, the state had no power over the "internal" mind, but full power over "external" actions like speaking or publishing</note>. Such acts are subject to **coercion** <term>coactionis</term> and can harm others who allow themselves to be misled; thus, they ought to be punished by the Prince as the avenger of all evil. If, nonetheless, they do not cease to spread their errors to others through speech, writings, or hymns, or if they violate civil and external peace, cause injury to citizens, or attempt to stir up unrest and **seditions**, then they are justly and deservedly expelled from the territory or even subjected to more severe punishments.

**§. VI.** This political tolerance sometimes becomes necessary if it is promised by a **pact** <note>original: *pacto*; a formal agreement or treaty</note>; for pacts, even those made with heretics, must be observed according to **natural law** <term>jus naturale</term> <note>The system of law based on reason and nature, held to be binding on all humans regardless of their specific religion</note>. **Germany** provides an example, in which three religions—the **Lutheran**, and those which are called **Catholic** and **Reformed** <note>Refers to the three confessions officially recognized within the Holy Roman Empire following the Peace of Westphalia in 1648</note>—due to the treaties of the Princes of the empire...

# CHAPTER VI. ON THE POLITICAL TOLERANCE OF HETERODOXY

...[of the Em]pire <note>original: *perii*, likely completing *imperii* from the previous page</note> are to be tolerated among themselves. However, the power to tolerate other religions besides these three <note>The "three religions" refers to the only three legally recognized denominations in the Holy Roman Empire after the Peace of Westphalia (1648): Catholicism, Lutheranism, and the Reformed (Calvinist) church</note> is not taken away from them by this fact. The intention of those who signed the peace treaties was that it should be necessary to tolerate these three religions, but not that it was forbidden for them to tolerate others besides those, if they so wished. Writers of **public law** <term>juris publici</term> demonstrate this matter more extensively from the fundamental laws of the Empire.

**§. VII.** This **political tolerance** is contrasted with **ecclesiastical tolerance**, the latter of which is prohibited by divine law. Therefore, although it is permitted for the **orthodox** <note>In this context, "orthodox" refers to those adhering to the state-sanctioned Lutheran confession</note> to live and converse with **heterodox** people in their capacity as citizens, and although they must even maintain external peace with them, nevertheless, insofar as they are heterodox, individual Christians must shun them and guard themselves against their doctrine. Specifically, the ministers of the church ought to refute their erroneous doctrine and warn the orthodox not to follow them. The **Prince** <note>The secular ruler</note> must ensure that this refutation is done with gentleness and without personal insult, as such insults would violate political tolerance.

However, the Prince cannot command that the names of the heterodox be omitted by the ministers of the church, because this would constitute ecclesiastical tolerance, which is forbidden by God. **Christ** also warned his Disciples to specifically beware of the "leaven of the **Pharisees**" <note>A reference to Matthew 16:6, where "leaven" represents corrupting influence or false teaching</note>, and in the **Book of Revelation** <note>original: *Apocalypsi*</note> the **Holy Spirit** even mentioned the **Nicolaitans** <note>An early Christian sect mentioned in Revelation 2:6 and 2:15, usually cited here as a precedent for naming specific heretical groups</note> by name. Nor are such names considered insulting, since it cannot...

It argues that while the state ensures civil peace, the church maintains its right to name and refute false doctrines.</summary>

### CHAPTER VII.
### Concerning ecclesiastical rites and ceremonies.

#### §. I.

Since enough has been said about religion itself, we proceed to ecclesiastical rites and ceremonies, without which the external worship of God cannot be carried out. And these things themselves have not been defined by God, because the Holy Spirit gave at least a general precept that all things should be done decently and in order, and for the building up of the church, 1 Corinthians 14:40. However, the worship of God would be chaotic <note>original: *tumultuarius*</note> unless certain rites are defined; hence it is clear that the **Sovereign** <term>Princeps</term> <note>In this context, the "Prince" refers to the territorial ruler who held legal authority over both civil and religious administration within his lands</note> can prescribe certain rites to be observed in the administration of divine worship, so that all **disorder** <note>original Greek: *ἀταξία* (ataxia)</note> and confusion may be avoided.

#### §. II. 

Furthermore, no one will doubt that the Prince has this right if they consider that all those things which belong to **natural liberty** <term>libertatis naturalis</term> <note>The concept that individuals surrender certain inherent freedoms to a ruler in exchange for social order</note> are transferred to the Prince from the moment his subjects submitted themselves to his power. Therefore, just as with other **associations** <term>Collegiis</term> in the state, the Prince can also prescribe to the church what they ought to follow in those matters which are neither commanded nor forbidden by God <note>These are often called "adiaphora" or "matters of indifference"—actions that are not essential to salvation but necessary for orderly practice</note>. Furthermore, the members of the church are not obligated to obey the will of one another,

<summary>This page introduces Chapter VII, arguing that because God did not specify exact rituals, the territorial Sovereign (Prince) has the legal right to establish church ceremonies to ensure public order and prevent confusion.</summary>

# CHAPTER VII. CONCERNING RITES
**Page 50**

...for indeed all others are destitute of sovereign power. Therefore, since the **Prince** <term>Princeps</term> <note>In this context, the ruler or sovereign</note> alone holds the **power of command** <term>imperium</term>, he alone can also command his subjects as to which rites they ought to employ in **divine worship** <term>cultus divinus</term>. Furthermore, if subjects could rightfully claim for themselves the right of prescribing **ecclesiastical ceremonies** <term>ceremoniæ ecclefiasticæ</term>, the Prince’s power would not be supreme, and there would exist a dual power and command within the state, which would be contrary to reason and against **Holy Scripture** <term>Sacra Scriptura</term>. Moreover, the Prince is the **Vicar of God** <note>original: *Dei Vicarius*</note> and the **nourisher of the church** <note>original: *ecclefiæ nutricius*; a traditional reference to Isaiah 49:23, viewing the ruler as a protective foster-father to the faith</note>; he must therefore see to it that divine matters are properly managed and that all things are done for the building up of the church. Finally, this view is confirmed by the examples of Kings recorded in Scripture: Joshua 6:3, 4, 5, 7; 2 Samuel 6:1, 2; 1 Kings 6; and Jonah 3:7, 8.

### §. III. 

For this reason, if the Prince seeks the advice of his **Ministers** <note>original: *Miniftri*; referring here to the clergy or ecclesiastical advisors</note> in these matters—who may show what they believe based on Holy Scripture, the examples of the **Apostolic church** <term>ecclefia Apoftolica</term>, and even experience—he himself must nevertheless be the final judge. He must decide whether such rites contribute to the beauty and splendor of divine worship, to good order in the church, and to the stirring of devotion and pious thoughts. He must also take care that excessive rites are not introduced, because internal worship and the devotion of the soul are often lost when the mind is overwhelmed by a multitude of ceremonies. It is evident from reading the **New Testament** <term>Novum Teftamentum</term> that in the times of the Apostles, the rites and ceremonies employed were simpler and fewer.

**Sovereign/Prince**, **Sovereign power**, **Divine worship**, **Ecclesiastical ceremonies**, **Vicar of God**, **Nourisher of the church**, **Holy Scripture**, **Ministers/Clergy**, **Apostolic church**, **New Testament**

->## AND ECCLESIASTICAL CEREMONIES.<-

<page-num>51</page-num>

§. IV. After the **Sovereign** <term>Princeps</term> has examined all options and chosen the best, only then does he empower the **liturgical books** <term>libri liturgici</term>—in which the rites and ceremonies are recorded—with public authority, adding to them the force of law. Once this is done, it is the duty of the subjects to do nothing to the contrary; for in those matters which God has neither commanded nor forbidden, they must follow the will and decree of the Sovereign (Romans 13:2). Moreover, ecclesiastical rites and ceremonies are in themselves **indifferent matters** <note>original: *indifferentes*; in theology, these are called *adiaphora*, practices that are not essential to salvation and thus can be regulated by human authority</note>, being neither commanded nor forbidden by God, as the Holy Spirit has provided only a general instruction <note>referring to the biblical principle that all things should be done "decently and in order"</note>. Therefore, subjects are bound by their **conscience** <term>conscientia</term> to observe whatever the Sovereign prescribes regarding them. By this reasoning, **Christian liberty** <term>libertas Christiana</term> is not taken away; rather, at least that **license** <note>original: *licentia*; here meaning an undisciplined or chaotic use of freedom</note> is removed which disturbs order, opposes the dignity of **divine worship** <term>cultus divinus</term>, causes offense to the pious, and hinders devotion. However, if subjects are unwilling to comply, and the Sovereign finds it necessary to waive any part of his right due to the fear of riot or rebellion, he should nevertheless ensure the subjects are warned. They must not believe that the rite they stubbornly wish to retain is commanded by God, or that it is an essential part of divine worship, or that they are pleasing God by that means; instead, they should cast out from their souls any notion of necessity or merit <note>The author warns that even if a ruler allows a certain custom for the sake of peace, the people should not mistakenly think that custom is a divine requirement or earns them "points" with God</note>.

§. V. Since only he who has the power to establish a **law** <term>lex</term> can also abolish it, it is evident that subjects cannot change or remove anything regarding the rites prescribed by the Sovereign; only the Sovereign...

### CHAPTER VII. ON RITES

<page-num>52</page-num>

[the Sovereign] has the right to abolish them. But the **Sovereign** <term>Princeps</term> also should not easily abolish or change the rites typically used in the external **worship of God** <term>cultus Dei</term>, because frequent changes to rites would conflict with the order and decorum necessary in the church. A Sovereign is permitted to change rites only if superstition or the belief that such acts earn divine merit <note>original: *opinio meriti*; the idea that performing a ritual automatically earns God's favor, a concept often critiqued during the Reformation</note> demands it, or if a different rite would provide greater spiritual edification. We read, for instance, that **King Hezekiah** <term>Hiskias Rex</term> destroyed the **bronze serpent** <term>serpens æneus</term>. Although it had been established by God himself and kept as a memorial until that time—and indeed could have been kept even longer—he destroyed it because the people had fallen into superstition (2 Kings 18:4–7). In such a case, the subjects must be reminded that such a rite is not part of the worship prescribed by God itself, but is an **indifferent matter** <note>original: *res indifferens*; also known as *adiaphora*, these are practices neither commanded nor forbidden by Scripture</note> which can be performed or omitted without sin, provided religion remains intact. Thus, no offense need be feared. <note>The original Latin text contains a typographical error, "metueudum" for "metuendum" (to be feared).</note>

§. VI. **Christian liberty** <term>libertas Christiana</term> is clearly visible in the different rites of different churches; however, this diversity does not destroy the unity of the faith itself. For the unity of the faith, it is sufficient if the substance of divine worship remains as God himself prescribed—namely, that the Word of God is preached without corruption, and the **Sacraments** <term>Sacramenta</term> are administered along with the "keys" <note>referring to the "Power of the Keys," the authority to forgive or retain sins</note> according to the revealed will of God. The **rites** <term>ritus</term> themselves are added to worship merely as its ornaments and aids; they are not defined by God, and consequently, they can be determined in one way by one church and in another way by another. Thus the use

<header>ON ECCLESIASTICAL CEREMONIES.</header>

[...the use of] **lights** <note>original Latin: *luminum*; the text picks up mid-word from the previous page</note> in the administration of the **Holy Supper** <note>the Eucharist or Communion</note> is rightly omitted in some places because it was not commanded by Christ; conversely, it is rightly used in other places because it was not forbidden by Christ. For the same reason, two lights are seen on the altar in some churches, and three in others, because clearly all these things are **indifferent matters** <note>original: *indifferentia*; also known as adiaphora, these are practices neither required nor prohibited by Scripture</note> and are to be determined one way or the other according to the **Sovereign’s** <term>Princeps</term> will.

### CHAPTER VIII.
## On the Ecclesiastical Ministry.

#### §. I.

Having spoken thus far of the Sovereign's power over religion itself and other sacred matters, we must now speak of the ministers assigned to them. Indeed, the ministry is not strictly necessary for the **safety of the state** <term>salus reipublicæ</term>. For although **revealed religion** <note>religion based on divine revelation, such as the Bible, as opposed to reason alone</note> is useful to the state and contributes to its **well-being** <note>original: *τό bene [esse]*; a Greek and Latin philosophical term referring to a state's flourishing or quality of life</note>, it is nevertheless not necessary, nor is it required for its **mere existence** <note>original: *τό esse*; referring to the basic survival or essential functioning of a state</note>. Rather, **natural religion** <note>moral and religious truths discoverable through human reason and conscience</note> alone suffices for the safety of the state, as it prescribes enough duties toward God, toward oneself, and toward others. On the contrary, revealed religion is very often harmful to states—not indeed by its own nature, but through the **fault of men** <note>original: *vitio hominum*</note>,

# CHAPTER VIII.
## On the Ministry.

<meta>catchword: can,</meta>

...of men <note>original: *hominum*; the text completes a word from the previous page</note> who abuse it, and who, under the title of religion, deny obedience to the highest powers or attempt to usurp their **royal rights** <term>jura regalia</term>, whether in secular matters or in sacred matters. Therefore, **revealed religion** <note>religion based on divine revelation, such as the Bible, as opposed to reason alone</note> is useful to the state, and sometimes even harmful, but it is not necessary; consequently, the ministry is also not necessary for the **safety of the state** <term>salus reipublicæ</term>.

#### §. II.

To be sure, the public worship of God is necessary even in such a nation that uses only **natural religion** <note>theology based on reason and observation of the natural world, rather than revelation</note>, even if it is corrupted by many fictions. This is so that the piety of the citizens may be evident to one another, because otherwise they could not trust each other nor live happily; we also read that such nations have Priests. But truly, this is because they have received knowledge of the worship of God and the Priests employed in it from their ancestors or through rumor; for all the sacred rites of the gentiles are from the imitation of the divine worship employed by the first men and the **Jewish people**. Therefore, if a nation were given which had heard nothing from other nations about Priests employed in their worship—whether true or erroneous—that nation could not help but be ignorant even of the name of "Priests." Nevertheless, it could govern the state quietly and conveniently by the help of natural religion alone, whose precepts in the public worship of such a nation either the **King** himself, or those sent by him, or those whom they might command in individual congregations, could explain. Therefore, it is true, I believe, that the ministry can indeed be useful to the state,

...**could be** <note>original: *posse*; this completes the sentence from the previous page: "that nation could [be]..."</note>, and even harmful, but it is not necessary, just as revealed religion itself <note>religion based on divine revelation, like the Bible, rather than just human reason</note> is not necessary [for the state's basic existence].

#### §. III.

But just as **revealed religion** <term>religio revelata</term> is necessary for the **salvation of souls** <term>salus animarum</term>—since **natural religion** <note>theology based on reason and observation of nature</note> is not sufficient to attain eternal salvation—so also the **ministry** <term>ministerium</term> is necessary for the salvation of souls. It was instituted for this very purpose by Christ himself, as seen in **Mark 16:15** <note>"Go into all the world and preach the gospel to every creature"</note>. Conversely, a promiscuous and disorderly license for teaching is forbidden; it is not permitted for anyone to teach publicly unless they have been **legitimately called** <term>vocatio</term>, according to **Romans 10:15** <note>"And how shall they preach unless they are sent?"</note>, **Jeremiah 23:21**, **Hebrews 5:4**, **James 3:1**, **Ephesians 4:12–13**, and **1 Corinthians 12:29**. For although all Christians are **Spiritual Priests** <term>Sacerdotes Spirituales</term>, as noted in **Revelation 1:6** and **Romans 12:1**, one cannot conclude from this that everyone has a right to the external priesthood and the public office of teaching; just as all Christians are "spiritual kings," yet they cannot all be worldly kings for that reason.

#### §. IV.

When God does not send ministers of the church **immediately** <note>directly or without human mediation, as he did with the Apostles</note>, the church itself possesses the **right of calling** <term>jus vocandi</term>. This is a matter of **natural liberty** <note>the inherent right of a society to choose its own leaders</note>, although it is less correctly called a "permissive divine law," since that term does not encompass the obligation which is the effect of law. Hence, in the **primitive church** <term>ecclesia primitiva</term>, either the Holy Spirit designated the ministers of the church, or the church elected them by common consent, or the Apostles appointed them with the church’s delegation or approval. This was necessary because the pagan Emperors despised and **oppressed** <note>original: *opprimerentur*; the text cuts off here and continues on the next page</note> the Christians...

### CHAPTER VIII. ON THE MINISTRY
<meta>Header: CAP. IIX. DE MINISTERIO</meta>

...[governed] <note>original: *regebant*; completing the word from the previous page</note>, and did not exercise the **right over sacred matters** <term>jus sacrorum</term>. This is supported by **Acts 1:24**, **6:5**, **13:2**, **14:23**, and **Titus 1:5** <note>These verses describe the early church selecting its own leaders, which the author is about to contextualize within the rights of a sovereign ruler</note>.

#### §. V.

However, when a **Prince** <term>Princeps</term> <note>the sovereign secular ruler or head of state</note> takes responsibility for the care of the church, he is also able to choose the ministers of the church himself. This is because natural liberty can be restricted or even entirely removed by the Prince, based on the fact that men have consented to his rule. Therefore, just as he can restrict or take away the freedom to write a will or the freedom to hunt, he can also take away the freedom to appoint church ministers and claim the **right of calling** <term>jus vocandi</term> <note>the legal authority to nominate or appoint clergy</note> for himself. It is also the duty of subjects to live according to the will and the instructions of those in power in all matters that are neither commanded nor forbidden by God, according to **Romans 13:2**. 

Furthermore, in the entirety of the Holy Scripture, there is no commandment stating that the subjects must choose the church’s ministers, nor is there any prohibition against a Prince choosing them. Therefore, if the Prince claims the right of appointment for himself, the subjects are obligated to obey him. The **supreme power** <term>summa potestas</term> is subject to God alone; hence, subjects cannot possess the right of appointment against the Prince's will, because otherwise it would follow that his power is not truly supreme. Finally, this point is confirmed by the examples of the **Kings of the Old Testament**, who designated the High Priest, the priests, the singers, and other administrators for sacred duties, as seen in **1 Kings 2:35**, **1 Chronicles 24:4–6**, **1 Chronicles 25:4–5**, and **2 Chronicles 17:7–9**.

#### §. VI.

There are, however, those who are convinced that the church itself ought to choose its own ministers,

# ON ECCLESIASTICAL MATTERS

Furthermore, these people bring forward as an argument that Christ gave the **keys** <term>claves</term> <note>original: *claves*; referring to the "power of the keys" from Matthew 16, symbolizing the authority to admit or exclude people from the church</note> to the whole church; consequently, they argue that the **right of electing** <term>jus eligendi</term> belongs to the entire church as well.

But Scripture testifies that Christ gave the keys not to the church [as a whole], but to its ministers—to the Apostles and their successors (**Matthew 16:19**, **18:18**). Whatever fictions are dreamed up here regarding "representation" <note>the idea that the clergy only represent the power inherent in the whole congregation</note> should be left to the **Papists** <note>original: *Pontificiis*; a term used by Protestant writers to refer to Roman Catholics</note>. Generally speaking, the church body does not possess the right to preach the word, administer the sacraments, or use the keys. If it were otherwise, every member of the church could be a Doctor and a Priest. For whoever possesses a right is also able to exercise it; a right that someone "has" but cannot actually use, even if they wished to, is a hollow and fabricated right. Therefore, only the ministers of the church possess this right, having received it from Christ.

The situation is comparable to **Majesty** <term>Majestas</term> <note>sovereignty or supreme civil power</note>—defined here as supreme power—and to **marital power** <term>potestas maritalis</term>. Majesty comes directly from God (**Romans 13:1**, **John 19:11**), but the question of who specifically should hold that power depends on the agreements and covenants of men coming together into a single commonwealth. Similarly, a husband becomes a husband by the consent and will of his wife, yet he receives his marital authority from God. 

In the same way, the ministers of the church receive the duties of their **ministry** <term>ministerium</term> from Christ, whose messengers and legates they are; however, who specifically ought to be the ministers of the church is a matter to be determined by the **Prince** <note>the sovereign civil ruler</note>. Furthermore, Christ did not acquire the **right of calling** <note>the legal authority to appoint clergy</note> for the church with his blood, but rather salvation...

### CHAPTER VIII. ON THE MINISTRY

<meta>catchword: confe-</meta>

...[eternal] salvation <note>completing the phrase *salutem æternam* from the previous page</note>, and those very people who maintain this position say that the **right of calling** <term>jus vocandi</term> <note>the legal and ecclesiastical right to invite or appoint someone to a pastoral office</note> is annexed to the inheritance of the kingdom of heaven; thus, it is not the inheritance itself. Regarding their objection—that Princes who exercised the right of electing were sometimes snatched away by swift and violent deaths—this is a fallacy of "non-cause as cause" <note>original: *non causæ, ut causæ*; a logical error where something is incorrectly identified as the cause of an event (in this case, suggesting God punished the Princes for choosing ministers)</note>, provided no abuse of power occurred. Therefore, the right of calling remains a matter of natural liberty, which the Prince is able to take up.

§. VII. Therefore, the Prince can, by his own right, choose the ministers of the church. Indeed, it is generally even better if he exercises this right himself than if he leaves it to his subjects. For a **popular election** <term>popularis electio</term> can easily stir up dissensions, factions, turmoils, or seditions because of the great number of voters. Such a multitude, moved by bribery, favoritism, influence, and various other passions, are often pulled in different directions. These outcomes are not so easily feared if a single Prince—who cannot be moved by favor or bribery as easily as a private individual—chooses the ministers of the church. But if the Prince should also abuse this right, he cannot be deprived of it for that reason; for he must render an account to God alone, as he is subject to Him alone.

§. VIII. When the Prince claims the right of calling for himself, the congregation of the church over which a **Pastor** is to be placed holds a "negative vote," or the right of refusing the person elected by the Prince, if that person is **heterodox** <note>original: *heterodoxus*; holding beliefs that differ from the established or "correct" doctrine of the church</note> or unfit for teaching. This is because all people must beware of false Prophets, as stated in **Matthew 7:15**. However, for that reason [they do] not... <note>the sentence remains incomplete at the end of the page</note>

While the Prince holds this authority, the congregation retains a "negative vote" to reject any candidate who is doctrinally unsound or incompetent.</summary>

# ON ECCLESIASTICAL LAW

...it follows <note>completing the catchword *confe-* from the previous page</note> that while members of the church may have a voice, this "negative vote" is entirely different from the **right of electing** <term>jus eligendi</term>. Indeed, the reasons for which the people might disapprove of an election are subsequently examined to determine whether those reasons are just or not. If the reasons are not just, the election made by the **Prince** <term>Princeps</term> <note>the sovereign ruler or head of state</note> remains valid, and neither the congregation nor the individual dissenter is heeded. If the cause for objection is just, the person elected is not established as **Pastor**; nevertheless, the Prince retains his full **right of calling** <term>jus vocandi</term> <note>the legal authority to appoint someone to a clerical office</note> and proceeds to call another candidate who is orthodox in faith and fit to teach.

§. IX. Nor, indeed, should it be thought that the right of calling belongs to the **Theologians** <term>Theologi</term> simply because they are the ones who conduct the examination of candidates. For Christ mandated nothing to them beyond the duty of teaching. Therefore, just as they preach the Word of God in Christ’s stead, they likewise examine future church ministers and judge their suitability in the stead and place of the Prince. It is the Prince’s responsibility to ensure that true religion is preserved and that the peace of the commonwealth is not disturbed; to this end, he provides the church with ministers who are orthodox and faithful subjects. 

Experience also shows that, in addition to theologians, **secular officials** <term>Politici</term> <note>referring here to civil administrators or lay counselors involved in state-church governance</note> are often involved in examining future pastors. However, they similarly lack the right of calling unless it has been specifically granted to them by the Prince. Indeed, many candidates are examined and judged fit to teach who are nevertheless never "called" to a position—a clear sign that the examination process is distinct from the act of election itself. The examination is conducted simply to establish whether the person to be called is suitable...

### CHAPTER VIII. ON THE MINISTRY

§. X. Because those matters which belong to natural liberty—and are not specifically defined by divine law—can be determined in either direction, the **Prince** <term>Princeps</term> <note>the sovereign ruler</note> is able to leave the **right of electing** <term>jus eligendi</term> to his subjects. This is similar to how he grants other societies and colleges the freedom to manage their own affairs by common consent. Nevertheless, just as he maintains supreme inspection and **sovereign command** <term>imperium</term> over all societies that exist within the **commonwealth** <term>respublica</term>, he must keep an even more watchful eye upon the **church** <term>ecclesia</term>. This is because this particular body has a greater potential to impact both the safety of the state and the truth of religion. 

Therefore, if he leaves the election to the subjects, he must then take care that no dissensions, factions, or riots arise from that election, and that the commonwealth does not suffer damage as a result. He must ensure that **heterodox** <note>original: *heterodoxi*; those holding beliefs contrary to established church doctrine</note> individuals or those who would lead the citizens astray are not chosen as **Pastors**, so that neither true religion nor the safety of the state is put at risk. If he detects a flaw of this kind in the election, the Prince himself can choose someone who is suitable. The supreme power of the ruler always remains, even if he has granted a certain liberty or right to his subjects. -># ON ECCLESIASTICAL MATTERS<-

...belongs to **theologians** <note>original: *theologis*</note>, and therefore they should not claim for themselves the **right of electing** <term>jus eligendi</term> ministers of the church under this name—as if it were a part of the **sovereign power over sacred matters** <term>imperium in sacra</term>. Nor should they turn the **Prince** into a mere executor who would only have to confirm the person elected by the people and judged fit by the **Doctors** <note>academic or theological experts</note>, adding his authority simply as a matter of weight.

§. XI. It is now clear that it depends on the will of the Prince whether he wishes to exercise the **right of calling** <term>jus vocandi</term> <note>the formal legal invitation to take up a ministerial office</note> himself, or to leave it to his subjects. Therefore, this is a matter of choice and is changeable, because the right of calling is not necessarily granted to the Prince nor to the subjects by **divine law** <term>jure divino</term>, nor is it taken away from them by it. 

On the contrary, however, it is a matter of divine and immutable law that, when the Prince chooses, the subjects have the **right of rejecting** <note>original: *jus recusandi*</note> the one elected by the Prince, if that person can be excluded from the sacred office for a just cause. It is also a matter of divine and immutable law that, when the liberty of electing is left to the subjects, the Prince has the right of disapproving the election if it is harmful either to religion or to the **commonwealth** <note>original: *reipublicae*</note>. The protection of both of these belongs to him; thus, he cannot grant that right to the Doctors or other subjects and abdicate it himself.

§. XII. The **Ordination** <term>ordinatio</term> <note>the formal ceremony of consecrating a minister</note> of the one elected is a matter of **human law** <term>juris humani</term> and is performed in the name of the Prince; consequently, it can even be performed by political officials. And Scripture testifies that **Moses**, who presided over the Israelite people by God's appointment and performed the office of a King, **consecrated** <note>original catchword: *secra-* from *consecravit*</note> **Aaron** and **Eleazar** the High Priests...

-># CHAPTER VIII. ON THE MINISTRY<-

...consecrated <note>original: *fecravisse* (sacravisse), referring to the ritual setting apart of priests</note>, Exodus 28:1, 41; Deuteronomy 8:12, 13 <note>The Latin text uses "IIX" as a Roman numeral for 8</note>. In the New Testament, it is indeed read that the Apostles ordained ministers of the church, but the **right over sacred matters** <term>jus sacrorum</term> <note>the legal authority to govern religious affairs within a territory</note> cannot be measured by the actions of the primitive church; for at that time, the church was despised, abandoned, or plainly oppressed by the emperors. Therefore, since these emperors did not exercise their right over sacred matters, the church was certainly able to entrust the right of ordaining to the Apostles.

Furthermore, the **laying on of hands** <term>impositio manuum</term> was an effective conferring of the **Holy Spirit** <note>original: *Spiritus Sancti*</note>, and was thus a unique and divine gift. The Apostles used it not only for future ministers of the church, but also for other believers (Acts 8:17) and even for the sick, so that they might recover (Acts 28:8). Today, however, the successors of the Apostles no longer enjoy such a miraculous gift.

For this reason, no argument can be shown as to why **Ordination** <term>Ordinatio</term> must necessarily be performed by **theologians**; it might just as well be performed by **civil authorities** <note>original: *Politicis*, referring to officials of the state</note>, because it is carried out in the name of the **Prince** <note>the sovereign ruler or head of state</note>, and because the example of Moses <note>who was a civil leader, not a priest</note> is found in Scripture. In the meantime, it should not be condemned that this task is usually entrusted to theologians. Since the Prince himself has the power to ordain the chosen person, he also has the freedom to choose whom he wishes to assign to that duty. Therefore, prelates or other theologians act as deputies of the Prince; they can at least teach in the place of Christ, but they have no further mandates from Christ. The **laying on of hands**, however, is a **matter of indifference** <term>res indifferens</term> <note>a theological term for a practice that is neither commanded nor forbidden by God, and thus up to human discretion</note>, just like Ordination as a whole—being neither commanded nor prohibited by God—and therefore it is certainly permissible to employ it during Ordination.

<summary>This page argues that ordination is a legal ceremony derived from the sovereign's authority rather than a divine power unique to clergy, noting that the miraculous "laying on of hands" ended with the Apostles.</summary>

# CONCERNING THE CHURCH

§. XIII. Furthermore, the **Prince** <note>the sovereign ruler</note> holds command over the ministers of the church who have already been appointed. For "every soul must be subject to the governing authorities" <note>original: *omnis enim anima potestati supereminenti subjecta esse debet*, quoting Romans 13:1</note>; it follows, therefore, that **theologians** must also be subject to him. This is true not only insofar as they are citizens and members of the state, but also insofar as they are theologians, and with respect to their duty of teaching or their ministry. 

For although their office was prescribed by Christ himself—consisting of the preaching of the word and the administration of the **Sacraments** and the **keys** <term>Power of the Keys</term> <note>original: *clavium*, the authority to grant or deny church fellowship and spiritual absolution</note>, such that the Prince cannot by any means abolish these parts of the ecclesiastical ministry—nevertheless, because he is the **minister of God** for all that is good and must preserve true religion and punish every evil, it is clearly apparent that authority over the ministers of the church and their office cannot be denied to him. This is because many evils are committed concerning religion, and these are even more serious than others because they violate religion itself (Romans 13:1, 2, 4). 

This very point can be understood through the example of **marital authority** <note>original: *potestatis maritalis*</note>. The Prince cannot abolish the rights of a husband, because that authority comes from God. Nevertheless, the supreme inspection and command of the Prince extends to that relationship as well, to ensure that no one abuses it. In the same way, the components of the ministry are from Christ and cannot be abolished by the Prince. However, it is the Prince’s duty to ensure that those duties are correctly carried out by the ministers of the church. He does this because he is the **Vicar of God** <term>Vicarius Dei</term> <note>one who acts as a representative or deputy of God in earthly governance</note>, the **Ruler of the entire state** <note>original: *Rector totius reipublicae*</note>, and God’s minister for good—excluding no one—and the punisher of evil, likewise excluding no one.

->§. XIV.<-

<meta>catchword: §. XIV.</meta>

<summary>This page argues that while the spiritual duties of clergy (preaching and sacraments) are ordained by Christ, the sovereign holds supreme legal authority over the ministers themselves to prevent abuse and ensure order. The author uses the analogy of marriage to show that a ruler can oversee a divinely-instituted relationship without abolishing its sacred nature.</summary>

# CHAPTER VIII. CONCERNING THE MINISTRY

->[con]secrated [them], Exodus 28:1, 41; Deuteronomy 8:12, 13.<-

In the New Testament, indeed, we read that the Apostles ordained ministers of the church, but the **right of sacred affairs** <note>original: *jus sacrorum*, referring to the legal authority to govern religious institutions</note> cannot be measured by the deeds of the primitive church; for it was then despised, abandoned, or clearly oppressed by the emperors. Therefore, since these emperors did not exercise their right of sacred affairs, the church was certainly able to entrust the right of ordaining to the Apostles. 

Furthermore, the **laying on of hands** <term>Impositio manuum</term> was an effective conferring of the **Holy Spirit**, and was thus a singular and divine gift; and the Apostles used it not only regarding future ministers of the church, but also regarding other believers (Acts 8:17), and even among the sick, so that they might recover (Acts 28:8). However, the successors of the Apostles today no longer enjoy such a miraculous gift. 

Wherefore no reason can be shown why **Ordination** must necessarily be performed by **Theologians**; rather, it ought to be performed by **Politicians** <note>civil magistrates or state officials</note>, because it is carried out in the name of the **Prince**, and because the example of **Moses** is read in Scripture <note>referring to Moses, a civil leader, consecrating Aaron and the priests</note>. In the meantime, it ought not to be disapproved that ordination is usually entrusted to Theologians, for since the Prince himself is able to ordain the chosen person, he also has the freedom of choosing to whom he wishes to give that task. Therefore, **Prelates** or other Theologians thus far perform the functions of the Prince; for they can at least teach in the place of Christ <note>original: *vice Christi*</note>, but they have nothing further in their mandates from Christ <note>meaning their legal authority to appoint others comes from the state, not a direct divine mandate</note>. 

The laying on of hands, however, is an **indifferent matter** <term>Adiaphora</term> <note>original: *res indifferens*; a theological term for actions that are neither commanded nor forbidden by God</note>, just like the whole of Ordination; it is neither commanded nor prohibited by God, and thus it is certainly permissible to employ it during Ordination.

<summary>This page argues that the legal right to ordain ministers belongs to the sovereign (the Prince) rather than exclusively to the clergy. It explains that the Apostles' role in ordination was a result of the early church's lack of state support and that the "laying on of hands" is now a symbolic tradition rather than a miraculous transfer of power.</summary>

<keywords>Ordination, Laying on of hands, Jus sacrorum, Sovereignty, Holy Spirit, Moses, Adiaphora, Civil Authority</keywords>

# ECCLESIASTICAL MATTERS

<meta>catchword: §. XIV.</meta>

§. XIII. Furthermore, the **Sovereign** <note>original: *Princeps*, referring to the supreme civil authority</note> holds command over the ministers of the church who have already been appointed; for "every soul must be subject to the higher powers," <note>referring to Romans 13:1</note> and it follows, therefore, that even **Theologians** <note>the clergy or church doctors</note> must be subject to him. This is true not only insofar as they are citizens and members of the **state** <term>respublica</term>, but also in their capacity as Theologians, and in view of their office of teaching or ministry. For although their office was prescribed by **Christ** himself—consisting in the preaching of the word and the administration of the **Sacraments** and the keys <note>the "power of the keys" refers to the authority to admit or exclude people from the church community</note>—and thus the Sovereign can by no means abolish these parts of the ecclesiastical ministry; nevertheless, because he is the minister of God for all that is good, he must preserve true religion and punish all evil. Since many evils are committed regarding religion, which are even more serious than others because they violate religion itself, it is clearly evident that command over the ministers of the church and their office cannot be denied to him (Romans 13:1, 2, 4).

This very point can be understood by the example of marital power, which the Sovereign cannot abolish because it is from God. Nevertheless, the supreme inspection and command of the Sovereign also pertains to marriage, lest anyone abuse that power. In the same way, the components of the ministry are from Christ and cannot be abolished by the Sovereign. Yet it is the Sovereign's duty to ensure that these tasks are performed correctly by the ministers of the church, because he is the **Vicar of God** <term>Vicarius Dei</term>, the **Governor** <term>Rector</term> of the entire state, the minister of God for good—excluding no one—and the avenger of evil—likewise excluding no one.

<summary>This section argues that the Sovereign holds supreme oversight over the clergy, asserting that while the spiritual duties of ministers are divinely ordained, the civil ruler must ensure they are carried out without abuse or harm to the state.</summary>
<keywords>Sovereignty, Ecclesiastical Ministry, Church and State, Romans 13, Vicar of God, Oversight, Clergy</keywords>

### CHAPTER VIII. ON MINISTRY

<meta>The catchword "ribus" from the previous page likely completes the word "superioribus" (superiors), referring to the Sovereign's inspection of marital and ecclesiastical power.</meta>

§. XIV. Therefore, the **Sovereign** <note>original: *Princeps*, the supreme civil ruler</note> ought to take care that the ministers of the church perform their office according to the command of **Christ**. They must ensure that the ministers teach the word of God uncorrupted and pure, and that—in refuting the errors of **heterodox thinkers** <note>original: *heterodoxorum*, those holding different or "heretical" beliefs</note> and in correcting the conduct of the ungodly—they omit all insults, abuse, and private passions. Such behaviors are an obstacle to men casting off false doctrine and ungodly habits; instead, they are driven even further to persist in their errors and impiety. Similarly, the Sovereign must take care that the ministers of the church do not teach anything that is contrary to the welfare of the **state** <term>respublica</term>. For under the title of religion and by the authority which they themselves usually wield, the common people are easily captured and led to oppose the supreme power, call its rights into question, and bring the state into danger.

§. XV. The matter is much the same regarding the administration of the **Sacraments** and the use of the **keys** <note>the "power of the keys" refers to the authority to admit or exclude people from the church through confession and excommunication</note>. For the Sovereign must take care that baptism is administered as Christ instituted it, and that the **Sacrament of the Supper** <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion</note> is not **mutilated** <note>original: *mutiletur*; likely a reference to the contemporary theological debate over "communion in one kind," where the cup was withheld from the laity</note> by the ministers of the church. He must ensure that the remission of sins and the offering of the Sacraments are not unjustly denied to anyone. This is because, in this way, the soul of an innocent subordinate can be driven to despair and destroyed; or, where this specific danger is not present, it is nonetheless unfair and an abuse of the keys. If any ministers sin in these matters—as both **Scripture** and experience testify—or even become a scandal to their listeners by their ungodly conduct...

<note>original vocabulary: Sovereign, ministers of the church, word of God, heterodox thinkers, the state, Sacraments, baptism, the Supper, abuse of the keys</note>

<summary>This page argues that the civil Sovereign has the right and duty to oversee the conduct and teaching of church ministers. The Sovereign must prevent clergy from using their influence to incite rebellion and ensure that Sacraments like baptism and communion are administered fairly and according to Christ's original intent.</summary>

If, furthermore, they are the Sovereign's **Deputies** <note>original: *Vicarii*</note>—either because they manage the property of the church or take part in **Consistories** <term>Consistories</term><note>Administrative and legal councils that managed church affairs, often composed of both clergy and lay lawyers</note>—it is certain that Sovereigns have authority over them in those roles.
