§. XVI. If the Sovereign exercises this authority and office of his in sacred matters incorrectly, and orders the ministers of the church to teach false things, or to offer the **Holy Supper** <note>original: *sacram cœnam*, the Eucharist or Communion</note> to a person who stubbornly persists in their wickedness, then they certainly ought not to obey his will. For it is a general rule that subjects should not obey the Sovereign if he orders something to be done, whether in sacred or secular matters, which they themselves cannot perform without sinning; for **one must obey God rather than men** <note>a reference to the biblical principle in Acts 5:29</note>. §. XVII. It is clear, therefore, that the authority of the Sovereign pertains to the very substance of the actions prescribed by Christ to the **Teachers** and ministers of the church. But the authority of the Sovereign also extends even further regarding those matters, as he determines those things which have not been defined by Christ, or which surround them as circumstances, and prescribes certain rites so that all things may be done in order and with decency. Hence, he prescribes at what time of the **Lord's Day** <note>original: *diei dominicæ*, Sunday</note> public worship of God ought to begin; on what other days public worship should be celebrated; which words from **Scripture** are to be explained on the Lord’s Day; how many sermons should be held; and at what hour and in what place in the church they should occur, and how long they should last. He has the same right regarding the **Sacraments**: which prayers are to be used (in addition to the **words of institution** <term>Words of Institution</term><note>The specific biblical words used to consecrate the bread and wine in the Eucharist or the water in Baptism</note> of each Sacrament), what rites and ceremonies should be applied, and in what part of the church they should be administered. He possesses the same right in all the rest of the public worship of God. And in these matters, the ministers of the church can neglect or change nothing without sacrilege and a violation of the Sovereign’s authority over sacred things, since all these things are commanded by the Sovereign in the place of God for the good order, decorum, and edification of the church, **Romans 13:1, 2**.

-># ON THE CHURCH #<-
-><note>original: *ECCLESIASTICO*</note><-

...those appointed <note>original: *[con]stitutorum*, completing the word from the previous page</note> he ought not to seize; for when some of the people of **Israel** did this, the earth swallowed them up <note>**Numbers 16:32**: The account of Korah, Dathan, and Abiram who challenged the religious leadership of Moses and Aaron</note>, and even **King Uzziah** himself was made a leper for that very reason, **2 Chronicles 26:16** <note>King Uzziah was punished for attempting to burn incense in the Temple, a duty reserved for priests</note>. Therefore, the **command over sacred matters** <note>original: *imperium sacrorum*</note> belongs to the **Sovereign**, but the **ministry of sacred matters** <note>original: *ministerium sacrorum*</note> belongs to the ministers of the church.

By natural law, a Sovereign could indeed perform the office of a **Priest**; for since he is able to grant that duty to others, he could also retain it for himself. This is much like a **Head of the Household** <term>Paterfamilias</term><note>The male head of a family who, in ancient or patriarchal societies, held absolute authority over his domestic domain</note> who, living outside of a formal commonwealth, is not only the **King** of his family but also its **Priest**, as long as he has not appointed another to the role. The **Holy Scripture** <note>original: *sacer Codex*</note> also teaches that **Melchizedek** was both King and Priest at the same time <note>**Genesis 14:18**</note>.

But this is no longer permitted according to the will of **God** revealed through **Christ**, as seen in **Matthew 20:25**, **Mark 10:42**, and **Luke 22:25**. Nor can it be argued as an objection that the command belongs to the Sovereign in his capacity as Sovereign, while the ecclesiastical office belongs to him in his capacity as **Pastor**—and thus he does not "rule" while acting as a Pastor. **Paul** teaches in **2 Timothy 2:4** that this was not the mind of Christ: *“No man that warreth entangleth himself with the affairs of this life,”* <note>The author uses this verse to argue that secular governance and the spiritual ministry are distinct callings that should not be mixed</note> and he says this to **Timothy** regarding the proper administration of his office.

Furthermore, it is impossible—due to the other business of a Sovereign and the daily pleasures he usually enjoys (which are certainly permitted if moderation is observed)—for him to rightly perform the duties of a minister of the church. **Splendor** <note>original: *Pompa*</note>, which is likewise permitted and even necessary for a ruler, does not suit the **Priestly office**, since Christ, without external splendor, entered into the wor- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; original: *mun-* for *mundum* (world)</note>

It concludes that the demands and worldly splendor of civil leadership are incompatible with the humble spiritual duties of the ministry established by Christ.</summary>

### Chapter VIII: On the Ministry of the Church ###

...he has been involved in the world: moreover, a change of clothing <note>original: *habitus*; referring to the clerical garb or the transition from secular to sacred dress</note> would always be necessary and would be somewhat unseemly.

**§. XIX.** It is also the **duty of the Sovereign** to provide for a **Salary** <term>Salarium</term> for the ministers of the church. This is owed to them by both **natural law** and **divine revelation**, so that they may support themselves and their families; for "every laborer is worthy of his hire" (**Luke 10:7**) and "he who serves at the Altar ought to live from the Altar" (**1 Corinthians 10:13**). This provision is also necessary so they can better devote their time to the **reading and meditation of the divine word**, spiritual tasks which would be hindered if they were forced to perform manual labor (**2 Chronicles 31:4**).

It is therefore the Sovereign's responsibility to command that this salary be paid from the church's assets; or, if the church lacks funds, the **listeners** <note>the congregation or "auditors"</note> should contribute from their own resources. However, if a very small congregation—perhaps one far removed from its neighbors—has its own Pastor but cannot afford to support him, nothing prevents that Pastor from working with his hands to sustain himself and his family. The **Apostle Paul** did exactly this (**Acts 18:3**) <note>Paul was a tentmaker by trade, famously working so as not to be a burden to the churches</note>. Such a Pastor would have fewer administrative duties in a small congregation, allowing him to still find time during the week for meditation on the Word of God. Furthermore, it is not entirely **impossible** <term>impossible</term> <note>original Greek: *ἀδύνατον*</note> to meditate on the Word while working with one's hands, just as craftsmen and other workers are able to pray and sing hymns during their labors. But where such dire necessity is absent, it remains the rule that ministers of the church are entitled to a salary by right, as was just stated.

<summary>This page outlines the Sovereign's responsibility to ensure church ministers receive a salary, justifying it through scripture and the need for ministers to focus on spiritual meditation. It also notes that while manual labor is acceptable for pastors in poor or isolated congregations, it is not the ideal standard.</summary>

<keywords>Sovereign's duty, church salary, natural law, manual labor, Apostle Paul, meditation on the Word, congregation support</keywords>

Furthermore, **Immunities** and other **Privileges** <note>original: *Immunitates autem aliaque Privilegia*</note> do not belong to them by divine right; for Christ himself, along with Peter, paid the tax (**Matthew 17:24-25**, **Romans 13:1, 7**). Instead, these are granted through the grace of the **Sovereign** <term>Princeps</term> (**Genesis 47:22**, **Ezra 7:24**).

### CHAPTER IX.
### On Ecclesiastical Judgments.

->§. I.<-

It is sufficiently clear from what has been said previously that authority over religion itself, other sacred matters, and the ministers who attend to them, belongs to the Sovereign. Just as in secular affairs—because he is not able to handle everything by himself—he rightly employs his ministers to administer justice in his stead; so too in sacred matters, he rightly utilizes the labor of his ministers and establishes **ecclesiastical courts** <note>original: *judicia ecclesiastica*</note>. In these courts, ecclesiastical cases are to be judged according to his own laws. He alone possesses the power to establish these laws, because this power is among the **greater regalia** <term>regalia majora</term> <note>the fundamental rights and powers reserved exclusively for a sovereign ruler, such as the power to make laws or declare war</note> upon which the safety of the state depends. These powers are of such a nature that they cannot be transferred to subjects, not even in regards to how they are exercised.

§. II. Moreover, it is left to the Sovereign's will whether he wishes to assign specific courts to ecclesiastical matters—just as **feudal cases** <term>causæ feudales</term> and other specific matters are typically handled in distinct courts—or whether he wishes to assign ecclesiastical cases to the same courts where civil business is judged.

**Vocabulary:** Immunities, Privileges, Sovereign, Ecclesiastical Judgments, Greater Regalia, Feudal Cases

<summary>This page argues that clerical privileges are granted by the state rather than divine right and establishes the Sovereign as the ultimate legal authority over ecclesiastical courts.</summary>
<keywords>ecclesiastical courts, sovereign authority, regalia majora, clerical immunity, church law, judicial system</keywords>

### CHAPTER IX. ON JUDGMENTS

For ecclesiastical courts are merely secular, inasmuch as they must be established by the **Sovereign** <term>Princeps</term> and exercised in his name. This is because he is the Sovereign; for the title of "bishop" <note>original: *episcopi* — the author is likely critiquing the idea that a ruler acts as a "chief bishop" (Summepiskopat) rather than by his own inherent civil authority</note> is incorrectly applied to him in this context. Furthermore, in the **Old Testament**, judgments concerning ecclesiastical and secular <note>original: *profanis*</note> cases were not distinct, as can be understood from **Deuteronomy 17:8-9** and **2 Chronicles 19:8, 10**.

->§. III.<-

These courts are secular even if theologians or church ministers participate in them; for in doing so, they act in the place of the Sovereign. They are to be considered not as theologians or church ministers, but should be numbered among the **politicians**, ministers, advisors, or judges of the Sovereign. By his command and in his place, they pronounce the law in ecclesiastical cases according to his own laws. They hold that part of the **right over sacred matters** <term>jus sacrorum</term> <note>the legal authority of a state to regulate the external affairs of the church</note> which pertains to its exercise from the Sovereign, in whom the right itself remains. This should certainly not be disapproved of, for the Sovereign has the free choice to decide whose assistance he wishes to use in his courts. 

Nevertheless, it is by no means necessary for theologians to be present in the Sovereign’s courts. For while it is certain that the priests of the **Old Testament** rendered judgment alongside other judges chosen from the people—not only in sacred cases but also in secular ones (**Deuteronomy 17:8-9**)—this belonged to the divine civil law <note>original: *juris divini forensis*</note> which was unique to the Jews and does not carry an obligation in the **New Testament**. Nor does any other command exist in the New Testament requiring church ministers to take part in these judgments, but rather...

<summary>This page argues that ecclesiastical courts are secular institutions derived from the Sovereign's authority, even when clergy participate. It distinguishes between the specific laws of the Old Testament and the freedoms granted under the New Testament.</summary>
<keywords>ecclesiastical courts, Sovereign authority, jus sacrorum, Old Testament law, New Testament, legal jurisdiction, theologians in court</keywords>

...are only commanded to teach. **Christ** also, whose messengers they are, set forth the laws of **marriage** and other divine laws only by explaining, vindicating, and teaching them; He exercised no **jurisdiction**, but on the contrary, rejected it. And whatever falls under the name of law, whether natural or revealed in **Scripture**, as well as what is just and unjust regarding all matters both divine and human, all these things belong to legislative jurisprudence, and also to the deliberative jurisprudence of the **Sovereign’s** ministers, whom he calls into counsel and whose opinions he requires. No less does it belong to the judicial jurisprudence of the judges to whom the **Sovereign** has granted jurisdiction in secular <note>original: *profanis* — literally "profane" or non-sacred matters</note> or sacred cases. Therefore, **Theologians** and ministers of the church are not necessarily part of ecclesiastical judgments by divine right, but only if the **Sovereign** commands it. Then, indeed, just as they teach publicly in the place of Christ, they exercise judgment in the place of the **Sovereign** as political and secular men and legal experts.

->§. IV.<-

It was demonstrated above that the ministry cannot be called a "representative church," and on that basis, they cannot claim for themselves the command and power of the **Sovereign** over religion itself—namely, the power to prescribe public doctrine and impose silence on false teachers. Now a new kind of representation must be considered, for some contend that **Consistories** <term>Consistoria</term> <note>administrative or judicial councils within certain Christian denominations, often handling matters of church discipline and law</note> represent the **Presbytery** <term>Presbyterium</term> <note>a body of elders or ministers who traditionally oversaw the affairs of a local church or district</note> of the church. ### CHAPTER IX. ON JUDGMENTS

72

...is easily understood. For indeed, in **Consistories** <term>Consistoria</term> <note>administrative and judicial councils in the church</note> sacred jurisdiction is exercised according to ecclesiastical laws; however, the **Presbytery** <term>Presbyterium</term> <note>the body of elders or ministers</note> does not possess jurisdiction, but must only teach and perform its ministry according to the institution of **Christ**. On the contrary, the **Sovereign** <term>Princeps</term> <note>referring to the supreme civil ruler</note> possesses all command and jurisdiction over sacred matters. Therefore, **Consistories** represent the **Sovereign**, and not the **Presbytery**. Indeed, the **Presbytery** itself is subject to the **Consistory**, and even this arrangement exists by the grace of the **Sovereign**; for otherwise it is not necessary by divine law for a special court to be established for them, but they ought to appear and answer in other courts just as the **Apostles** did (*Acts 24:10*). Nor can it be proved from *Matthew 18:17* <note>original: *Matth. XIIX. 17.* — "If he refuses to listen to them, tell it to the church."</note> that jurisdiction belongs to the ministry by divine law; for that saying must either be explained as referring to the whole **church**, or to those to whom the **church** has entrusted the administration of its affairs. If the former, it is not proven, for then the affairs of the **church** are ordained by common consent; if the latter, it is likewise not proven, for the **church** can employ other elders or wiser men besides the ministers of the **church**.

->§. V.<-

Others say that **Consistories** possess **Episcopal right** <term>jus Episcopale</term>, but they do so falsely. For indeed, episcopal right—excepting those parts of the ministry which are also referred to it—is a fictitious right, not founded on divine law. Instead, it encompasses certain rights in sacred matters which **Bishops** have snatched away from those in command through fraud and force, which...

It challenges the "episcopal right" of bishops as a historical overreach rather than a scriptural necessity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal argument that ecclesiastical jurisdiction is not an inherent divine right of the clergy but a delegated power from the civil Sovereign. It specifically addresses the transition of "episcopal rights" to Protestant rulers after the Reformation.</meta>

# ON ECCLESIASTICAL MATTERS

73

For this reason, it <note>referring to "episcopal right" mentioned on the previous page</note> cannot even be defined, but only so much right is attributed to it as the bishops have taken away from the **Sovereigns** <term>Principes</term> <note>the supreme civil rulers</note>. Furthermore, this whole right ceases in the lands of **Evangelical** <term>Evangelicorum</term> <note>Protestant</note> **Sovereigns**, since all **right over sacred matters** <term>jus sacrorum</term> has been restored to them, which belongs to them by divine, natural, and revealed law. Therefore, because the **Sovereign** possesses command over sacred matters by his own right—and insofar as he is the **Sovereign**—he can also exercise it in whatever manner pleases him. Nor is it necessary for him to establish **Consistories** <term>Consistoria</term> <note>administrative and judicial councils</note>; far from it that **Consistories** possess **episcopal right** <term>jus episcopale</term> <note>the legal authority traditionally held by a Bishop</note> of their own.

->§. VI.<-

Because **ecclesiastical judgments** <term>judicia ecclesiastica</term> are exercised by the command and in the place of the **Sovereign**, it also follows that ecclesiastical judges have only as much right as the **Sovereign** has granted them. Indeed, they can take no further right for themselves unless they wish to sin against the **Sovereign** and his **royal rights** <term>jura regalia</term> <note>the exclusive prerogatives of a monarch</note>. Moreover, the **Sovereign** can even grant them the power of decreeing capital punishment, even though **Theologians** or **Pastors** sit as their advisors. For even the **Priests of the Old Testament** <term>Sacerdotes Veteris Testamenti</term> exercised judgments of blood by the mandate of God (*Deuteronomy 17:8*). And, as was said before, the **Theologians** who serve as **Assessors** <term>Assessores</term> <note>legal assistants or advisors to a judge</note> of ecclesiastical judgments are to be considered in this capacity as **secular men** <note>original: *Viri Politici*; meaning they act as civil officials rather than spiritual ministers in this context</note>; and thus this role does not prevent them from also being **Teachers** or ministers of the church. The **Papists** <term>Pontificii</term> <note>Roman Catholics</note> indeed state otherwise in this matter, yet they do not hesitate [to combine] the **means of grace** <term>media gratiæ</term>, the word of God, and the **Sacrament**...

<meta>catchword: of the supper</meta>

<summary>This page argues that Protestant rulers have reclaimed "episcopal rights" from the Catholic Church, meaning that ecclesiastical courts (Consistories) operate solely under the Sovereign's authority, even when dealing with capital punishment or involving clergy in legal roles.</summary>

### Chapter IX. On Ecclesiastical Judgments.

74

...to snatch the Supper <note>referring to the Lord's Supper or Holy Communion</note> away from men, and to slaughter their souls, Matthew 23:24. <note>original: "cœnæ hominibus eripere, animasque eorum trucidare." The author likely references the Pharisees here to criticize clergy who misuse the power of excommunication.</note>

->§. VII.<-

Just as the **Sovereign** <term>Princeps</term> <note>the supreme civil ruler</note>, as the punisher of all evil, holds supreme oversight and command over all subjects, so that each may perform his duty correctly, or, if he has sinned, suffer the owed penalties; so he must care much more that judgments concerning sacred matters are administered correctly, 2 Chronicles 19:6–7. Therefore, the **Sovereign** can—which some wrongly deny—take cases away from the **Consistories** <term>Consistoria</term> <note>administrative and judicial councils of the church</note> and decide what is just himself; likewise, subjects can appeal from these courts to the **Sovereign** for a just cause. It follows that the **Consistories** are established by the **Sovereign** and are carried out in his name; consequently, they are subject to him as their superior, so much so that he can abolish them entirely and entrust the adjudication of ecclesiastical matters to other judges.

->§. IIX.<-

This is also certain—despite some fruitlessly disagreeing—that judges of ecclesiastical matters have no jurisdiction over the **Sovereign** himself, nor is the **Sovereign** subject to them; on the contrary, they hold all their authority <term>jus</term> from the **Sovereign** and are subject to him. The **Sovereign**, however, is subject only to **God**, by whose universal, natural, and moral laws (specifically so-called), universal positive laws, and the precepts of the Christian religion he must live; to **God** alone he shall render an account, and by **God** alone, if he sins, can he be punished.

<summary>This page argues that the Sovereign holds supreme authority over church courts (Consistories), including the power to hear appeals and abolish such courts. It concludes that while the Sovereign oversees all subjects, he is personally accountable to no earthly authority, only to God.</summary>

### CHAPTER X.
### The Church Does Not Hold Sovereignty Over Sacred Matters.

-> §. I. <-

Since the **Evangelical Sovereigns** <term>Principes Evangelici</term> <note>The Protestant rulers who assumed legal authority over church administration following the Reformation</note> received the command over sacred things—which I have explained up to this point—through public treaties, a "new Anti-Christ" has emerged who has attempted to snatch that power away from the Sovereigns. This figure has been introduced by those who attribute sovereignty over sacred matters either to the church (beyond the private care of salvation) or to the ministers of the church (beyond the actual ministry of sacred rites). These people even go so far as to label as "Anti-Christ" those who defend the command over sacred matters rightfully owed to the Sovereigns. However, it is manifest from what I have said above, and will become even more clear now, that this is a great injustice.

-> §. II. <-

We read indeed in 1 Chronicles 14:1 <note>original: "I. Chron. XIV. 1." In modern Bibles, the account of David consulting the leaders about the Ark is found in 1 Chronicles 13:1. The author likely uses a different chapter numbering common in older Latin editions.</note> that **David** took counsel with the leaders and asked the people whether it pleased them to bring back the Ark of God; yet these actions pertain to the *method* of exercising power, which remains at the discretion of the **Sovereign**. Indeed, in other places, we do not read that David or other Kings ordained religion and the worship of God by the counsel and consent of the church. Instead, they exercised the command over sacred matters by their own right alone, as **Vicars of God** <term>Vicarii Dei</term> <note>Earthly representatives or deputies acting on God's behalf</note>. Furthermore, only those Kings who reformed and preserved religion are praised by God—not the church or the subjects. Conversely, only the Kings are rebuked by God,

# CHAPTER X.
### The Church Does Not Hold Sovereignty Over Sacred Matters.

...from God, those who did not remove the worship of idols, but not the church or the subjects. Therefore, the church does not hold **sovereignty over sacred matters** <term>imperium sacrorum</term> <note>The legal right to govern religious institutions and public worship</note>, but the **Sovereign** <term>Princeps</term> alone does, as seen in Exodus 32:19, 20, 21 <note>original: "Exod. XXXII. 19. 20. 21." This refers to Moses, acting as the civil leader, destroying the Golden Calf and holding Aaron accountable.</note>.

-> §. III. <-

Furthermore, regarding those things read in the **Old Testament**, such as Isaiah 2:23 <note>This likely refers to Isaiah 49:23, which describes kings as "nursing fathers" to the faithful</note>, 60:10, and 60:12—namely, that it shall come to pass that Sovereigns, falling on their faces, shall serve the church—these passages are so far from granting sovereignty to the church that they instead openly confirm the Sovereign’s authority over sacred matters. For this is taught by those words: that it will happen that Kings will embrace the true religion, that they will "kiss Christ with the kiss of subjection" <note>A reference to Psalm 2:12, interpreted here as the political submission of a ruler to the kingship of Christ</note>, and serve Him with all zeal. They do this specifically in their capacity *as Kings*, ensuring that the "milk of the Gospel" is provided to their subjects and commanding them to expect eternal salvation from Christ alone. This is how the **Holy Spirit**, the best interpreter of His own words, explains the saying in Psalm 72:9, 10, 11, where things are said of **Christ** that Isaiah said of the church, because Christ alone is worshipped and adored within the true church.

-> §. IV. <-

In the **New Testament** as well, sovereignty over sacred matters was not granted to the church. For when Christ advises in Matthew 18:15, 16, 17, that one should, in the case of a stubborn sinner, finally "tell it to the church," or to its Elders or ministers to whom the church has entrusted its affairs, it is easily apparent that the saying pertains to a church that is self-governing <note>original: "sui juris," meaning a body that possesses its own legal standing</note>, or at least one neglected or plainly oppressed by the ruling power. But if indeed the church is under a Sovereign...

Therefore, if the Sovereign does not abandon his right—which he certainly cannot do without sinning—the church can in no way claim the care of sacred matters for itself.

<margin>1 Corinthians 6:2, 4, 7, 8.</margin>
§. V. Nor did the Apostle Paul intend to exempt the church from the sovereignty of Sovereigns and grant sovereignty over sacred matters to the church itself; rather, he speaks of **arbitrators** <term>arbitris</term>, to whom litigants committed a dispute to be decided by mutual consent. He said these should be chosen from the members of the church itself rather than from the pagans, because it depended on their own will whom they wished to choose as judges. Furthermore, because it was a matter of poor example for them to have disputes, he acts primarily to prevent one person from doing injury to another and providing cause for litigation, lest they be a scandal to the pagans, and the name of God and His doctrine be blasphemed. But that the church should not be subject to the ruler, but should itself hold sovereignty over sacred matters—this the Apostle does not intend at all. On the contrary, he teaches that **every soul must be subject to the higher powers** <note>original: "omnem animam potestati eminenti subjectam esse debere," a reference to Romans 13:1</note>, and he showed this by his own example, when even in religious cases he answered his accusers before the Emperor and judges—even pagan ones. <note>Acts 24:10</note>

# CHAPTER 10. THE CHURCH DOES NOT POSSESS [SOVEREIGNTY]

->**CHAPTER 10. THE CHURCH DOES NOT POSSESS [SOVEREIGNTY]**<-

§. VI. It is also not proven from the practice of the **Apostolic church** <term>ecclesia Apostolica</term> <note>The church as it existed during the lifetime of the Apostles</note> that sovereignty over sacred matters belongs to the church. To be sure, the **Holy Spirit** <term>Spiritus Sanctus</term> commanded certain things, such as when He designated **Matthias** by lot <note>Referencing the selection of a replacement for Judas Iscariot in Acts 1:26</note>. To this also belong those things which the Apostles ordained by the instinct of the Holy Spirit, or when they performed certain acts by divine power. Among these acts is the delivering of the wicked into the power of **Satan** for the purpose of inflicting bodily pain <note>original: "traditio facinorosorum in potestatem Satanae ad dolores corpori infligendos"; a reference to 1 Corinthians 5:5, often interpreted as a form of miraculous apostolic discipline</note>. Other matters the church either entrusted to and approved of the Apostles as they were established by them, or approved what the Apostles substituted, advised, and urged; or finally, the church itself, of its own accord and by the will of all its members, dispatched the business at hand. Therefore, the Apostolic church exercised no sovereignty <term>imperium</term>, but rather settled its affairs by common consent. The reason for this was that the **pagan Emperors** <term>Imperatores gentiles</term> did not know the truth, or certainly condemned it; hence, the church could not help but administer and arrange its affairs by the will of everyone, because those in power took no care for it, but rather neglected or even openly persecuted it. If, therefore, the **Sovereign** <term>Princeps</term> <note>The civil ruler or head of state</note> exercises the government of the church himself, the church ought not to claim for itself power in sacred matters.

§. VII. Those who nonetheless attribute sovereignty over sacred matters to the church are accustomed to object that there are three **Estates or Orders** <term>Status sive Ordines</term> <note>In early modern political theology, these usually referred to the Clergy, the Magistrate (the nobility/ruler), and the People (the laity)</note> given in the church, and by consequence, all three Estates ought to consent regarding the affairs of the church. But the three estates—

# SOVEREIGNTY OVER SACRED MATTERS.

...[others] are not given in the church, but only **Teachers** <term>Doctores</term> and **Listeners** <term>Auditores</term> <note>In this context, "Teachers" refers to the clergy or those in the teaching office, while "Listeners" refers to the laity or congregation</note>. To the former belongs the ministry; the latter, however, hear and receive the Word and the Sacraments. No other division finds a place in the church. If a **Prince** <term>Princeps</term> <note>The secular ruler or sovereign</note> is in the church, he is then considered as a member of the church and a listener; if he is not present, the church nonetheless remains. For no one denies that in the times of the Apostles and those that followed, the church existed, even though the Emperors were not members of the church. Therefore, all who are in the church are either Teachers or Listeners, whatever their status might otherwise be in the **state** <term>respublica</term> <note>original: "republica"; referring to the civil commonwealth or political community</note>. 

And even if there were three **estates** <term>status</term> <note>The "three estates" theory usually divided society into the clergy, the nobility, and the commoners. In contemporary Lutheran political thought, these were often the Ecclesiastical, Political, and Domestic estates</note> in the church, the church would not for that reason possess sovereignty over sacred matters. For indeed, in a state there are three estates, yet the Prince holds the sovereignty, not the three estates; rather, the ecclesiastical and **domestic estates** <term>status oeconomicus</term> <note>original: "status oeconomicus"; this refers to the sphere of the household and private labor, not the modern financial economy</note> constitute the subjects. If these estates were to wish to take sovereignty for themselves when laws must be passed, or when those guilty of more serious crimes must be punished with the death penalty, the state and the power and majesty of the Prince could not remain safe and unharmed. 

Therefore, the church—or the subjects—cannot usurp sovereignty even over sacred matters against the will of the Prince without sacrilege, according to **Romans 13:1-2** <note>A biblical passage commanding every person to be subject to the governing authorities, as they are appointed by God</note>. But if the form of the state is democratic, then the subjects arrange the affairs of the church by the common consent of all members of the church or of the majority; and thus they do so without [coercive] sovereignty, but rather as colleagues, making agreements as equals. §. VIII. What touches all must be approved by all <note>This is a reference to the legal maxim *Quod omnes tangit ab omnibus approbari debet*, originally from Roman law but widely used in medieval and early modern constitutional and ecclesiastical law</note>: however, the church does not for that reason possess **sovereignty over sacred matters** <term>imperium sacrorum</term> <note>The supreme power or legal authority to govern religious affairs</note>. From this, however, no sovereignty is established.

He does this so that he may command his subjects to follow the true religion, and so that he may judge controversies in matters of faith based on **Holy Scripture** in order to prescribe public doctrine and impose silence upon **false Teachers**. This is necessary since all Teachers claim to teach the truth, and they have no authority over one another. # CHAPTER 10. THE CHURCH DOES NOT HAVE SOVEREIGNTY

The logic or consideration of the estates <note>The "estates" refers to the traditional social classes—clergy, nobility, and commoners—who often held distinct legal rights.</note> is also plainly absent here.

§. 8. **What touches all must be approved by all** <note>original: "Quod omnes tangit, ab omnibus debet probari," a famous principle of Roman and Canon law often used to argue for the necessity of consent in governance.</note>; however, the church does not hold the **sovereignty over sacred matters** <term>imperium sacrorum</term> for that reason. Subjects, teachers <term>Doctores</term>, and other Christians must care for what touches them, but the **Prince** <term>Princeps</term> <note>The sovereign ruler or civil magistrate.</note> must also care for what touches him. A subject is touched by the private care of his own salvation: that he may know the true religion from **Scripture** <term>Scriptura</term> and embrace it once known; that he may judge controversies in matters of **faith** <term>fides</term> from the word of God for his own private knowledge; that he may guard himself against false prophets and flee from an outside **shepherd** <term>Pastorem</term>; that he may refuse a **heterodox** <note>Someone holding beliefs that differ from the established or true doctrine.</note> person chosen by the Prince; that he may not consent to sin; and that he may bring the wanderer back to the [right] path—which is the duty of every Christian because he is a Christian—to remove himself from the ungodly (Matthew 7:15; John 10:5; 1 Corinthians 5:9; 1 Timothy 6:5; 2 Timothy 3:5; 2 Thessalonians 3:6, 14; Titus 3:10; James 5:19, 20). These things must be their care, and they should not obey a Prince who commands otherwise or a teacher who teaches otherwise. Rather, any force—if any force is inflicted by the Prince for this cause—must be endured, for it is not permitted to resist, neither in secular nor in sacred matters. Truly, no **sovereignty** <term>imperium</term> is established by this.

§. 9. It touches the **theologians** <term>Theologos</term> and **ministers** <term>ministros</term> of the church to teach according to the will of God revealed in His word, and to judge controversies in matters of faith from the word of God for the purpose of [public] knowledge...

# CHAPTER 10. THE CHURCH DOES NOT HAVE SOVEREIGNTY

->**THE CHURCH DOES NOT HAVE SOVEREIGNTY**<-

...[He should] retain supreme inspection, so that an orthodox person is elected without disturbances; that he may command the **Teachers** <term>Doctores</term> <note>Academic theologians or authorized church instructors.</note> to teach the uncorrupted word of God, and to thunder against vices while sparing names <note>original: "nominibus parcere"—a common instruction for preachers to condemn sins in general without publicly shaming specific individuals by name.</note>; that he may establish due penalties for all who do not hesitate to disseminate among others errors harmful to either religion or the state; that he may remove from office those who are not true Teachers, but ravenous wolves; that he may command that the **sacred Supper** <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> not be denied to anyone unjustly; that he may prescribe the external order and rites to be used in divine worship; that he may appoint judges for ecclesiastical matters; that he may have inspection over the goods of the church, so that they are spent correctly; and that he may defend religion from enemies, if any wage war for the sake of religion. For it is permitted even to defend a false religion [from attack], even though not even the true religion ought to be propagated by force of arms. 

All these things concern the **Prince** <term>Princeps</term> <note>The sovereign ruler or civil magistrate.</note>; therefore, he must take care of them, and not simply follow the Teachers and others who wish to lead him astray. Instead, he himself should judge from the word of God what is true doctrine and what things are useful for the church in the external worship of God. This **sovereignty over sacred matters** <term>imperium sacrorum</term> does not belong to the church, the Teachers, or other subjects, but only to the Prince. 

Therefore, the church or the subjects do not have sovereignty in sacred matters, just as they do not have sovereignty in secular matters when taxes are commanded, even though that matter affects everyone. From this it also appears that the Prince does not hold sovereignty over sacred matters as a **Vicar of the Church** <term>Vicarius ecclesiae</term> <note>An official acting as a representative or deputy of the church institution.</note>, just as he does not hold all his sovereign power as a deputy of his subjects, but as the **Vicar of God** <term>Vicarius Dei</term>.

<summary>This page defines the Prince's extensive duties in governing the church, including overseeing elections, doctrine, and finances. It concludes that the Prince derives his authority over the church directly from God, rather than from the consent of the church or its people.</summary>

This sets up the distinction between "internal" spiritual duties and "external" legal governance.</meta>

# CHAPTER 11.

## The Ministry Does Not Possess Sovereignty Over Sacred Matters.

->§. I.<-

That theologians specifically so-called, or the **Teachers** <term>Doctores</term> <note>Authorized instructors of church doctrine.</note> and ministers of the church, do not possess **sovereignty over sacred matters** <term>imperium sacrorum</term> <note>This refers to the supreme legal power to command or make laws regarding religion.</note> can be established from the very fact that they are members of the church—and the church itself does not possess such sovereignty. But this is even more apparent from the mandates they received from Christ; for they must teach and administer the **Sacraments** along with the **Keys** <note>The "Power of the Keys" refers to the authority to forgive or retain sins, based on Matthew 16:19.</note> according to Mark 16:15. To these three acts, all things that fall to their duty can be referred. All these things, however, consist in **service** <term>ministerio</term> <note>original: "ministerio"—the Latin word for "ministry" literally means "service" or "attendance," implying a lack of independent power.</note> and involve no sovereignty.

Nevertheless, from this has been fabricated a certain power and sacred jurisdiction: an internal and spiritual ecclesiastical power, known as the **power of order** <term>potestas ordinis</term> <note>The authority to perform religious rites like the Eucharist.</note> and the **power of jurisdiction** <term>potestas jurisdictionis</term> <note>The authority to govern or judge in spiritual matters.</note>. This ecclesiastical power is said to belong to "internal sacred matters," consisting in the preaching of the Word, the administration of the Sacraments, and the use of the Keys. However, it is contrasted with "external ecclesiastical power," which is said to belong to external sacred matters and to consist in the external government of the church. The former is said to belong to the ministry, so that no right over it belongs to the **Prince** <note>The sovereign ruler or civil magistrate.</note>; it is also called the "care of internal sacred things." The latter is left to the Prince.

§. II. It is easily understood, however, that no pow-

### CHAPTER 11.

## The Ministry Does Not Possess Sovereignty Over Sacred Matters.

This ecclesiastical power is said to pertain to **internal sacred matters** <term>sacra interna</term> and to consist in the preaching of the Word, the administration of the Sacraments, and the use of the Keys. The former is said to belong to the ministry, to such an extent that the Prince <note>Referring to the civil sovereign or ruler.</note> has no legal right in it; it is also called the **care of internal sacred matters** <term>cura sacrorum interiorum</term>. The latter is left to the Prince.

# CHAPTER 11. THE MINISTRY DOES NOT HAVE [SOVEREIGNTY]

**power**, no **sovereignty**, and no **jurisdiction** can be established from the aforementioned parts. Indeed, the **power of order** <term>potestas ordinis</term> <note>The authority to perform religious rites.</note> is nothing other than the preaching of the word itself and the offering of the **Sacraments**; whereas the **power of jurisdiction** <term>potestas jurisdictionis</term> <note>The authority to exercise spiritual judgment.</note> is the announcement of sins being forgiven or withheld. Now, it is one thing to teach and to announce the will of God, but quite another to exercise sovereignty and power. To be sure, those who teach are sometimes called "presidents" and "leaders" in the **Scriptures** (1 Timothy 5:17, 1 Thessalonians 5:12, Hebrews 13:7, 17), but these and similar titles imply nothing other than their duty of teaching. They preside, lead, and rule by praying, persuading, teaching, exhorting, rebuking, and consoling.

§. III. Furthermore, the ministers of the church do not exercise legal jurisdiction but perform a **service** <term>ministerium</term> when they forgive sins to the pious and the repentant in the place of Christ <note>original: "vice Christi"</note> and offer the **Sacrament of the Supper** <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>. Likewise, they do not exercise jurisdiction, but perform a service, when they do not forgive the sins of the openly unrepentant, but instead preach of God's wrath and withhold the offering of the Supper. This is just as individual Christians do not exercise legal jurisdiction when they reject a false Teacher or turn away from and avoid the wicked. Therefore, the entire function of those who teach consists in service; it encompasses no legal power.

§. IV. Indeed, the ministers of the church ought to teach the **Prince** <note>The "Prince" refers to the supreme secular ruler or magistrate.</note> himself, and admonish him—

§. VI. He has shown that **Princes**, and those sent by Him, must have care for sacred matters for two reasons: first, so that true religion may be preserved—for **Paul**, when accused concerning religion before **Gallio**, wished to plead his case—and second, so that the welfare of the state <term>salus reipublicæ</term>, as well as external quiet and peace, may not be disturbed under the pretext of religion. Indeed, it is attributed to **Gallio** as a fault that he did not prevent the injury described in Acts 18:13, 14, 15, 16, 17.

# SOVEREIGNTY OVER SACRED MATTERS.

->Lest it be disturbed by **Teachers** <note>original: *Doctoribus*; here referring to the clergy, theologians, or church authorities.</note> and others who abuse religion.<-

§. XI. It can be further proved that no power belongs to the **Teachers**, because they are the **Successors** of Christ and the Apostles. Christ, however, did not exercise sovereignty; He did not bring new [civil] laws, but rather explained and defended the law of God and announced the will of God. He did not seek a kingdom or any legal jurisdiction, but rejected them, and said He was sent by God to teach (Luke 4:43, 12:14). The Apostles also exercised no sovereignty or power. Therefore, the Teachers, as their successors, have no power, jurisdiction, or sovereignty by **divine right** <note>original: *jure divino*; authority granted directly by God.</note>. 

Furthermore, it is proved that they have no power by divine right because Christ attributed sovereignty and power to **Kings** and their **Nobles** and those sent by them, but denied that such things belong to the ministers of Christ (Matthew 20:25, Mark 10:42, Luke 22:25). Peter also taught that the **Elders** <note>original: *Presbyteros*; the leaders of the early church congregations.</note> ought not to lord it over the clergy or the assembly committed to them (1 Peter 5:3). And it is not only a <term>contrary pattern <note>original Greek: *ἀντίγραφον* (antigraphon); usually meaning a copy or transcript, but here used to mean a logic that runs counter to the established order.</note></term> but also contradictory and <term>contrary to political reason <note>original Greek: *ἀπολίτικον* (apolitikon); something that is illogical from the perspective of statecraft or governance.</note></term> for a Prince to have supreme power and yet not have sovereignty over sacred matters—that is, power over the church, over religion itself, over the Teachers as such, or over the various parts of the ministry. **Coercion** <note>original: *Coactio*; the legal power to compel obedience through force or punishment.</note> is also removed from the Teachers, for a **bishop** <note>original: *episcopus*; an overseer in the church.</note> must not be a violent man (1 Timothy 3:3, Titus 1:7).

It asserts that supreme power over all matters, including religion, must reside solely with the secular Prince.</summary>

# CHAPTER XII. THE PRINCE HOLDS SOVEREIGNTY

Finally, it is the utterance of truth itself: *no one can serve two masters*. Therefore, it is true that to the **Teachers** <note>original: *Doctoribus*; referring to the clergy, theologians, or church authorities.</note>, beyond the duty of teaching, nothing belongs by **divine right** <note>original: *jure divino*; authority granted directly by God.</note>; they possess no power. Instead, they must be subject to the authority of the **Prince** not only as citizens, but also in their capacity as Teachers. Indeed, all power both over religion itself and over other sacred matters belongs to the Prince by divine right.

### CHAPTER XII.

## The Prince possesses Sovereignty over Sacred Matters by virtue of being Prince.

->Section I.<-

After we have shown that sovereignty over sacred matters belongs not to the church, nor to the church's ministers, but to the **Prince**, it now follows to consider further by what reasoning or by what title he holds it. And indeed Moses and David, who by the testimony of the Old Testament exercised sovereignty over sacred matters, were not only **Kings** but also **Prophets**. Nevertheless, they were the supreme overseers of sacred matters not in their capacity as Prophets, but as Kings. For it was the role of the Prophets to announce to men what God had commanded, but <term>the authority to command <note>original: *τό imperare*; the author uses a Greek article with the Latin verb to emphasize the specific legal function of "ruling" or "commanding."</note></term> belongs to Kings. Furthermore, **Joshua**, who succeeded Moses, held sovereignty over sacred matters by the same right as he did, yet he was not a Prophet. Therefore, just as Joshua did, so also Moses presided over sacred matters as a **King**

<summary>This page argues that religious leaders have no independent power and must be subject to the Prince. It begins a historical-theological defense of state control over religion by arguing that biblical figures like Moses and David ruled over sacred matters through their royal authority, not their prophetic gifts.</summary>
<keywords>sovereignty, divine right, church and state, Moses, David, Joshua, Teachers, royal authority</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the authority to organize and govern religious life (sacred matters) is a royal or civil power rather than a purely prophetic or clerical one. # SACRED MATTERS, INSOFAR AS HE IS PRINCE.

The King or Leader of the Israelite people presided over these matters, as seen in Deuteronomy 33:5. Furthermore, one never reads that **David the Prophet**, but always **David the King**, organized divine worship; indeed, many Kings who are praised for their care of sacred matters were not Prophets. Hence, even those who were Kings and Priests simultaneously nevertheless reigned as Kings, not as Priests.

§. II. Meanwhile, it is clear from a reading of the **Old Testament** that the Kings of the Israelites did many things according to the command of the Prophets. Nor should they have done otherwise, since the word of the Prophets was the word of God himself, and every **Prince** <note>original: *Princeps*; used here in the general sense of a sovereign ruler or head of state.</note> is subject to God. For this reason, even today, a Prince must exercise his <term>sovereignty over sacred matters <note>original: *imperium sacrorum*; the legal right of the state to regulate the external affairs of the church.</note></term> according to the rule of the divine word. He must command nothing concerning matters of faith except what agrees with the word of God (Isaiah 8:20). Likewise, he should prescribe nothing regarding religious rites except by observing the law of the **Holy Spirit**, by the power of which all things in the church must be done "decently and in order" (1 Corinthians 14:40).

Therefore, this limitation does not stand in the way of the Prince's sovereignty in sacred matters, but on the contrary, confirms it remarkably well. For the mandates of God given through the Prophets regarding the worship of God were for the most part general; the Kings then determined all the specific details with regard to the specific persons and circumstances. Thus we read that **Samuel** foretold that a "faithful Priest" would be raised up by God, yet that person was not specifically named by God (1 Samuel 2:35). Afterward, however, **King Solomon** [deposed] **Abiathar** the High Priest, because Ado- <note>The text breaks off at the catchword "Ado-", referring to Adonijah. Solomon deposed Abiathar because the priest had supported Adonijah’s failed attempt to seize the throne.</note>

<meta>catchword: Ado-</meta>

<summary>This page argues that biblical kings like David and Solomon exercised authority over religious life in their capacity as civil rulers, not as prophets. It establishes that while a Prince must follow God's word, he retains the right to determine the specific "order and decency" of religious practice.</summary>

CHAPTER XII. THE PRINCE HAS SOVEREIGNTY
<meta>catchword: The King</meta>

In the end, it is the declaration of Truth itself that no one can serve two masters <note>original: *neminem posse duobus dominis servire*; a reference to Matthew 6:24.</note>. Therefore, it is true that nothing belongs to the **Teachers** <note>original: *Doctoribus*; here referring to the clergy or those authorized to teach doctrine.</note> by divine law except the duty of teaching; they possess no power of their own, but must be subject to the sovereignty of the **Prince** not only as citizens, but also in their capacity as Teachers. Indeed, all power over religion itself and other sacred matters belongs to the Prince by divine law.

## CHAPTER XII.

### The Prince holds Sovereignty over Sacred Matters by virtue of being Prince.

#### §. I.

After we have shown that sovereignty over sacred matters belongs neither to the church nor to the ministers of the church, but to the Prince, it now remains to be further considered by what reasoning or by what title he holds it. Both **Moses** and **David**, who exercised sovereignty over sacred matters according to the testimony of the Old Testament, were not only Kings but also Prophets; nevertheless, they were the supreme heads of sacred matters not as Prophets, but as **Kings**. Indeed, it was the role of the Prophets to announce to men what God had commanded, but <term>ruling <note>original: *imperare*; the exercise of legal command and state authority.</note></term> belongs to Kings. Furthermore, **Joshua**, who succeeded Moses, held sovereignty over sacred matters by the same right as his predecessor, yet he was not a Prophet; therefore, just like Joshua, so too did Moses [rule] over sacred matters as a King

### §. III.

He ought to read this Word until the end of his life, and he is also able to ask others (besides the official Doctors of Scripture) what they perceive from the Scripture, provided they have knowledge of it.

...for proposing and explaining this matter, and applying it correctly to scholarship, it is certainly not necessary [for the Prince to possess such specialized learning]. Just as such knowledge is not required for the private understanding of individual subjects, the **Prince's judgment** aims to define **public doctrine** by his own law. If, however, he seeks the opinions of **theologians** in matters of faith, nothing belongs to them—or to others summoned by the Prince to handle doctrine—except the **duty of teaching** <note>original: *τό docere*; the author uses the Greek article *to* with the Latin verb *docere* to signify "the act" or "function" of teaching.</note>. Rather, the Prince must examine everything according to the **Scriptures**, so that he may reject what is false and command that what is true be taught publicly. Just as he should not simply follow the advice of his ministers even in secular causes, he must instead test all things and choose the best path.

### §. IV.

In the same way, it is not necessary for the Prince to submit to the will of theologians when establishing **liturgical books** and other **ecclesiastical ordinances** <term>Ordinationibus ecclesiasticis <note>Official regulations and legal frameworks governing church structure, ceremonies, and worship.</note></term>, nor is he required to listen to their advice. Indeed, it would be an insult to say the Prince keeps advisors who are unable to provide him with what is just, useful, or conducive to piety and the building up of the church when he desires it. 

If he does choose to employ theologians—for it is his own choice whom he wishes to consult—their advice does not constitute laws that can bind the Prince. Instead, the Prince nevertheless retains **sovereignty over sacred matters** <term>imperium sacrorum <note>The supreme legal authority of a state over religious institutions and public worship.</note></term>...

# Chapter XII. The Prince holds Sovereign Authority

...retains. From this point, he himself can and must consider, to the ultimate degree, what is best to be done and what things are useful; he can then reject those things which are displeasing, and command those things which seem right.

### §. V.
Generally, regarding the whole of the **Prince’s** sovereign authority <term>imperium <note>In this context, *imperium* refers to the supreme legal power to command and enforce laws, traditionally held by a monarch or state.</note></term> in sacred matters, it must be observed that in its exercise he ought not to follow the opinions, counsels, and will of **theologians**, nor is it necessary that he admit them into his council. 

If, however, he has sought their advice, he must thereafter examine everything according to the **Scriptures**, and only then command what has seemed right to him. This same principle of law applies if he has sought the advice of political advisors; he can do this no less in the government of sacred things than in the government of secular things <note>original: *profanorum*; literally "profane" things, meaning non-religious or civil affairs.</note>, because theologians are commanded only to teach. <note>The author cites Jonah 3:7–9, where the King of Nineveh—a secular ruler—orders a fast and repentance for his people, illustrating that the ruler directs religious action.</note> 

Indeed, the credit for the casting out of idols from the people of Israel, and the restoration and preservation of true religion and true worship, is never given to the **High Priest** <note>original: *Pontifici summo*</note>, let alone the other priests, but to the **Kings** alone. Conversely, the blame for the retention of idolatrous worship is never attributed to the High Priest, much less to the inferior priests, but to the Kings alone. 

Thus, if the Prince alone must give an account to God for the government of sacred matters, it follows that the Teachers <note>original: *Doctores*; here referring to the "Doctors of the Church" or academic theological authorities.</note> can command nothing to him. Instead, he alone holds authority over religion itself and other sacred matters, and he must judge all things for himself—just as judgment must precede every action for it to be conducted rightly.

### §. VI.
Furthermore, the **Prince** does not exercise sovereign authority over sacred matters in the capacity of a **Bishop**. For "episcopal right" <term>jus episcopale <note>The legal authority and bundle of rights traditionally claimed by a bishop within the church hierarchy.</note></term> includes, first and foremost, those things which pertain to the ministry <term>ministerium <note>Referring to the "office of the word and sacraments"—the specific spiritual duties of preaching and administering rituals.</note></term>; however, it is not permitted for a Prince to be a Bishop or a Pastor. 

Even if it were permitted, he would still not hold authority over sacred matters in the capacity of a Pastor. If he did, it would follow that the Lord of the region would merely be a colleague to the Pastors. This would mean they would not only equal him in dignity but could also exercise authority over sacred things alongside him. This would be sacrilegious <note>original: *sacrilegum*</note> and contrary to the words of **Christ**, who said: *The kings of the world reign, and those who have authority over them are called benefactors: But you shall not be so.* <note>original: *Reges mundi regnant, & magnates eorum potestatem exercent: Vos autem non sic*; see Luke 22:25–26. The author uses this to argue for a strict separation between the nature of kingly rule and pastoral service.</note> Therefore, the Prince does not possess the right over sacred matters as a bishop.

### §. VII.
The remaining parts of episcopal right are referred to as **diocesan law** <term>lex diœcesana <note>The body of rules governing the administration, property, and discipline within a specific territory or diocese.</note></term>. This category includes the care of true religion, the right to hold Synods <note>original: *jus Synodorum*; formal assemblies of church leaders to decide on doctrine or administration.</note>, the right to appoint Parish Priests <note>original: *jus constituendi Parochos*</note>, the inspection of ecclesiastical persons and property, the right of church visitations, and other such duties. 

Furthermore, it includes **Jurisdiction**, which covers the "episcopal hearing" regarding church legal cases and parish priests, ecclesiastical punishments, the right to establish **Consistories** <term>Consistoria <note>Administrative and judicial councils, often composed of both lay and clerical members, that managed church affairs under the sovereign’s authority.</note></term>, and similar functions. Finally, it includes "dignity," which consists of titles and prerogatives held above others in the ecclesiastical order. 

Yet, to anyone looking closely at this "right," it is immediately clear that it is fictitious and wrongly seized, having been taken away from the supreme **Rulers** <note>original: *summis Imperantibus*</note>. Because episcopal right encompasses a large portion of the law of sacred things—which does not actually belong to the bishops, but to the Prin- <note>The text breaks off here at the catchword, continuing the thought on the following page that these administrative powers rightfully belong to the sovereign.</note>

<summary>This page argues that a sovereign's religious authority is not derived from the clerical office of a bishop, which would wrongly imply he is a mere "colleague" of the clergy. Instead, the author asserts that administrative powers (hiring priests, managing property, holding courts) traditionally claimed by bishops were actually stolen from the rightful civil rulers.</summary>

### CHAPTER XII. THE PRINCE HAS SOVEREIGNTY

...which is owed to the **Prince** by natural and revealed law, it cannot be said that he holds the **right over sacred matters** <term>jus sacrorum <note>The legal authority to regulate the external affairs of the church, including its property and public order, as opposed to the internal "power of the keys" (sacraments and preaching).</note></term> in the capacity of a **bishop**. Just as one who recovers his own property from a robber or a thief does not possess it as a robber or a thief, but as its true Lord and owner. And because the **Pope** also seized the right over sacred matters in bad faith and took it away from the **Princes** to whom it rightfully belonged, for this same reason the Prince cannot be said to hold sovereignty over sacred things in the capacity of a Pope.

### §. VIII.
Furthermore, although one of the **bishops** or **elders**—for by divine right they are the same <note>The author references Acts 20:17 and 28 to support the "Presbyterian" or "Low Church" view that in the New Testament, the terms 'bishop' (*episkopos*) and 'elder' (*presbyter*) referred to the same office.</note>—may have some degree of oversight over the others in the Prince’s stead and place (an oversight subordinate to this supreme oversight), and may even excel them in dignity, this is something that can also happen by the will of the church itself if it has been neglected or oppressed by its **Ruler** (as was the case in the **primitive church**). 

Moreover, although **elders** <term>presbyteri <note>Literally "priests" or "elders," referring here to the ordained clergy who assist in the administration of the church.</note></term> may participate in legal judgments by the command and in the place of the Prince, this is not a matter of divine law, but of human law. As I have said, it happens by the grace and will of the Prince, who nonetheless retains the right over sacred matters, while they merely hold the exercise of it in some part. Therefore, when the Prince takes back that exercise for himself, even then he cannot be said to exercise the right over sacred matters "as a bishop." It is just as if he were to take back secular **jurisdiction** <term>jurisdictio <note>The legal power to hear and determine a cause.</note></term> from some College or judge; he does not thereby take on their titles, but thereafter holds that jurisdiction by his own right.

It also asserts that any authority held by bishops or clergy is granted by the Prince's grace or human law, rather than by divine mandate.</summary>

### §. IX. 
Regarding **dignity** specifically, the matter is equally clear: thus far, the **Prince** must not be considered a **Bishop**. For the dignity of **Bishops** or **Pastors** arises from the fact that they are **ministers of Christ**. If they possess any greater dignity beyond this, it is entirely a matter of human law and is owed to the favor of the **Prince**. The **Prince**, however, possesses his majesty or dignity from the fact that he is the **Vicar of God** <note>original: "Vicarius Dei" — a title asserting that the ruler acts as God's direct representative in earthly governance.</note> in the rule of the commonwealth and, within that commonwealth, of the church. Therefore, he does not possess his dignity in the capacity of a **Bishop**.

### §. X. 
Finally, if **episcopal right** <term>jus episcopale <note>The legal authority traditionally held by a bishop to oversee a diocese.</note></term> is considered in a general sense, the **Prince** cannot be said to hold authority over sacred matters as a bishop. This is because the authority over sacred matters, which is owed by **divine law** <term>jus divinum <note>Law believed to derive directly from God or scripture, as opposed to man-made laws.</note></term>, was restored to the **Evangelical Princes** <note>original: "Principes Evangelici" — referring to Protestant rulers during and after the Reformation who broke away from Roman Catholic authority.</note> through public treaties; consequently, **episcopal** or **papal law** is no longer granted within their lands. For this same reason, the **Prince's** right in sacred matters is not to be measured by the regulations of **canon law** <term>juris canonici <note>The body of laws and regulations made by ecclesiastical authority (the Church) for the government of the Christian organization and its members.</note></term>.

### §. XI. 
Furthermore, the **Prince** does not hold authority over sacred matters as a **member of the church**, for the members of the church are either **Teachers** <term>Doctores <note>The clergy or theologians whose role is to instruct the faithful.</note></term> or **Hearers** <term>Auditores <note>The laity or congregation who receive instruction.</note></term>. It is the role of the former to teach and to administer the **Sacramenta** <term>Sacraments <note>Rituals such as Baptism and the Lord's Supper.</note></term> along with the **keys** <note>Referencing the "Power of the Keys" (Matthew 16:19), the authority to forgive or retain sins and to excommunicate.</note>. It is the role of the latter to hear sermons and to wait for the remission or retention of sins, as well as the offering or denial of the **Sacraments**. And these things are true even of the **Prince**, insofar as he is a member of the

§. XII. Furthermore, for the same reason, the **Prince** cannot be said to exercise authority over sacred matters <term>as a primary member <note>original: "tanquam membrum ecclesiae primarium"</note></term> of the church or as its "most noble part." Social nobility and other worldly prerogatives do not belong to the church.

Consequently, the doctrine of those who say that a **Prince** who follows a false religion—or is even a total pagan <note>original: "ethnicus"</note>—has no authority over sacred matters because he is not a member of the church, collapses at the same time. In truth, the **Prince** does not hold authority over sacred matters insofar as he is a member of the church, but insofar as he is the **Prince** and **Ruler** of the church, established above it just as he holds authority over all **Associations** <term>which exist in the commonwealth <note>original: "Collegia, quae sunt in republica." The author is using a legal argument: just as the King oversees a guild or a city council as a legal body, he oversees the church as a legal organization.</note></term>. Therefore, a **Prince** who is **Christian** possesses no more right in sacred matters than a **Prince** who is not **Christian**. On the contrary, a non-Christian **Prince** possesses just as much right in sacred matters as a **Christian Prince**, unless his right has been restricted by a specific treaty. ### CHAPTER XII. THE PRINCE HOLDS SUPREME AUTHORITY
<meta>catchword: bus</meta>

[is] <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...established above [the church]."</note>, if he neglects the <term>right over sacred matters <note>original: "jus sacrorum"</note></term> or does not exercise it correctly, he must render an account of that matter to God.

§. XIII. Therefore, the **Prince** holds <term>authority over sacred matters <note>original: "imperium sacrorum"</note></term> insofar as he is the **Prince**. For whenever the care of religion is enjoined upon Kings and Princes by God in the **Holy Scripture**, the title of "Kings" and "Princes" is everywhere added, a matter which was discussed above. Furthermore, when the **Scripture** records that the worship of idols was overthrown by the **Kings of Israel**, and true religion was restored by them, it likewise testifies that they did so as **Kings**. Indeed, it does not add any other title, such as "Prophet," "Bishop," "member of the church," or "most noble member of the church." Moreover, in the **New Testament**, we read that **Christ** and the **Apostles** stood in cases concerning religion before **Emperors**, **Kings**, **Governors**, **Proconsuls**, and **Judges** of the Gentiles who were not members of the church. Nor does the **Holy Spirit** say that they inquired into or judged religion as bishops; on the contrary, He teaches that they exercised this principal part of <term>authority <note>original: "imperii"</note></term> in sacred things because they were **Caesars** and **Kings**, or their deputies, **Governors** and **Judges** of the nations (*Acts 24:10; 25:11*).

§. XIV. **ON SACRED MATTERS, OF WHICH THE PRINCE IS CHIEF.**

...magistrates <note>This completes the word "magistratibus" and the sentence from the previous page: "...the Holy Spirit commands that every [soul be subject to higher] magistrates."</note>, and others who hold authority from the supreme power, it necessarily follows that all subjects—even in the capacity that they constitute the church, whether they be **Teachers** <note>original: "Doctores," referring to the clergy or those authorized to teach doctrine</note> or **Listeners** <note>original: "Auditores," the laity or congregation</note>—are subject to the command of the **Prince**, insofar as he is Caesar, King, or Prince. They are likewise subject to the Prince’s deputies, who exercise authority in the provinces or in legal judgments.

These same supreme powers and **Princes** are from God; they are "gods" <note>original: "Dii." A common 17th-century reference to Psalm 82:6, "I have said, Ye are gods," used to emphasize that rulers act as God’s representatives on earth.</note> and ministers of God for every good; they bear the sword and are the avengers of wrath against all who do evil. It follows, therefore, that the **Prince** has authority over religion and command over all members of the church. He is able to command **Teachers** to correctly perform their duty according to the prescription of the divine word, both regarding the office of teaching, the **use of the keys**, and the administration of the **Sacraments**. Furthermore, if they fail to act correctly, he may even determine the due punishments to be set against them; and he does this specifically insofar as he is the **Prince** and a minister of God for every good, who bears the sword and must be a punisher of evil. 

These facts utterly destroy that notion of an "internal ecclesiastical power" and "care of internal sacred matters"—over which the **Prince** is said to have no right—which the **Teachers** arrogantly claim for themselves, <note>The sentence continues onto the next page with the catchword "ut" (so that).</note>

§. XVI. It is also apparent from what I have said that the title of **"political magistrate"** is unworthy of the super-eminent power and office of the **Prince**. Truly, the term **magistrate**, when used to define an office, signifies an inferior power which the magistrate—as one holding a lower authority—receives from a Superior. Yet the power of the **Prince** is supreme, subject only to God; in his own lands, he does not even tolerate an equal, let alone a superior. Furthermore, he is called a "political" magistrate to distinguish him from a supposed "ecclesiastical" power, over which some believe the **Prince** has no legal right. ### CHAPTER XII. THE PRINCE HOLDS SUPREME COMMAND

This super-eminent and sublime power encompasses and exhausts all authority in both **secular and sacred matters**, and to this extent, it is truly called the supreme political power. 

Any other ecclesiastical power—one that does not originate from the **Prince** or is not subject to the **Prince**—is a fiction. It does not have its origin in God, but was instead devised by the **Roman Antichrist** <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Pope and the Roman Catholic hierarchy.</note> and the **new Antichrist** <note>original: "novo Antichrifto." They even dare to deny him the command over religion itself and the judgment necessary to exercise that command rightly. Instead, they claim this power for the **Teachers** by right of their office of teaching, or they attribute it to the **Prince** and the **Teachers** jointly, as if they were colleagues. Even when they include the Prince, they often do so only for the sake of appearances, leaving him nothing but the role of carrying out their decisions.

In the Epistle to the Romans itself, **Paul the Apostle** employs the proper titles; he does not describe **Princes** by the name of "magistrates," even though the various ranks and conditions of those officials could not have been unknown to the Romans. By this very choice, the Apostle shows that the name "magistrate" does not fit the royal office. Thus, he preferred to call those who command "super-eminent powers," **Princes**, **Caesars**, and **Kings**, so that it would be clear that all power resides within them. 

They ought not to be regarded as mere political magistrates—as if, besides them, there were also "ecclesiastical magistrates" who might possess some independent church power over which the **Prince** has no legal right. Such people claim they should represent the church in **Councils**, be represented in **Consistories** <note>Governing bodies or courts of a church.</note>, and that public doctrine should be prescribed, and ecclesiastical rites and ceremonies determined, according to their advice and will.

# ON THE NECESSARY ORDERING OF THE ESTATES.

...[political] magistrate <note>The text continues from the previous page, arguing against those who claim the clergy should hold political authority.</note>, and power at least over the external worship of God, or, to put the matter more clearly, that they [the Princes] ought to be the executors of those things which the **representative church** <term>representative church <note>original: "ecclesia repraesentativa." This refers to the clergy or a church council acting as the official voice of the religious community.</note></term> has decreed. Consequently, they argue that the **ecclesiastical estate** is to be preferred to the **political estate**, since the command over religion and sacred matters, which the **Bishops** and **Doctors** <note>In this context, "Doctors" refers to learned theologians or high-ranking church teachers.</note> hold, excels the command over profane <note>Non-religious or secular.</note> matters, which they judged should be left to the **Prince**.

But that ecclesiastical power of the **Doctors** rests on no foundation; for the church awaits the "means of grace" <note>The Word of God and the Sacraments.</note>, and the **Doctors** apply them through their ministry. However, neither the church nor the ministers possess sovereign command; rather, the **Prince** alone does, whose power is supereminent, sublime, and supreme, insofar as it is given by God and is His vice-regency <note>original: "vicaria." The Prince acts as God's representative on earth in a political sense.</note>. Thus, his power is political—pertaining to the whole commonwealth and, by consequence, also to the church which exists within that commonwealth. Therefore, the **Ecclesiastical Estate** cannot be placed before the **Political Estate** on this basis.

§. III. It must indeed be confessed that the dignity of the **Doctors** or ministers of the church is great. Honor is deservedly preserved for those who lead well through both doctrine and example, because they are ministers of **Christ** and successors in His **prophetic office** <term>prophetic office <note>original: "officium Propheticum." Referring to the role of teaching, preaching, and interpreting the divine will, one of the three traditional offices of Christ.</note></term>. They hold the ministry of divine things, lead their listeners toward eternal salvation, and, by stirring up true piety within them, can be of great use to the commonwealth. But

### CHAPTER XIII. ON THE ORDERING OF THE THREE ESTATES.

**§. IV.** Indeed, **Aaron** was the **High Priest**, and likewise a **type** <note>original: "typus." In theology, a "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament seen as prefiguring or symbolizing Christ.</note> of **Christ**; nevertheless, **Moses**, who performed the office of **King**, was called the **God of Aaron** by God Himself. All **Princes** are also called **Gods**, and they are the **Vicars of God** <term>Vicars of God <note>original: "Vicarii Dei." Those who act in the place or stead of God on earth; here referring to temporal rulers as God's earthly representatives.</note></term> in the government of the commonwealth and of the **church** that exists within it.
