**§. V.** Therefore, the dignity of the **Prince** is greater than that of the **Doctors** and **ministers** of the church, for the former is over the church (**Deuteronomy 17:15**, **1 Samuel 8:5, 19**), the supreme **Presider** over sacred matters from God and in the place of God, the **Rector** <note>Ruler or Guide</note> of the church. The latter are **ministers** in the church, **assistants** to sacred matters, and **Pastors** of the church. To the former belongs the **command** over sacred matters; to the latter, the **ministry** of sacred matters. The **Prince** preserves the true religion through his command and decrees, and he orders from the **Word of God** what subjects ought to believe; the **ministers** can teach, but they cannot command so as to force the listeners to have faith in the truth. The **Prince** holds command over the **Doctors** and **ministers** of the church, both insofar as they are **citizens** and insofar as they are **Doctors**; and he commands from the **Word of God** what they ought to teach, and likewise how they ought to administer the **Sacraments** and use the **keys** <term>power of the keys <note>original: "clavis." The authority to forgive or retain sins, which this author argues is subject to the Prince's oversight.</note></term>. The ministers, in turn, ought to instruct the **Prince** insofar as he is a member of the church.

# CHAPTER XIII. ON THE ORDER AMONG THE THREE [ESTATES]
<page-num>112</page-num>

<meta>catchword: §. VIII.</meta>

...ministry in the customary way, whether in writing or by voice, and then individually discuss first the **ecclesiastical estate** <term>ecclesiastical estate <note>original: "statu ecclesiastico." This refers to the clergy as a distinct social and legal class.</note></term>, and only in the second place do they begin their discourse concerning the **Prince** <note>original: "Princeps." The sovereign or supreme ruler of the territory.</note>. This is done all the more because they themselves ought to advise their listeners to show the honor due to the Prince, and also to serve as an example for them, that they might honor the Prince as one appointed by God (**Romans 13:1, 7**; **1 Peter 2:17**).

§. VII. It is indeed not to be disapproved of that the **Order of Theologians** <note>original: "Ordo Theologorum." Referring to the Faculty of Theology within the university hierarchy.</note> precedes the **Order of Jurists** <note>original: "Ordinem Jureconsultorum." The Faculty of Law.</note>. For although this too may stem from the **Papacy** <note>original: "ex Papatu." A reference to the Roman Catholic system where the church claimed legal and social precedence over secular authorities.</note>—since just as the Prince was forced to yield to the ecclesiastical estate because of their so-called **ecclesiastical power** <note>original: "potestatem ecclesiasticam."</note>, so much more did the Order of Jurists have to yield to the Order of Theologians for the same reason—nevertheless, this order can be observed without harm due to the will of the **Prince**, who has the right to command in such matters according to his own pleasure.

Meanwhile, no one should think that this order of precedence is necessary, as if the **Prince** could not, if he wished, order otherwise. For those things that have been written by a certain famous **Academy** <note>original: "Academia." Referring to a university.</note> regarding the **nobility of theological study** <note>original: "nobilitas studii Theologici."</note> above all other types of study have no foundation. Rather, the **nobility of legal study** is greater than that of theological study, which I shall demonstrate in a few words—although such a question could have been omitted as useless and hardly becoming for **Doctors of Theology** (**Matthew 17:1**) <note>The author likely intended to cite Matthew 18:1, where the disciples ask "Who is the greatest?" as the current citation (the Transfiguration) fits the context less clearly than a debate over status.</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the social and academic hierarchy between theologians, jurists, and the sovereign ruler. The author argues that while theologians traditionally hold the highest rank due to Catholic tradition, this precedence is not divinely mandated and the study of law holds a higher practical "nobility" in the state.</summary>
<keywords>social hierarchy, ecclesiastical estate, sovereignty, Faculty of Theology, Faculty of Law, precedence, University rank, divine right</keywords>

While the previous page conceded that the Faculty of Theology often takes precedence over the Faculty of Law due to custom or the Prince's will, the author now begins a provocative counter-argument for the superior status of legal studies based on the nature of their subject matter.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>

->**NECESSARY FOR THE ARRANGING OF THE ORDERS.**<-

H

§. VIII. <note>The original uses "IIX," an archaic way of writing 8 (two from ten).</note> The nobility of theological study has indeed been demonstrated by its object, because **Theology** is concerned with divine matters. But nevertheless, it remains true that the nobility of the **study of law** <note>original: "studii juridici."</note> is greater than that of the study of theology. Indeed, the foundation of all **Jurisprudence** <term>Jurisprudence <note>The theory or philosophy of law.</note></term> is **natural law** <term>natural law <note>original: "jus naturale." In this period, this referred to a system of right or justice held to be common to all humans and derived from nature or reason, rather than from the specific laws of a state.</note></term>, and this law is entirely divine, possessing agreement with the justice, holiness, and goodness of God; it is thus completely immutable, just like the justice of God itself. Furthermore, those theoretical principles regarding truth and falsehood are themselves a part of natural law. Therefore, jurisprudence is concerned with divine matters that are necessary and unchangeable. 

By contrast, **Theology** is concerned for the most part with **contingent matters** <note>original: "arbitraria." In this context, it refers to things that depend on God's will or choice rather than being logically necessary. For example, the specifics of salvation are "arbitrary" because God chose them out of grace, not because he was forced to by nature.</note>, which God performed and revealed out of his sole grace according to his own will; for it cannot be denied that God, if he had wished, could have condemned all humans, just as he did the fallen angels. Furthermore, that [natural] law is repeated in the **moral law** specifically so called, which likewise belongs to jurisprudence. For when the **Prince** <note>The sovereign ruler.</note> establishes laws, he keeps that moral law before his eyes as a standard, and the **Jurists** <note>original: "Jureconsulti." Legal experts or advisors who interpreted the law.</note> provide their counsel specifically from that law to the Prince when he wishes to establish new laws; and judges also administer justice based on the laws of the Prince, which are themselves composed from the moral law. 

**Theologians**, however, are occupied with this law only by way of teaching it. Jurisprudence also encompasses universal **positive divine laws** <term>positive divine laws <note>Laws that are not necessarily inherent in nature but were specifically "posited" or commanded by God at a certain time, such as specific religious rituals.</note></term>, which God gave from his sole pleasure so that all people might observe them. The laws of Princes also, in place of the Ordinatio-

<summary>The author argues that Jurisprudence is "nobler" than Theology because it deals with the immutable, necessary natural law rooted in God's essence, whereas Theology deals with matters of divine will and grace which God could have chosen differently.</summary>
<keywords>jurisprudence, natural law, theology, moral law, divine justice, positive law, Prince, legal theory</keywords>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII. ON THE ORDER AMONG THE THREE <note>The "Three" refers to the higher academic faculties: Theology, Law, and Medicine.</note></header>

...[regula]tions <note>The text begins mid-word, continuing "constitutionibus" from the previous page.</note> they ought to be held as divine, not only because they are established by universal divine law, but also because the Prince is the **Vicar of God** <note>A representative or deputy of God on earth; here referring to the doctrine of the Divine Right of Kings.</note>, as stated in Romans 13:1–2.

§. IX. The word of God itself and the whole of Holy Scripture, along with all its mysteries, pertain not only to the **legislative jurisprudence** of Princes, but also to the **deliberative jurisprudence** of legal experts. This is so that the Prince may rightly exercise the governance of the church and command the Doctors <note>In this context, "Doctors" refers to Doctors of Theology—the professors and high-ranking clergy.</note> from the Word of God as to what they must teach and how they should conduct themselves in the rest of their **ministry**; and further, so that these legal experts may provide counsel to the Prince whenever he sees fit to require it, as seen in Jonah 3:7–9. 

Even upon every judge, some care of sacred matters is incumbent, a subject which it is not necessary to discuss further here. Therefore, **Jurisprudence**—whether legislative, deliberative, or judicial—is concerned with all divine matters: by prescribing laws for Theologians based on the Word of God, by commanding the rest of the subjects, by providing counsel to the Prince, and by declaring the law. **Theology**, however, is concerned with these matters only by teaching. 

Furthermore, the word of God ought to be taught for an invisible effect—for the purpose of awakening faith in men, or for instructing those who must thereafter perform their duties in the ministry—and, in a word, for the **salvation of souls** <note>original: "salutem animarum."</note>. It is not taught for some political effect or for seeking a **prerogative** <term>prerogative <note>A right or privilege exclusive to a particular individual or class; here, the author argues theologians should not use their office to claim political rank.</note></term>. This is even more the case because even Christ, the supreme Teacher, was nevertheless humble, seeking not his own honor but that of his Father—a fact which is also true of the Apostles. By this reasoning, the arguments collapse which are used for defending...

<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>ON THE NECESSARY ARRANGEMENT OF THE ORDERS.</header>

...laws <note>The text begins mid-word, continuing the discussion of the three "higher" academic faculties: Theology, Law, and Medicine.</note> regarding the three Orders are usually brought forward in their customary arrangement. **Jurisprudence** <note>original: *Jurisprudentia*; the science and study of law</note>, however, exists for the purpose of governing the **commonwealth** <note>original: *rempublicam*; the state or public body</note>, so that both sacred and secular affairs—both within the state and within the **church** <note>original: *ecclesia*</note> that exists inside it—may be rightly managed. It is clear from what has been said that the study of law is far more noble than the study of theology. Consequently, if a **prerogative** <note>A right of precedence or higher rank</note> were necessarily required for either Order, it would belong to the Order of **Jurists** <note>original: *Jureconsultorum*; legal experts and professors of law</note>. Yet, in truth, such a prerogative is not strictly owed to either by necessity; rather, the matter depends entirely on the will of the **Prince** <note>The sovereign ruler</note>. It is by his favor that the Order of Theologians precedes the Order of Jurists, a practice which occurs without sin or any lingering traces of the **Papacy** <note>The author argues that giving theologians first place is a civil choice by the ruler, not a divine right or a remnant of the Catholic belief that the Church is superior to the State.</note>.

§. X. Nevertheless, the ecclesiastical status cannot be preferred over the political status without crime and scandal, as this would be contrary to **Scripture**. For the Bible states that **Melchizedek** was a King who was also a Priest, but not the reverse—it does not say Melchizedek the Priest was also a King. **Moses** is also always placed before **Aaron**, the High Priest. Furthermore, in the Scriptures, the Holy Spirit mentions first the Kings and Great Men, then the Priests, and finally the rest of the people or citizens (Genesis 14:18; Jeremiah 13:13, 32:32; Haggai 1:12, 14; Hebrews 7:1). To this, one must add that the Prince defends the church just as he defends the commonwealth, for the church exists within the commonwealth. For this reason alone, the honor due to him must be preserved—all the more so because commonwealths are established for the **happi**—

<note>The text ends mid-word. The catchword "tem" suggests the next page continues with *felicitatem* (happiness or prosperity).</note>

Using biblical examples like Melchizedek and Moses, the author asserts that civil authority is inherently superior to ecclesiastical authority.</summary>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the intersection of civil law and moral theology. Having established that the Prince (the sovereign ruler) holds authority over both secular and sacred organizational matters, the author now applies this to specific cases of life and death, such as homicide, dueling, and adultery.</meta>

...and so God is provoked to anger. It is the duty of **Princes** <note>The sovereign rulers or heads of state</note> to take care that this divine wrath is not poured out upon the entire region because of sins that have been increased by **homicide** <note>original: *homicidio*; the killing of one human by another</note>. Furthermore, what is read in Matthew 26:52 <note>"...all who draw the sword will die by the sword."</note> and Revelation 13:10 cannot be used to support the opposite opinion. For these passages show that subjects must not use force against their Prince, even if he inflicts injustice, violence, pain, or death for the sake of religion. Indeed, mention is made of the **sword**, which is granted to Princes in Romans 13:4. In that biblical context, the Jews had already seized Christ, and the "patience and faith of the Saints" is being commemorated. Finally, the specific method of execution is left to the judgment of Princes, because God has commanded nothing regarding it.

### III.

The **custom** of challenging another to a fight, particularly common among soldiers who wish to fight in single combat, is contrary to the **Fifth Commandment** <note>original: *Præcepto qvinto*; "Thou shalt not kill."</note>, which prohibits homicide without making any distinction between men. It is likewise contrary to the **First Commandment** <note>original: *Præcepto primo*; "You shall have no other gods before me," interpreted here as putting God's will above all else.</note>, which commands us to hold the divine will in higher regard than our own honor or fortunes. Similarly, the law regarding the killing of a murderer applies equally to such people. Therefore, these contests cannot be made just by any human law.

### IV.

**Laws** grant impunity to a husband if he kills an adulterer in the very act of **adultery**. However, the laws neither approve of the killing itself, nor can they make it lawful, for vengeance belongs to God alone and to the Prince (Romans 13:4, James 1:20). Rather, the laws tolerate such a killing only because of the violence of human emotions and anger.

### V.

The **controversy** over whether marriage is a **Sacrament** is not merely a **war of words** <note>original: *logomachia*; a dispute about words rather than reality</note>. For when that matter is debated, it must be under-

intel- <note>Catchword for the following page, likely beginning the word *intelligendum* (understood).</note>

<meta>This page continues the legal and theological discussion from the previous page. While the preceding text addressed the Prince's authority over life and death (homicide and dueling), this page transitions into the definition of Sacraments and the specific nature of marital authority within the household. It concludes the argument that marriage is a civil and divine institution rather than a strictly ecclesiastical sacrament.</meta>

-><image-desc>A decorative horizontal printer's ornament consisting of three floral/foliate woodcut motifs separated by two small circles.</image-desc><-

...a **Sacrament** <note>original: *Sacramentum*</note> is understood, such as **baptism** and the **Holy Supper** <note>original: *Sacra coena*; Holy Communion or the Eucharist</note>, besides which two no other was instituted by Christ. Therefore, **marriage** <note>original: *matrimonium*</note> is not a Sacrament. The matrimonial laws of **Protestants** are also very far removed from the **Papal** <note>original: *Papistico*; referring to Roman Catholic doctrine</note> error regarding the Sacrament of marriage, and they can be maintained for other just reasons.

### VI.

The power of a husband over a wife is a true power, for no one denies that commands are given to the will; however, the will of the wife ought to be subject to the husband. Just as subjection in subjects and obedience in servants is required, so too must the will of the wife be subject to the husband. Furthermore, the husband ought to be the **Lord** <note>original: *Dominus*</note> of the wife, and thus he is able to command her. Finally, this same point is proved by the analogy which Paul employed in **Ephesians 5:24** <note>"Now as the church submits to Christ, so also wives should submit to their husbands in everything."</note>. Therefore, it is not a "non-entity," nor is it a dominion spoken of only in a metaphorical sense.

Neither does the duty of the husband proposed in **Colossians 3:19** <note>"Husbands, love your wives and do not be harsh with them."</note> and **1 Peter 3:7** <note>Instructions for husbands to treat their wives with respect as the "weaker partner."</note> take away marital power, nor does it make that power "improper" <note>meaning metaphorical or unofficial</note>; rather, the husband is warned by those verses to use his right and power correctly and prudently, just as the power of **Princes**, **Parents**, and **Masters** <note>original: *Herorum*; heads of households or owners of estates</note> is not abolished by their own respective duties.

The law regarding **marital power** <note>original: *potestate maritali*</note> is also true and perfect, for God showed His serious will by proposing the law on the part of the wife and repeating it on the part of the husband. This is a **positive law** <note>original: *lex positiva*; a law specifically decreed by a legislator (God), rather than a law inherently obvious through nature alone</note>, which God gave out of His will alone. In the **state of integrity** <note>original: *statu integritatis*; the condition of humanity before the Fall or original sin</note>, marital power would not have been necessary because of the highest unanimity of mind; hence we read that **Adam** immediately ate the fruit of the forbidden tree offered by **Eve**, although he sinned by this very act. 

Therefore, after the Fall, this law could not have been known by **natural law** <note>original: *jure naturali*; laws perceived by human reason alone</note>, and the matter would have had to be defined by a special contract; for not all women would have yielded that right to husbands simply because of the excellence of the male sex, or masculine strength and prudence, since these qualities are also sometimes greater in women. For this reason, God brought forth the law concerning marital power. This is a penal positive law,

<summary>This page argues that marriage is not a religious sacrament but a divinely ordained hierarchy where the husband holds "true power" over the wife, a status established by God's "positive law" after the Fall of Man.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal and theological argument regarding domestic authority and the hierarchy of power. The previous page ended while discussing the duties of husbands as outlined in Colossians 3:19. This page completes that thought by linking the subjection of women to the biblical account of the Fall and extends the discussion to the "Divine Right" of princes over the church (Caesaropapism).</meta>

-><image-desc>A decorative woodcut headpiece featuring floral motifs and a central circular element.</image-desc><-

...for it obliges women themselves to offer obedience to their husbands, because **Adam** <note>original: *Adamus*</note> yielded to **Eve** <note>original: *Evæ*</note> as she was leading him into sin. Otherwise, **penal laws** <note>original: *leges pœnales*; laws prescribing punishment for crimes</note> regularly oblige judges to inflict punishments upon those who have done wrong. Furthermore, this law is universal, as it was given to the first human beings from whom all future people would descend. The reasoning behind it is also universal: namely, because the wife led the husband into sin, she who is a wife must have her will subject to her husband, and he who is the husband must be the **Lord** <note>original: *Dominus*</note> of the wife. Even in the **New Testament**, this is frequently repeated: **1 Corinthians 14:34**, **1 Timothy 2:11–12**, and **1 Peter 3:6**. Finally, this law cannot be changed by men. While anyone can indeed renounce their own legal right, the issue here is not primarily the right of the husband; rather, God enjoined the obedience to be performed by the wife toward the husband as a form of punishment. Therefore, it is not merely a favor granted to the husband, but a law that all people must observe. Since the universal laws of God must not be abolished by men, such a renunciation has no place here. Consequently, a **Queen** who has reserved power over her subjects for herself by a contract still owes obedience to her husband insofar as she is a wife, even if she commands him in her capacity as Queen; conversely, the husband has dominion over her insofar as he is a husband, even though he must also obey her as a subject.

### VII.

If a contract is absent, it is truly said: **"Whose the region, his the religion"** <note>original: *cujus eft regio, illius etiam eft religio*; a legal principle meaning a ruler determines the religion of his territory</note>. Therefore, **Caesaropapism** <note>original: *Καισαροπαπία* (Kaisaropapia); the idea of a head of state also being the head of the church</note>, regarding the actual matter signified by this word, is the foremost virtue and duty of every **Prince**. However, the name itself is unfitting, because those of the ecclesiastical order do not possess the power over sacred things; rather, the Prince holds that power by his own right as the **Vicar of God** <note>original: *Vicarius Dei*; one who acts in the place of God on earth</note>. This term, like many others, was therefore devised to exclude the Prince from the **government of sacred matters** <note>original: *imperio facrorum*</note>. On the other hand, **Papocaesarism** <note>original: *Παποκαισαρία* (Papokaisaria); the idea of a church leader exercising political power</note> ought to be considered among crimes—not only if those of the ecclesiastical order usurp power over secular things, but even if they usurp power over sacred things <note>The author argues that the Prince, not the clergy, is the rightful ultimate authority over the church's administration</note>.

<meta>This page continues a legal and theological discussion regarding the natural and divine order of hierarchy, moving from the specific domestic relationship of husband and wife to the broader political relationship between Church and State.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut headpiece featuring floral motifs and a central circle.</image-desc>

For it binds women themselves to show obedience to their husbands, because Adam yielded to Eve when she was seducing him <note>The author refers to the biblical narrative of the Fall in Genesis, where Adam followed Eve’s lead in eating the forbidden fruit.</note>. Furthermore, penal laws regularly bind judges to inflict punishments upon wrongdoers. This law is also universal, as it was given to the first human beings, from whom all future people were to descend. The reason for it is likewise universal: namely, because the wife seduced the husband, the woman who is a wife must have her will subject to her husband, and he who is the husband must be the master of the wife. This is also frequently repeated in the New Testament: 1 Corinthians 14:34, 1 Timothy 2:11–12, and 1 Peter 3:6. Finally, this law cannot be changed by human beings. While anyone can renounce their own rights, we are not dealing here primarily with the "right" of a husband; rather, God imposed obedience upon the wife toward her husband as a form of punishment. Therefore, it is not merely a favor granted to the husband, but a law that all people must observe. Since the universal laws of God must not be abolished by men, a renunciation of this right has no place. Thus, a Queen, who has reserved the power of command over her subjects for herself by treaty, still owes obedience to her husband insofar as she is a wife, even though she commands him in her capacity as Queen. Conversely, the husband has dominion over her insofar as he is the husband, even though he must also obey her as a subject.

### VII.

If a specific treaty is absent, it is truly said: **Whose region, his religion** <note>original: "cujus est regio, illius etiam est religio." This was a famous legal principle in the Holy Roman Empire meaning the religion of the ruler dictated the religion of the ruled.</note>. Therefore, **Caesaropapism** <note>original: "Καισαροπαπία" (Kaisaropapia). This refers to a system where the secular ruler (Caesar) also acts as the head of the church (Pope).</note>, as it pertains to the actual matter signified by the word, is the primary virtue and duty of every Prince. However, the name itself is unsuitable, because those of the ecclesiastical order do not possess the power over sacred matters; rather, the Prince holds that by his own right as the **Vicar of God** <term>Vicarius Dei: a representative of God on earth, usually implying that the ruler's authority comes directly from a divine mandate</term>. This term, like many others, was therefore devised to exclude the Prince from the government of sacred affairs. On the other hand, **Papocaesarism** <note>original: "Παποκαισαρία" (Papokaisaria). This refers to the inverse of Caesaropapism, where the church or Pope usurps the power of the secular ruler.</note> should be regarded as a crime, not only if those of the ecclesiastical order usurp power over secular affairs, but also if they usurp supreme power over Sacred affairs.
